\Pr\left[\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\left|\sum_{e\in X}w(e)-|X|\right|>\delta\cdot x\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{48}\cdot\delta^{2}\cdot px\right).

Proof.

For ease of notation, let
\widetilde{\lambda}
be the vector of edge connectivities
(\widetilde{\lambda}_{e})_{e\in E}
from
Algorithm

2
, and
\lambda
is the vector of edge connectivities
(\lambda_{e})_{e\in E}
of
G
. We will condition on the values
\widehat{\lambda}
that
\widetilde{\lambda}
can take, and define

\widehat{p}_{e}=\min\left\{1,\frac{2}{\widehat{\lambda}_{e}}\cdot\frac{C\log^{3}n}{\delta^{2}}\right\}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widehat{j}_{e}=\left\lfloor\log\frac{1}{\min\{1,\widehat{p}_{e}\}}\right\rfloor\,.

Then,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\left|\sum_{e\in X}w(e)-|X|\right|>\delta\cdot x\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\widehat{\lambda}\in\mathbb{N}^{E}}\Pr\left[\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\left|\sum_{e\in X}w(e)-|X|\right|>\delta\cdot x\text{ and }\widetilde{\lambda}=\widehat{\lambda}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\widehat{\lambda}\in\mathbb{N}^{E}\,:\,\frac{1}{2}\lambda\leq{\widehat{\lambda}}\leq\frac{3}{2}\lambda}\Pr\left[\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\left|\sum_{e\in X}w(e)-|X|\right|>\delta\cdot x\text{ and }\widetilde{\lambda}=\widehat{\lambda}\right]\,.

By conditioning we get

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\left|\sum_{e\in X}w(e)-|X|\right|>\delta\cdot x\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\widehat{\lambda}\in\mathbb{N}^{E}\,:\,\frac{1}{2}\lambda\leq{\widehat{\lambda}}\leq\frac{3}{2}\lambda}\Pr\left[\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\left|\sum_{e\in X}w(e)-|X|\right|>\delta\cdot x\middle|\widetilde{\lambda}=\widehat{\lambda}\right]\cdot\Pr\left[\widetilde{\lambda}=\widehat{\lambda}\right]

We now analyse the first factor in each term of the sum above. We use three facts to do this: (1) whenever we have
\neg\mathcal{B}
, for all
e\in X
one has that
e\in E_{j_{e}}^{\prime}
if and only if
e\in E_{j_{e}}
(this is the case because
\frac{1}{2}\lambda\leq{\widetilde{\lambda}}\leq\frac{3}{2}\lambda
, so
\max\{0,\tau_{e}-2\}\leq j_{e}\leq\tau_{e}
, and the event
\mathcal{B}^{(2)}_{e}
does not happen); (2) recall that, for
i=0,\dots,2\log n
,
e\in E_{i}
if and only if
\prod_{t=1}^{i}h_{t}(e)=1
(see
Algorithm

2
); (3) also recall that
Algorithm

2
assigns weight
2^{j_{e}}
to an edge recovered from
E^{\prime}_{j_{e}}
(see
Algorithm

2
).
Using these three facts, we get

\Pr\left[\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\left|\sum_{e\in X}w(e)-|X|\right|>\delta\cdot x\middle|\widetilde{\lambda}=\widehat{\lambda}\right]=\Pr\left[\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\left|\sum_{e\in X}2^{j_{e}}\prod_{t=1}^{j_{e}}h_{t}(e)-|X|\right|>\delta\cdot x\middle|\widetilde{\lambda}=\widehat{\lambda}\right]\,.

Next, by the condition
\widetilde{\lambda}=\widehat{\lambda}
, we have
\widehat{j}_{e}=j_{e}
for all
e\in X
, so we get

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\left|\sum_{e\in X}w(e)-|X|\right|>\delta\cdot x\middle|\widetilde{\lambda}=\widehat{\lambda}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\left|\sum_{e\in X}2^{\widehat{j}_{e}}\prod_{t=1}^{\widehat{j}_{e}}h_{t}(e)-|X|\right|>\delta\cdot x\middle|\widetilde{\lambda}=\widehat{\lambda}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[\left|\sum_{e\in X}2^{\widehat{j}_{e}}\prod_{t=1}^{\widehat{j}_{e}}h_{t}(e)-|X|\right|>\delta\cdot x\middle|\widetilde{\lambda}=\widehat{\lambda}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[\left|\sum_{e\in X}2^{\widehat{j}_{e}}\prod_{t=1}^{\widehat{j}_{e}}h_{t}(e)-|X|\right|>\delta\cdot x\right]\,.

Now we apply the following form of the Chernoff bound.

Fact 4.10

(
[
FKM23
]
)
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}
be independent random variables distributed in
[0,a]
. Let
X=\sum_{r\in[N]}X_{r}
and
\mu=\ex[X]
. Then, for
\zeta\in(0,1)
and
\alpha\geq 0
one has

\Pr\left[\left|X-\mu\right|>\zeta\mu+\alpha\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\zeta\alpha}{3a}\right)

Before applying this bound, we note that the random variables
2^{\widehat{j}_{e}}\prod_{t=1}^{\widehat{j}_{e}}h_{t}(e)
are independent across
e\in X
and have each mean
1
. Moreover, each of this random variables have value at most
2^{\widehat{j}_{e}}\leq 2^{\lfloor\log\frac{1}{\min\{1,p\}}\rfloor}
, so we set
a=1/p
. We also set
\zeta=\delta/2
and
\alpha=\delta\cdot(x-|X|)+\delta|X|/2
. Then we get

\Pr\left[\left|\sum_{e\in X}2^{\widehat{j}_{e}}\prod_{t=1}^{\widehat{j}_{e}}h_{t}(e)-|X|\right|>\delta\cdot x\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}}{48}px\right)\,.

This concludes the proof.
∎

4.3
Cut counting and proving the sparsification lemma

We hereafter fix a cluster
U\subseteq V
, and we partition the edge set
E
into
F_{i}=\{e\in E:\,2^{i}\leq\lambda_{e}\leq 2^{i+1}-1\,\}
for
i=0,\dots,\log n
. Note that these
F_{i}
’s are pairwise disjoint and their union gives
E
. We recall that
E(A,B)
denotes the set of edges in
E
with one endpoint in
A
and one in
B
, for
A,B\subseteq V
. Then, we write
{\partial}_{U}^{i}S=|F_{i}\cap E(S,U\setminus S)|
to denote the size of the projection of the local cut of
S
onto the edge set
F_{i}
, for all
i=0,\dots,\log n
and
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
. Analogously, we also write
{\partial}_{U}^{i\,w}S=w(F_{i}\cap E(S,U\setminus S))
to denote the total weight of the projection of the local cut of
S
onto
F_{i}
, where
w
is the weight function of
H
.

We show that
H
preserves the cuts of a cluster
U\subseteq V
within each
F_{i}
individually, with high probability. This follows by two of the technical lemmas we proved in the previous section, namely
Lemma

4.8
and
Lemma

4.9
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
be the output of
Algorithm

2
, and let
i=0,\dots,\log n
. Then

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\neg B\,\text{ and }\,\exists\,\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U\,:\,\left|{\partial}_{U}^{i\,w}S-{\partial}_{U}^{i}S\right|>\delta\cdot\frac{{\partial}S}{1+\log n}\right]\leq\frac{1}{\poly(n)}\,.

Proof.

We proceed by first showing a probability bound for each cut
S
, and then combine them using a union bound to get the final result. Specifically, for all
j\in[2\log n]
we define the set

\mathcal{Y}_{ij}=\{E(S,V\setminus S):\,\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U\text{ and }2^{i+j-1}\leq{\partial}S\leq 2^{i+j}-1\}\,,

and we will show a probability bound for all
j\in[2\log n]
and
Y\in\mathcal{Y}_{ij}
. Taking a careful union bound over all such choices will be enough to get the lemma statement. It shall be useful to define
\Pi_{U}^{i}(Y)=Y\cap F_{i}\cap\binom{U}{2}
to be the projection onto
F_{i}
and onto the cluster
U
of the edges in
Y
, for any
Y\subseteq E
. Moreover, we denote by
\mathcal{F}_{Y}
the failure event that

\left|w(\Pi_{U}^{i}(Y))-|\Pi_{U}^{i}(Y)|\right|>\delta\frac{|Y|}{1+\log n}\,,

For ease of writing we shall denote
\Pi_{U}^{i}(E(S,V\setminus S))
and
\mathcal{F}_{E(S,V\setminus S)}
by
\Pi_{U}^{i}({\partial}S)
and
\mathcal{F}_{{\partial}S}
respectively. To appreciate their purpose, we remark that for any
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{{\partial}S}\quad\iff\quad\left|{\partial}_{U}^{i\,w}S-{\partial}_{U}^{i}S\right|>\delta\frac{{\partial}S}{1+\log n}\,

since
\Pi_{U}^{i}({\partial}S)=\Pi_{U}^{i}(E(S,V\setminus S))=E(S,V\setminus S)\cap F_{i}\cap\binom{U}{2}=F_{i}\cap E(S,U\setminus S)
.

Claim 4.12
.

Let
j\in[2\log n]
and
Y\in\mathcal{Y}_{ij}
. Then

\Pr[\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\mathcal{F}_{Y}]\leq 2n^{-\frac{C}{768}2^{j}}\,.

Proof.

We start by observing that we can consider only the set
X=\{e\in\Pi_{U}^{i}(Y)\,:\,p_{e}<1\}
, since edges with
p_{e}=1
are always added to
E^{\prime}
with weight set to
1
(when
\mathcal{B}
does not occur) by Lemma
4.8
, so

\left|w(\Pi_{U}^{i}(Y))-|\Pi_{U}^{i}(Y)|\right|=\left|w(X)-|X|\right|\,.

If
X=\emptyset
, the probability of
\mathcal{F}_{Y}
is zero, so let us consider the case that
X\neq\emptyset
, for which we want to upper bound the probability

\Pr\left[\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\left|w(X)-|X|\right|>\delta\frac{|Y|}{1+\log n}\right]\,.

To do so, we use Lemma
4.9
and set
p=\min\{1,C\cdot 2^{-(i+1)}\delta^{-2}\log^{3}n\}
, which is a lower bound on all the probabilities
\{p_{e}\}_{e\in X}
. This is the case because every
e\in X
has
p_{e}<1
and
\lambda_{e}\leq 2^{i+1}
(since
X\subseteq F_{i}
). We also note that
|Y|\geq|X|
, thus we can safely apply Lemma
4.9
and get

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\left|w(X)-|X|\right|>\delta\frac{|Y|}{1+\log n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{48}\cdot\delta^{2}\cdot p\cdot\frac{|Y|}{(1+\log n)^{2}}\right)\,.

If we recall
Y\in\mathcal{Y}_{ij}
, and in particular
|Y|\geq 2^{i+j-1}
, we can then conclude the claim by plugging the value of
p
into the exponent.
∎

Now that we have bounded the probability that a single cut from
\mathcal{Y}_{ij}
is not well preserved once projected onto
U
and
F_{i}
, we show that the union over
j
of the collections
\mathcal{Y}_{ij}
is representative of all the local cuts of
U
.

Claim 4.13
.

Let
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
such that
\Pi_{U}^{i}({\partial}S)\neq~\emptyset
. Then there exists
j\in[2\log n]
so that
E(S,V\setminus~S)~\in~\mathcal{Y}_{ij}
.

Proof.

Since
\Pi_{U}^{i}({\partial}S)\neq~\emptyset
, there exists
e=\{u,v\}\in E(S,V\setminus S)
that is also in
F_{i}
. In particular this means that
E(S,V\setminus S)
is a
uv
-cut in
G
, and
e
has edge connectivity at least
2^{i}
, thus
{\partial}S\geq 2^{i}
. At the same time,
{\partial}S\leq 2^{i+j}-1
for some
j\in[2\log n]
since
{\partial}S\leq|E|\leq n^{2}-1
.
∎

Now, for all
j\in[2\log n]
and all
Z\subseteq F_{i}
, we define the worst-case edge set
Y^{*}_{j}(Z)\subseteq E
of
Z
as

Y^{*}_{j}(Z)\in\argmin_{Y\in\mathcal{Y}_{ij}:\Pi_{U}^{i}(Y)=Z}|Y|\,.

The reason why we call
Y^{*}_{j}(Z)
worst case for
Z
is the following. Among all the global cuts in
\mathcal{Y}_{ij}
whose projection onto
F_{i}
and
U
is
Z
, the cut
Y^{*}_{j}(Z)
is the one which allows for the smallest error term, which intuitively makes
\mathcal{F}_{Y}
more likely to happen. Formally we have the following claim, which follows from the definition.

Claim 4.14
.

If there exist
j\in[2\log n]
and
Y\in\mathcal{Y}_{ij}
such that the event
\mathcal{F}_{Y}
happens, then there exist
j\in[2\log n]
and
Z\in\{\Pi_{U}^{i}(Y^{\prime}):Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}_{ij}\}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{Y_{j}^{*}(Z)}
happens.

Claim
4.13
and
4.14
together suggest that we can restrain ourselves to union bound over
\{\Pi_{U}^{i}(Y):Y\in\mathcal{Y}_{ij}\}
, which is exactly what we are going to do. Before that, we make sure that this set is not too large.

Claim 4.15
.

For every
j\in[2\log n]
we have
|\{\Pi_{U}^{i}(Y):Y\in\mathcal{Y}_{ij}\}|\leq n^{2\cdot 2^{j}}
.

Proof.

We will employ the following cut counting result, where
E^{k}
denotes the set of edges
e\in E
with edge connectivity
\lambda_{e}\geq k
, for any integer
k
.

Theorem 4.16

(
[
FHHP11
]
Theorem 1.6)
.

For any
k,s\geq 1
one has

|\{E(S,V\setminus S)\cap E^{k}\,:\,\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq V\text{ and }{\partial}S\leq s\cdot k\}|\leq n^{2s}\,,

i.e. the number of distinct subsets of edges of connectivity at least
k
from cuts of size at most
s\cdot k
is at most
n^{2s}
.

Fix
j\in[2\log n]
. We recall that every
e\in F_{i}
has
\lambda_{e}\geq 2^{i}
, which means
F_{i}\subseteq E^{2^{i}}
. Also, for any
Y\in\mathcal{Y}_{ij}
there is by definition a cut
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq V
such that
{\partial}S\leq 2^{i+j}-1
and
Y=E(S,V\setminus S)
. Hence

\displaystyle|\{\Pi_{U}^{i}(Y):\,Y\in\mathcal{Y}_{ij}\}|

\displaystyle\leq|\{E^{2^{i}}\cap E(S,V\setminus S):\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq V\text{ and }{\partial}S\leq 2^{i+j}\}|\,,

and applying Theorem
4.16
with
k\coloneqq 2^{i}
and
s\coloneqq 2^{j}
we conclude the claim.
∎

Using the above claims we finally have

\displaystyle\Pr[\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\exists\,\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U\text{ s.t. }\mathcal{F}_{{\partial}S}]

\displaystyle\text{by Claim~\ref{claim:calyisenough}}\quad\quad\leq

\displaystyle\Pr[\exists j\in[2\log n]\text{ s.t. }\exists Y\in\mathcal{Y}_{ij}\text{ s.t. }\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\mathcal{F}_{Y}]

\displaystyle\text{by Claim~\ref{claim:worstcaseproj}}\quad\quad\leq

\displaystyle\Pr[\exists j\in[2\log n]\text{ s.t. }\exists Z\in\{\Pi_{U}^{i}(Y):Y\in\mathcal{Y}_{ij}\}\text{ s.t. }\neg\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\mathcal{F}_{Y_{j}^{*}(Z)}]

\displaystyle\text{by Claim~\ref{claim:boundprobsingleset}}\quad\quad\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[2\log n]}\quad\sum_{Z\in\{\Pi_{U}^{i}(Y):\,Y\in\mathcal{Y}_{ij}\}}2n^{-\frac{C}{768}2^{j}}

\displaystyle\text{by Claim~\ref{claim:smallunionbound}}\quad\quad\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[2\log n]}n^{2\cdot 2^{j}}\,2n^{-\frac{C}{768}2^{j}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\poly(n)}

∎

Finally, we can conclude the main sparsification lemma
Lemma

4.2
by a simple combination of
Lemma

4.7
,
Lemma

4.11
, and
Lemma

4.6
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.2
.

Recall that the
F_{i}
’s partition and cover
E
entirely, and that
\neg\mathcal{B}
happens with high probability by
Lemma

4.7
. Then, one can see that conditioning on
\neg\mathcal{B}
, summing over
i=0,\dots,\log n
, and applying
Lemma

4.11
gives
Lemma

4.2
.
∎

5
Testing expansion and finding sparse cuts in sparsifiers

The key ingredient to our ED algorithms is that sparsifiers as per
Definition

4.1
serve as good proxies to the subgraphs of
G
for any subroutine that either asserts expansion or outputs an approximate most-balanced sparse cut. We will in fact show that the result of running such subroutine on a boundary-linked subgraph of
G
is almost the same as if we run it on the corresponding subgraph of a sparsifier.

Before we proceed further, we need to define what we demand exactly from the approximate most-balanced sparse cut subroutine. Loosely speaking, it is a procedure that takes as input a graph and a sparsity parameter, and either asserts that the graph is an expander, or outputs a sparse cut. In particular, the returned cut should be not too small compared to the largest cut among those certifying that the input is not an expander. For technical reasons, we ask that the algorithm also returns an approximation to the volume of the returned cut. Inspired by the definition of
[
FKM23
]
, we have the following definition, which captures what the subroutine should return.

Definition 5.1

(Balanced sparse cut witness)
.

Let
\psi,\xi\in[0,1]
and
\alpha,\lambda\geq 1
, and let
H=(U,E^{\prime},w)
be a weighted graph with self-loops. A
(\psi,\xi,\alpha,\lambda)
-balanced sparse cut witness of
H
(for short,
(\psi,\alpha,\lambda,\xi)
-BSCW) is an element
\omega
of
\{\bot\}\cup(\{R\,:\,\emptyset\neq R\subsetneq U\}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})
such that:

1.

if
\omega=\bot
, then
H
is a
\psi
-expander;

2.

if
\omega=(R,\nu)
, then:

(a)

\Phi_{H}(R)<\alpha\cdot\psi
,

(b)

for every other cut
\emptyset\neq T\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{H}(T)<\psi
and
\vol_{H}(T)\leq\vol_{H}(U\setminus T)
one has
\vol_{H}(T)\leq\lambda\cdot\vol_{H}(R)
,

(c)

\vol_{H}(R)\leq(1+\xi)\vol_{H}(U\setminus R)
,

(d)

(1-\xi)\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq\nu\leq(1+\xi)\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)
.

A balanced sparse cut subroutine is then simply an algorithm returning a balanced sparse cut witness. For convenience of later discussions, we also have the following definition.

Definition 5.2

(Balanced sparse cut algorithm)
.

Let
\alpha,\lambda\geq 1
. An algorithm
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
is an
(\alpha,\lambda)
-balanced sparse cut algorithm (for short,
(\alpha,\lambda)
-BSCA) if, taken as input a weighted graph with self-loops
H=(U,E^{\prime},w)
and a parameter
\psi\in(0,1)
,
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H,\psi)
outputs a
(\psi,\alpha,\lambda,0)
-BSCW of
H
.

Ultimately, we will use either an exponential time brute force BSCA, or a fast BSCA with worse parameters, leading to our exponential and polynomial time ED algorithms, respectively.

Using these definitions, the main result of this section is the following: one can almost seamlessly run a BSCA on the sparsifier, without heavily deteriorating the quality of the BSCW hence obtained.

Lemma 5.3

(Proxying lemma)
.

Let
b,\phi,c,\delta\in(0,1)
such that
c\leq 1/30
is a constant,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
,
\delta\leq c^{2}b/\log n
, and let
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
be an
(\alpha,\lambda)
-BSCA with
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
. Fix a cluster
U\subseteq V
and also let
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
such that
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
. Let
\omega
be the output of
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[U]^{\circ},(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi)
. Then,
\omega
is a
(\phi,(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[U]^{\circ}
.

In
Section

5.1
and
Section

5.2
we show properties that will let us compare cuts and volumes in
H[U]^{\circ}
and
G[U]^{\circ}
. In
Section

5.3
we use these tools to prove
Lemma

5.3
.

5.1
Characterizing boundary-linked cuts

Structurally, we will divide the cuts of a subgraph
G[U]^{\circ}
into two groups: those those that are boundary-linked and those that are not. This concept
4
4

4

The notion of boundary-linked cuts was already used in previous works, but not in the context of sparsification and streaming
[
GRST21
,
Li21
]
., which we introduce next, is key to our goal: it captures the cuts that we are able to sparsify to within multiplicative error. Loosely speaking, a cut of
G[U]^{\circ}
is boundary-linked if its local cut is a decent fraction of its global cut.

Definition 5.4

(Boundary-linked cuts)
.

Let
\beta\in(0,1)
be a parameter, let
U\subseteq V
be a cluster, and let
S\subseteq U
be a cut. We say that
S
is
\beta
-boundary-linked in
U
if

{\partial}_{U}S\geq\beta\cdot\min\{{\partial}S,{\partial}(U\setminus S)\}\,.

We shall then group the cuts of
G[U]^{\circ}
into boundary-linked ones and those that are not. As shown by the two lemmas below, each of these groups has a useful properties for our goal: for a correct set of parameters, boundary-linked cuts are very well approximated by a sparsifier, while those that are not boundary-linked must be sparse.

Lemma 5.5

(Characterization of boundary-linked cuts)
.

Let
b,\phi,c,\delta\in(0,1)
such that
c\leq 1/2
is a constant,
\phi\leq b
,
b\leq c
, and
\delta\leq c^{2}b/\log n
. Fix a cluster
U\subseteq V
and also let
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
such that
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
. Then, the following hold:

1.

for all non
b/2
-boundary-linked cuts
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
we have
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\phi
, and

2.

for all
b/2
-boundary-linked cuts
S\subseteq U
we have
(1-\frac{c}{\log n})\cdot{\partial}_{U}S\leq\partial^{w}_{U}S\leq(1+\frac{c}{\log n})\cdot{\partial}_{U}S
.

Proof.

To show (
1
), let
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
be a cut with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)\geq\phi
. By definition this means that
{\partial}_{U}S\geq\phi\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S),\operatorname{vol}_{S}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)\}
. Since
{\partial}_{U}S={\partial}_{U}(U\setminus S)
, we can in fact assume without loss of generality that
{\partial}_{U}S\geq\phi\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)
. Recalling how these volumes are defined, we have that every vertex in
u\in S
has a contribution of at least
b\phi\cdot\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(\{u\})
to
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)
, so
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\geq b/\phi\cdot\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)
. Hence,
{\partial}_{U}S\geq\phi\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)
yields
{\partial}_{U}S\geq b\cdot\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)
. Finally, we observe that the global cut of
S
, i.e.
{\partial}S
, can be decomposed as the sum of
{\partial}_{U}S
and
\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)
. By virtue of this decomposition, from
{\partial}_{U}S\geq b\cdot\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)
we get
{\partial}_{U}S\geq b\cdot({\partial}S-{\partial}_{U}S)
. Rearranging the terms, and since we assume that
b
is bounded by
c\leq 1/2
, we conclude
{\partial}_{U}S\geq b/2\cdot{\partial}S
, i.e.
S
is
b/2
-boundary-linked.

To show (
2
), from definition of boundary-linked we know
{\partial}_{U}S\geq b/2\cdot\min\{{\partial}S,{\partial}(U\setminus S)\}
, while from the assumption that
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
we know
|{\partial}_{U}S-\partial^{w}_{U}S|\leq\delta\cdot{\partial}S
. Again, without loss of generality we can assume
{\partial}_{U}S\geq b/2\cdot{\partial}S
, since
{\partial}_{U}S={\partial}_{U}(U\setminus S)
and
\partial^{w}_{U}S=\partial^{w}_{U}(U\setminus S)
. Therefore, the sparsification error
|{\partial}_{U}S-\partial^{w}_{U}S|
can actually be bounded by
\delta\cdot{\partial}S\leq c^{2}b/\log n\cdot 2/b\cdot{\partial}_{U}S\leq c/\log n\cdot{\partial}_{U}S
(since
c\leq 1/2
).
∎

5.2
Preserving volumes and sparsities

Lemma

5.5
says that for any cut
S
in
U
, either
S
is sparse, or the sparsifier gives a multiplicative approximation to its local cut. Intuitively, for the latter cuts, one would expect the sparsifier to preserve their sparsity as well. However, this is not trivially true, because the sparsity depends on both the local cut and the volume in the boundary-linked subgraph: while we have a multiplicative preservation of boundary-linked cuts, we may not have the same guarantee for their volume. To circumvent this issue, we consider the case of sparse and expanding cuts separately: the former have in fact their volumes preserved within a relative error, while for the latter we get that their volume in the sparsifier is still not too large. We combine these observations by treating separately boundary-linked and non boundary-linked cuts, and conclude that the distinction between sparse and expanding is very accurately preserved in a sparsifier. We begin with analysing the volume of sparse cuts in the sparsifier.

Lemma 5.6

(Multiplicative error for volumes of sparse cuts)
.

Let
b,\phi,\widehat{\phi},c,\delta\in(0,1)
such that
c\leq 1/5
is a constant,
\phi<b
,
\delta\leq c^{2}/\log n
, and
\widehat{\phi}\leq\phi/b
. Fix a cluster
U\subseteq V
and also let
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
such that
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
. Then, for all cuts
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\widehat{\phi}
we have

\left(1-\frac{c}{\log n}\right)\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S)\leq\left(1+\frac{c}{\log n}\right)\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\,.

Proof.

By definition of
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S)
, the error in estimating the volume can be written as

\left|\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S)-\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\right|\leq\left|\operatorname{vol}^{w}(S)-\vol_{U}(S)\right|+\frac{b-\phi}{\phi}\left|\operatorname{bnd}_{U}^{w}(S)-\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)\right|\,.

(5.1)

The first term is easily upper-bounded by
\delta\cdot\vol(S)
, because of the global cut preservation property of
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
(see
Definition

4.1
) applied to singleton cuts. For the second term, we can further split it as

\left|\operatorname{bnd}_{U}^{w}(S)-\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)\right|\leq\left|\partial^{w}S-{\partial}S\right|+\left|\partial^{w}_{U}S-{\partial}_{U}S\right|\,,

(5.2)

We now upper-bound the right hand side using again the cut preservation properties from
Definition

4.1
: we employ the multiplicative error for the global cut
{\partial}S
, and the additive error for
{\partial}_{U}S
. Combining these with the assumption that
S
is sparse, one gets

\displaystyle\Big|\partial^{w}S-{\partial}S\Big|+\Big|\partial^{w}_{U}S-{\partial}_{U}S\Big|

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta\cdot{\partial}S

(5.3)

\displaystyle=2\delta\cdot\big({\partial}_{U}S+\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)\big)

(5.4)

since
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\widehat{\phi}

\displaystyle<2\delta\cdot\widehat{\phi}\cdot\vol_{U}^{\circ}(S)+2\delta\cdot\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)

(5.5)

since
\widehat{\phi}\leq\phi/b
and
\vol_{U}^{\circ}(S)\geq\frac{\phi}{b}\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta\cdot\frac{\phi}{b}\cdot\vol_{U}^{\circ}(S)+2\delta\cdot\frac{\phi}{b}\cdot\vol_{U}^{\circ}(S)

(5.6)

\displaystyle\leq 4\delta\cdot\frac{\phi}{b-\phi}\cdot\vol_{U}^{\circ}(S)\,,

(5.7)

We now backtrack and plug (
5.7
) into (
5.2
), and then (
5.2
) into (
5.1
) to conclude the claim:

\displaystyle\left|\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S)-\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\operatorname{vol}^{w}(S)-\vol_{U}(S)\right|+\frac{b-\phi}{\phi}\left|\operatorname{bnd}_{U}^{w}(S)-\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\delta\cdot\vol(S)+4\delta\cdot\vol_{U}^{\circ}(S)

\displaystyle\leq 5\delta\cdot\vol_{U}^{\circ}(S)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{5c^{2}}{\log n}\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\log n}\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\,,

since
c\leq 1/5
.
∎

Using this fact together with the cut preservation properties of a cluster sparsifier, we can already conclude that there cannot be cuts that look “very” expanding in
H[U]^{\circ}
but are actually sparse in
G[U]^{\circ}
. In particular, if the sparsity of a cut in the sparsifier is above
\phi
by a factor that dominates the error of the sparsifier, then its sparsity must be at least
\phi
in the original graph. We show this in
Lemma

5.7
.

Lemma 5.7

(Very expanding cuts in a sparsifier are indeed expanding)
.

Let
b,\phi,\widehat{\phi},c,\delta\in(0,1)
such that
c\leq 1/30
is a constant,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
,
\delta\leq c^{2}b/\log n
, and
\widehat{\phi}\geq(1+\frac{1}{3\log n})\cdot\phi
. Fix a cluster
U\subseteq V
and also let
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
such that
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
. Then, for all cuts
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S)\geq\widehat{\phi}
we have
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)\geq\phi
.

Proof.

For the sake of a contradiction, let
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
be a cut with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S)\geq\widehat{\phi}
such that
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\phi
.

Consider first the case that
S
is
b/2
-boundary-linked. Then we know from
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
(see
Definition

4.1
) that
\partial^{w}_{U}S=(1\pm c/\log n){\partial}_{U}S
. Also, as we are assuming
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\phi
, from
Lemma

5.6
(our parameters verify stronger conditions than what is demanded by the lemma, so it applies) we know
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S)=(1\pm c/\log n)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)
. These two together give
\Phi_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S)<(1+10c/\log n)\phi<\widehat{\phi}
for small enough
c
, a contradiction.

Next consider the case that
S
is not
b/2
-boundary-linked. Then we do not have a multiplicative approximation to
\partial^{w}_{U}S
. However,
S
is
\phi
-sparse in
G[U]^{\circ}
so we still have
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S)=(1\pm c/\log n)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U\setminus S)=(1\pm c/\log n)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)
by Lemma
5.6
. Therefore, because
\widehat{\phi}\geq(1+\frac{1}{3\log n})\phi
we get

\displaystyle\partial^{w}_{U}S

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{c}{\log n}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{3\log n}\right)\cdot\phi\cdot\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)\}

\displaystyle\geq\left(1+\frac{1}{10\log n}\right)\cdot\phi\cdot\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)\}\,,

and at the same time the additive error approximation from
Definition

4.1
is enough to yield

\partial^{w}_{U}S\leq{\partial}_{U}S+\delta\cdot{\partial}S<\phi\cdot\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)\}+\delta\cdot{\partial}S\,.

Therefore,

\displaystyle{\partial}S

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\delta\cdot 10\log n}\cdot\phi\cdot\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)\}

since
\delta\leq c^{2}b/\log n

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{10c^{2}}\cdot\min\{\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S),\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(U\setminus S)\}\,.

Now let us assume
\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)\leq\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(U\setminus S)
without loss of generality. Then the above is essentially saying that
{\partial}S
is much larger than
\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)
. Recalling now that we can decompose
{\partial}S={\partial}_{U}S+\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)
, the above lower bound is actually saying that much of the advantage that
{\partial}S
has over
\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)
must come from
{\partial}_{U}S
. More precisely, since
c\leq 1/30
we get

{\partial}_{U}S\geq\frac{1}{c}\cdot{\partial}S>\frac{b}{2}\cdot{\partial}S\,,

which contradicts the assumption that
S
is not
b/2
-boundary-linked.
∎

Next, we handle the case of expanding cuts, and show that their volume does not get overshot too much. Even though this is a much weaker guarantee than the one we obtained in Lemma
5.6
for sparse cuts, it will still be enough for our purposes: this will later be useful in concluding that their sparsity has not dropped by a lot.

Lemma 5.8

(Upper bound for volumes of expanding cuts)
.

Let
b,\phi,\widehat{\phi},c,\delta\in(0,1)
such that
c\leq 1/2
is a constant,
\phi\leq b
,
\delta\leq c^{2}/\log n
, and
\widehat{\phi}\leq\phi/b
. Fix a cluster
U\subseteq V
and also let
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
such that
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
. Then, for all cuts
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)\geq\widehat{\phi}
we have

\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U\setminus S)\}\leq\frac{1+\frac{c}{\log n}}{\widehat{\phi}}\cdot{\partial}_{U}S\,.

Proof.

Let
S^{*}\in\{S,U\setminus S\}
be the side of the cut achieving the minimum
\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)\}
. Even though
\operatorname{bnd}_{U}^{w}(S^{*})
may not be a very good approximation of
\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S^{*})
, we can upper-bound it by decomposing
\partial^{w}S^{*}=\partial^{w}_{U}S+\operatorname{bnd}_{U}^{w}(S)
and using the local cut preservation property of
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
(see
Definition

4.1
): we get

\displaystyle\operatorname{bnd}_{U}^{w}(S^{*})

\displaystyle=\partial^{w}S^{*}-\partial^{w}_{U}S^{*}\leq\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S^{*})+\delta\cdot{\partial}S^{*}\,.

Using the above bound, together with the global approximation from
Definition

4.1
applied to singleton cuts, we can upper-bound
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S^{*})
as

\displaystyle\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S^{*})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{vol}^{w}(S)+\frac{b-\phi}{\phi}\operatorname{bnd}_{U}^{w}(S)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta)\cdot\vol(S^{*})+\frac{b-\phi}{\phi}\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S^{*})+\frac{b-\phi}{\phi}\cdot\delta\cdot{\partial}S^{*}

since
{\partial}S^{*}={\partial}_{U}S+\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta)\cdot\vol(S^{*})+(1+\delta)\cdot\frac{b-\phi}{\phi}\cdot\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(S^{*})+\frac{b-\phi}{\phi}\cdot\delta\cdot{\partial}_{U}S^{*}

by definition of
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta)\cdot\vol_{U}^{\circ}(S^{*})+\frac{b-\phi}{\phi}\cdot\delta\cdot{\partial}_{U}S^{*}

since
\widehat{\phi}\leq\frac{\phi}{b}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta)\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S^{*})+\delta\cdot\frac{1}{\widehat{\phi}}\cdot{\partial}_{U}S^{*}\,.

By our assumption that
\Phi_{U}^{b/\phi}(S)\geq\widehat{\phi}
, we also know
\vol_{U}^{b/\phi}(S^{*})\leq\frac{1}{\widehat{\phi}}{\partial}_{U}S
. We then conclude

\displaystyle\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S^{*})

\displaystyle\leq(1+2\delta)\cdot\frac{1}{\widehat{\phi}}{\partial}_{U}S\leq\left(1+\frac{c}{\log n}\right)\frac{1}{\widehat{\phi}}{\partial}_{U}S\,,

since
c\leq 1/2
.
∎

Having a bound on the volumes of expanding cuts, allows to bound the sparsity of those cuts in the sparsifier (since all such cuts are boundary-linked, and hence the local cut is well preserved). This is almost the reverse of
Lemma

5.7
.

Lemma 5.9

(Expanding cuts are almost expanding in the sparsifier)
.

Let
b,\phi,\widehat{\phi},c,\delta\in(0,1)
such that
c\leq 1/6
is a constant,
\phi\leq b
,
b\leq c
,
\delta\leq c^{2}b/\log n
, and
\phi\leq\widehat{\phi}\leq\phi/b
. Fix a cluster
U\subseteq V
and also let
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
such that
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
. Then, for all cuts
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)\geq\widehat{\phi}
we have
\Phi_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S)\geq(1-\frac{1}{3\log n})\widehat{\phi}
.

Proof.

First note that our parameter regime is strictly stronger than that of
Lemma

5.5
and
Lemma

5.8
. Hence, by
Lemma

5.5
, we know that
S
is
b/2
-boundary-linked, since it is
\widehat{\phi}
-expanding and
\widehat{\phi}\geq\phi
.
Lemma

5.5
then also guarantees that
\partial^{w}_{U}S\geq(1-\frac{c}{\log n}){\partial}_{U}S
. On the other hand,
Lemma

5.8
gives
\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U\setminus S)\}\leq(1+\frac{c}{\log n})/{\widehat{\phi}}\cdot{\partial}_{U}S
. Hence,

\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U\setminus S)\}\leq\frac{1+\frac{c}{\log n}}{1-\frac{c}{\log n}}\frac{1}{\widehat{\phi}}\cdot\partial^{w}_{U}S\leq\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{3\log n}}\cdot\frac{1}{\widehat{\phi}}\cdot\partial^{w}_{U}S\,,

since
c\leq 1/6
is small enough.
∎

5.3
Proving the proxying lemma

From the previous section, we have that the distinction between sparse and expanding cuts of
G[U]^{\circ}
is very accurately preserved by
H[U]^{\circ}
, and also the volume of sparse cuts of
G[U]^{\circ}
is approximated to within a small relative error in
H[U]^{\circ}
. As these are exactly the quantities that a BSCA is interested in, we can then prove the main lemma of the section, restated here for convenience of the reader.

See
5.3

Proof.

By definition of
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
, we know that
\omega
is a
((1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi,\alpha,\lambda,0)
-BSCW of
H[U]^{\circ}
, i.e.

1.

if
\omega=\bot
, then
H[U]^{\circ}
is a
(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi
-expander;

2.

if
\omega=(R,\nu)
, then:

(a)

\Phi_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)<(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\alpha\cdot\phi
,

(b)

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U\setminus R)
,

(c)

for every other cut
\emptyset\neq T\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)<(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(T)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U\setminus T)
one has
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(T)\leq B\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)
:

(d)

\nu=\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)
.

Our goal is to translate these properties to
G[U]^{\circ}
by showing that
\omega
is a
(\phi,(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[U]^{\circ}
, i.e.

1.

if
\omega=\bot
, then
G[U]^{\circ}
is a
\phi
-expander;

2.

if
\omega=(R,\nu)
, then:

(a)

\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(R)<(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\cdot\alpha\cdot\phi
,

(b)

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq(1+c)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus R)
,

(c)

for every other cut
\emptyset\neq T\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(R)<\phi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus T)
one has
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)\leq(1+c)B\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)
:

(d)

(1-c)\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq\nu\leq(1+c)\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)
.

Correctness of the case
\omega=\bot
.

The idea is that if the cluster
U
is non-trivially more than a
\phi
-expander in the sparsifier, then it should be at least a
\phi
-expander in
G
. Let us say that
\omega=\bot
, i.e.
\Phi_{U}^{\circ\,w}(S)\geq(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi
for all
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
. Let
\widehat{\phi}=(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi
, so that we are in the parameter regime of
Lemma

5.7
. Then, this lemma ensures that
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)\geq\phi
for all
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
. In other words,
G[U]^{\circ}
is a
\phi
-expander.

Correctness of the case
\omega=(R,\nu)
.

Assume
\omega=(R,\nu)
. We need to show the four properties of a BSCW for
G[U]^{\circ}
.

•

Property (
2a
)
. Intuitively,
R
cannot be much more expanding in
G
than what it looks like in
H
, so if it is sparse in
H
it is basically just as sparse in
G
. To prove this formally, it is convenient to distinguish the case of this cut being boundary-linked or not.

If
R
is not
b/2
-boundary-linked, then
Lemma

5.5
implies
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(R)<\phi
. The property is then already proved in this case.

If it is
b/2
-boundary-linked, suppose for the sake of a contradiction that
R
has
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(R)\geq(1+{1}/{\log n})\alpha\cdot\phi
. Let
\widehat{\phi}=(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha\cdot\phi
. This value of
\widehat{\phi}
fulfils the condition that
\widehat{\phi}\leq 1/b
from
Lemma

5.9
, since
\alpha\leq 1/(2b)
by assumption. We then meet the requirements to apply
Lemma

5.9
(all other parameters also meet its preconditions, as our parameter regime for
b,\phi,c,\phi
is no weaker than its), so

\Phi_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{3\log n}\right)\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{\log n}\right)\cdot\alpha\cdot\phi\geq\left(1+\frac{1}{2\log n}\right)\alpha\cdot\phi,

which contradicts the definition of
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
. We have then showed property (
2a
).

•

Properties (
2b
) and (
2d
)
. From the previous point we know that
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(R)<(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha\cdot\phi
. Since our parameter regime is only stronger than that of
Lemma

5.6
if we set
\widehat{\phi}=(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha\cdot\phi
, we get
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)=(1\pm c/\log n)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U\setminus R)=(1\pm c/\log n)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus R)
. Recalling that
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U\setminus R)
(see (
2b
) for
H[U]^{\circ}
), this yields

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq\frac{1}{1-\frac{c}{\log n}}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)\leq\frac{1}{1-\frac{c}{\log n}}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U\setminus R)\leq\frac{1+\frac{c}{\log n}}{1-\frac{c}{\log n}}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus R)\,,

so
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq(1+c)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus R)
. Recalling that
\nu=\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)
(see (
2d
) for
H[U]^{\circ}
), one also has

\left|\nu-\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\right|=\left|\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)-\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\right|\leq c/\log n\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq c\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\,.

•

Property (
2c
)
. This property aims to bound the volume of
R
against the volume of every other
\phi
-sparse cut. We want to exploit the bound that
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
gives for these cuts in
H
, i.e. that for every cut
\emptyset\neq T\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)<(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\phi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(T)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U\setminus T)
one has
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(T)\leq\lambda\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)
(see (
2c
) for
H[U]^{\circ}
). Now one can see that if we prove all such
T
to be also
\phi
-sparse in
G[U]^{\circ}
, then we could conclude simply using the fact that the volumes of
H[U]^{\circ}
are good proxies for those in
G[U]^{\circ}
. We now implement this strategy.

By virtue of property (
2a
), we have that
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(R)<(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha\cdot\phi
, and from an application of
Lemma

5.6
we have
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)=(1\pm c/\log n)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)
(again, the preconditions of the lemma are met if we take
\widehat{\phi}=(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha\cdot\phi
).

Consider now any other cut
\emptyset\neq T\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(T)<\phi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus T)
. Note that for any such
T
we have
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(T)=(1\pm c/\log n)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U\setminus T)=(1\pm c/\log n)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus T)
, again by applying
Lemma

5.6
(setting
\widehat{\phi}=\phi\leq\phi/b
, and all other parameters satisfy the needed preconditions).

Moreover, any such
T
is also
((1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\ \cdot\phi)
-sparse in
H[U]^{\circ}
, by applying Lemma
5.7
with parameter
\widehat{\phi}=(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\phi\geq(1+\frac{1}{3\log n})\phi
. From (
2c
) for
H[U]^{\circ}
, we then know

\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(T),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U\setminus T)\}\leq\lambda\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)\,.

Recall that
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)
,
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(T)
,
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U\setminus T)
all are within a
(1\pm c/\log n)
multiplicative error of
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)
,
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)
,
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus T)
respectively. Hence,
\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(T),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U\setminus T)\}\leq\lambda\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(R)
translates to

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)=\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus T)\}\leq(1+10c/\log n)\lambda\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq(1+c)\lambda\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\,.

We have then proved (
2c
).
∎

6
Space efficient recursive partitioning

The main goal of this section is to show Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
, restated here for convenience.

See
2.1

See
2.2

The only difference between the algorithms of Theorem
2.1
and
2.2
lies in the balanced sparse cut subroutine that they employ. Therefore, we present and analyse them in a unified manner by deferring the choice of the balanced sparse cut algorithm to later. Using the results from
Section

4
and
Section

5
(i.e.
Lemma

4.2
and
Lemma

5.3
respectively), we prove the following.

Lemma 6.1

(Unified algorithm)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph given in a dynamic stream, let
b\in(0,1)
be a parameter such that
b\leq{1}/{\log n}
. Let
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
be an
(\alpha,\lambda)
-BSCA where
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{b\log n}
and
\lambda=O(1)
. Also let
k
be an integer such that
k\geq{\log{(n^{5}\alpha)}}/{\log{(b^{-1/2}}/{\lambda})}
, and
k\leq\log n
. Then there is an algorithm that maintains a linear sketch of
G
in
\widetilde{O}(n/b^{3})\cdot\alpha^{O(k)}
space. For all
\epsilon\in[n^{-2},b\log n]
, the algorithm decodes the sketch using
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
to compute, with high probability, a
(b,\epsilon,\phi,\gamma)
-BLD of
G
for

\phi=\Omega\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha\log n}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad\gamma=O(\alpha^{k+1})\,.

Moreover, the decoding runs in
\widetilde{O}(n/b^{3})\cdot\alpha^{O(k)}+S(n,\widetilde{O}(n/b^{2})\cdot\alpha^{O(k)})
space and
T(n,\widetilde{O}(n/b^{2})\cdot\alpha^{O(k)})\cdot\poly(n)
time, where
S(p,q)
and
T(p,q)
, denote the space and time complexity of
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
on a graph with
p
vertices and
q
edges respectively. Furthermore, the decoding only makes calls to
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(\cdot,\psi)
with sparsity parameters
\psi\in(0,1)
that satisfy
\psi\leq\frac{1}{10\alpha}
.

From Lemma
6.1
one readily obtains the theorems by either using a brute force
(1,1)
-BSCA or a polynomial time
(O(\log^{3}n),O(1))
-BSCA.

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

It is easy to get an exponential time
(1,1)
-BSCA as per Definition
5.2
. Given a graph and a parameter
\psi\in(0,1)
, one can do this by brute-forcing all possible cuts in the input graph: either return
\omega=\bot
if all the cuts are
\psi
-expanding, or return
\omega=(R,\nu)
where
R
is the
\psi
-sparse cut with maximum volume, and
\nu
is its volume. The space requirement of this algorithm is linear in the size of its input.

We then have parameters
\alpha=1,\lambda=1
, and we set
k=\log n
. Hence, for any
b\leq 1/\log^{2}n
and
\epsilon\leq b\log n
, we meet the preconditions of
Lemma

6.1
:
b\leq 1/\log n
,
\alpha=1\leq\frac{1}{b\log n}
,
\lambda=O(1)
,
k\leq\log n
, and

\frac{\log{n^{5}\alpha}}{\log\frac{1}{\lambda\sqrt{b}}}\leq\frac{O(\log n)}{\Omega(\log\log n)}\leq k\,,

where the first inequality follows because
\alpha=\lambda=1
, and
b\leq 1/\log^{2}n
.
Therefore, by
Lemma

6.1
there is a dynamic stream algorithm that with high probability outputs a
(b,\epsilon,\phi,\gamma)
-BLD of
G
where

\phi=\Omega\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha\log n}\right)=\Omega\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\log n}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad\gamma=O(\alpha^{k+1})=O(1)\,.

Its space complexity is

\widetilde{O}(k\cdot n/b^{3})\cdot\alpha^{O(k)}=\widetilde{O}(n/b^{3})\,,

since for the brute force algorithm we have
S(p,q)=\widetilde{O}(q)
. The running time is
2^{O(n)}
since the brute force algorithm has
T(p,q)=\widetilde{O}(q)\cdot 2^{p}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.2
.

For the polynomial time version, we use the following BSCA.

Theorem 6.2

(
[
SW19
,
LNPS23
,
LR99
]
)
.

For
b\in(0,1)
, let
p\leq n
,
q\leq\min\{p^{2},n/b^{2}\cdot\log^{O(\frac{\log n}{\log 1/b})}n\}
and
W\leq\poly(n)
be integers.
For
p
-vertex
q
-edge input graphs with weighted edges and weighted self-loops, and total volume bounded by
W
, there is a
(c_{\sw}\log^{3}n,C_{\sw})
-BSCA that runs in
\poly(n)
time and
n/b^{2}\cdot\log^{O(\frac{\log n}{\log 1/b})}n
bits of space, uses
n/b^{2}\cdot\log^{O(\frac{\log n}{\log 1/b})}n
random bits, and works with probability
1-1/\poly(n)
for every input sparsity parameter
0<\psi\leq\frac{1}{10c_{\sw}\log^{3}n}
, where
c_{\sw},C_{\sw}\geq 1
are absolute constants.

Hereafter let us set
\alpha=c_{\sw}\log^{3}n,\lambda=C_{\sw}
and
k=\frac{\log{n^{5}\alpha}}{\log{b^{-1/2}}/{\lambda}}
. We want to apply
Lemma

6.1
in this parameter regime with
Theorem

6.2
as
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
.

One can see that the decoding of
Lemma

6.1
(i.e.
Algorithms

3
and
4
) calls
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
on disjoint portions of
b^{-1}\cdot O(k\log n)=\widetilde{O}(1/b)
many sparsifiers (see the proof of
Lemma

6.1
). Then, because each sparsifier has at most
\widetilde{O}(n/b^{2})\cdot\alpha^{O(k)}=\widetilde{O}(n/b^{2})\cdot\log^{O(\frac{\log n}{\log 1/b})}n
edges (see the proof of
Lemma

6.1
), we only need
\widetilde{O}(n/b^{3})\cdot\log^{O(\frac{\log n}{\log 1/b})}n
random bits in order to avoid adaptivity and at the same time ensure that all the calls work correctly with probability
1-1/\poly(n)
. As this fits into our claimed space budget, we can then assume that this BSCA always outputs correctly. From
Lemma

6.1
we also know that the algorithm never calls
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
with sparsity parameters larger than
\frac{1}{10\alpha}
, so our setting matches the requirement of
Theorem

6.2
. We defer the proof of
Theorem

6.2
to
Section

A.1
.

We now verify the rest of the preconditions of
Lemma

6.1
. For any
b\leq 1/\log^{5}n
and
\epsilon\leq b\log n
, have that
\lambda=C_{\sw}=O(1)
and

\alpha=c_{\sw}\log^{3}n\leq\log^{4}n\leq\frac{1}{b\log n}\,.

We also have that
k=O(\log n/\log\log n)\leq\log n
, since
\alpha=O(\log^{3}n)
,
\lambda=O(1)
, and
b\leq 1/\log^{5}n
. Therefore, the lemma gives a dynamic stream algorithm that with high probability outputs a
(b,\epsilon,\phi,\gamma)
-BLD of
G
where

\phi=\Omega\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha\log n}\right)=\Omega\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\log^{4}n}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad\gamma=O(\alpha^{k+1})=\log^{O(\frac{\log n}{\log 1/b})}n\,.

Since the BSCA from
Theorem

6.2
takes at most
n/b^{2}\cdot\log^{O(\frac{\log n}{\log 1/b})}n
bits of space, the overall space complexity is

\widetilde{O}(k\cdot n/b^{3})\cdot\alpha^{O(k)}+n/b^{2}\cdot\log^{O(\frac{\log n}{\log 1/b})}n=n/b^{3}\cdot\log^{O(\frac{\log n}{\log 1/b})}n\,.

The running time is
\poly(n)
, since the BSCA from
Theorem

6.2
takes at most
\poly(n)
time.
∎

Overview of the algorithm.

We now give the BLD construction for Lemma
6.1
. Algorithms
3
and
4
outline the core procedure, which is almost the same as the one of
[
FKM23
]
. For BLD parameters
b
and
\epsilon
we set
\phi
appropriately in terms of
\epsilon
, and our goal is to show that calling Algorithm
3
as
\textsc{Decompose}(V,0)
gives a BLD of
G
.

Algorithm 3

Decompose
: space efficient implementation of
Algorithm

1
for BLD construction

1:

\triangleright

b,\phi\in(0,1)
are global BLD parameters

2:

\triangleright

\alpha,\lambda
are the parameters of
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}

3:

\triangleright

C\geq 1,\,c\in(0,1)
are constant parameters

4:

procedure

\textsc{Decompose}(U,\ell)

\triangleright
cluster
U\subseteq V
, recursion level
\ell\geq 0

5:

H=(V,E^{\prime},w)\leftarrow\textsc{Sparsifier}^{c}_{\ell}(U,b)

\triangleright
sparsifier for
U
in this level

6:

\omega\leftarrow\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[U]^{\circ},(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi)

7:

8:

if

\omega=\bot

then

return

\{U\}

\triangleright
if
U
is an expander, return

9:

(R,\nu)\leftarrow\omega

\triangleright
otherwise, if
R
is balanced enough, recurse on both sides

10:

if

\nu\geq\frac{1}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U)

then

return

\textsc{Decompose}(R,\ell+1)\,\cup\,\textsc{Decompose}(U\setminus R,\ell+1)

11:

12:

(S,\exp)\leftarrow\textsc{Trim}(U,\ell)

\triangleright
find an expander inside
U
, or settle for a less sparse balanced cut

13:

if

\exp=\top

then

\triangleright
if
S
induces an expander, recurse on the complementary only

14:

return

\{S\}\cup\textsc{Decompose}(U\setminus S,\ell+1)

15:

else
\triangleright
otherwise,
S
should be decently sparse and balanced, so recurse on both sides

16:

return

\textsc{Decompose}(S,\ell+1)\,\cup\,\textsc{Decompose}(U\setminus S,\ell+1)

Algorithm 4

Trim
: trim some cuts to find an expander, or find a less sparse but balanced cut

1:

\triangleright

b,\phi\in(0,1)
are global BLD parameters

2:

\triangleright

\alpha,\lambda
are the parameters of
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}

3:

\triangleright

C\geq 1,\,c\in(0,1)
are constant parameters

4:

\triangleright

k
is a positive integer

5:

procedure

\textsc{Trim}(U,\ell)
\triangleright
cluster
U\subseteq V
, recursion level
\ell\geq 0

6:

b_{0}\leftarrow b,\,\,\,\phi_{0}\leftarrow\phi,\,\,\,A\leftarrow U

7:

for

j=1,\dots,k+1

do

8:

b_{j}\leftarrow\frac{b_{j-1}}{(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\cdot\alpha},\,\,\,\phi_{j}\leftarrow\frac{\phi_{j-1}}{(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\cdot\alpha}

9:

10:

for

h=1,2,\dots,\infty

do

11:

H=(V,E^{\prime},w)\leftarrow\textsc{Sparsifier}^{c}_{\ell,j,h}(A,b_{j})
\triangleright
sparsifier for
A
in this level and iteration

12:

\omega\leftarrow\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[A]^{\circ},(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi_{j})

13:

14:

if

\omega=\bot

then

return

(A,\top)

\triangleright

\top
means
A
is an expander

15:

(R,\nu)\leftarrow\omega

16:

17:

if

\nu\geq\frac{1}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U)

then

return

(R,\bot)

\triangleright

\bot
means
R
is a cut

18:

if

\nu\geq\frac{1}{C\lambda}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U)\right)^{1-({j-1})/{k}}

then

19:

A\leftarrow A\setminus R

\triangleright
trim

20:

else

21:

break

\triangleright
go to next outer loop iteration, if this happens we should have
j\leq k

Algorithm
3
is a space efficient implementation of
Algorithm

1
for BLD. In line (
6
), it uses a BSCA on a sparsifier either to certify that the given cluster is already an expander, or to find a sparse cut in it. In the former case it can simply return the cluster. In the latter case, if the condition (
10
) finds the cut to be balanced enough, the algorithm can afford to recurse on both sides keeping the recursion depth under control. Otherwise, it calls Algorithm
4
. This procedure is supposed to either trim off a small volume mass from the cluster in line (
19
) so that the remainder induces an expander (see return statement in line (
14
)), or find a less sparse but more balanced cut than the one obtained in line (
6
) of
Algorithm

3
(see return statement in line (
17
)). In the former case,
Algorithm

3
can then recurse only on the trimmed part of the cluster (recursion of line (
14
)), whereas in the latter it is still acceptable to recurse on both sides while keeping the recursion depth under control (recursion of line (
16
)).

As both algorithms run a BSCA on weighted subgraphs of
G
, we use
Lemma

5.3
to analyse their behaviour with respect to the input
G
. It is then crucial that the graphs on which the BSCA is run are sparsifiers for the cluster at hand. Therefore, we will implement lines (
5
) and (
11
) in
Algorithms

3
and
4
respectively via the algorithm from Lemma
4.2
. Crucially, we want to instantiate enough independent copies of the sparsification algorithm, so that lines (
5
) and (
11
) can always access a fresh sparsifier. The memory requirement is then determined by the sparsifiers that we maintain, while the correctness will be proved using
Lemma

5.3
.

Roadmap.

In the next sections we prove Lemma
6.1
. In
Section

6.1
, we work under the assumption that line (
5
) of
Algorithm

3
and (
11
) of
Algorithm

4
always behave as expected, meaning that they return a sparsifier for the cluster at hand. In this ideal setting, we show that Algorithm
3
outputs a BLD in small recursion depth and few iterations. Then, with
Lemma

4.2
, in
Section

6.2
we lift the assumption that line (
5
) of
Algorithm

3
and (
11
) of
Algorithm

4
are deterministically correct, thus concluding a proof of
Lemma

6.1
.

6.1
Offline analysis

The algorithms have hardwired parameters
k,\alpha,\lambda,C,c
. The BLD parameters
b,\phi\in(0,1)
are given as input (for now, we parametrize the BLD by
b
and
\phi
instead of
b
and
\epsilon
, and will later specify how to define
\phi
from
\epsilon
).
Hereafter, whenever we use
b,\phi,k,C,c,\alpha,\lambda,(b_{j})_{j},(\phi_{j})_{j}
, we are referring to the corresponding parameter in
Algorithms

3
and
4
.

As one can see from the pseudocode, the input graph
G
is not simply fed to
Algorithms

3
and
4
, but they have restricted access to it: their interaction with
G
is limited to lines (
5
) and (
11
) respectively, i.e. they only have sparsifier-access to it. In this section we work under the assumption that the algorithms can always obtain a sparsifier that meets the precondition of
Lemma

5.3
, i.e. they are cluster sparsifiers with appropriate error (see
Definition

4.1
).

Assumption 1
.

Every time line (
5
) of
Algorithm

3
calls
\textsc{Sparsifier}^{c}_{\ell}(U,b)
for some
\ell\geq 0,\,U\subseteq V
, it gets a graph
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
such that
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
, where
\delta=c^{2}b/\log n
.

Assumption 2
.

Every time line (
11
) of
Algorithm

4
calls
\textsc{Sparsifier}^{c}_{\ell,j,h}(A,b_{j})
for some
\ell~\geq~0
,
j,h~\geq~1
,
A\subseteq V
, it gets a graph
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
such that
H[A]\approx_{\delta_{j}}G[A]
, where
\delta_{j}=c^{2}b_{j}/\log n
.

Throughout this section, we will use the definition
\delta=c^{2}b/\log n
and
\delta_{j}=c^{2}b_{j}/\log n
for all
j\in[k+1]
, as in
Assumption

1
and
Assumption

2
. To illustrate why in the second assumption we have
\delta_{j}
instead of
\delta
, we make the following observation.

Observation 6.3
.

For a constant
c\leq 1/30
,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
, a cluster
A~\subseteq~V
, and a sparsifier
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
such that
H[A]\approx_{\delta_{j}}G[A]
,
Lemma

5.3
guarantees that calling an
(\alpha,\lambda)
-BSCA as
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[A]^{\circ},(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi_{j})
gives a
(\phi_{j},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[A]^{\circ}
(as in line (
12
) of
Algorithm

4
).

Proof of
Observation

6.3
.

For a constant
c\leq 1/30
, parameters
b,\phi,\delta,\alpha
such that
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
, a cluster
A~\subseteq~V
, and a sparsifier
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
such that
H[A]\approx_{\delta}G[A]
,
Lemma

5.3
guarantees that calling an
(\alpha,\lambda)
-BSCA as
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[A]^{\circ},(1+0.5/{\log n})\cdot\phi)
gives a
(\phi,(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[A]^{\circ}
. Then, we recall that
G[A]^{\circ}=G[A]^{b/\phi}
and
H[A]^{\circ}=H[A]^{b/\phi}
, so it also holds that
G[A]^{\circ}=G[A]^{b_{j}/\phi_{j}}
and
H[U]^{\circ}=H[U]^{b_{j}/\phi_{j}}
, since we can see from
Algorithm

4
that
b_{j}/\phi_{j}=b/\phi
for all
j\in[k+1]
. Moreover, observe the following:
\phi<b
if and only if
\phi_{j}<b_{j}
,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
implies
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b_{j}}
(by definition of
b_{j}
in
Algorithm

4
), and
b\leq c
implies
b_{j}\leq c
(again, by definition of
b_{j}
in
Algorithm

4
). One can then use
Lemma

5.3
replacing
b,\phi,\delta
with
b_{j},\phi_{j},\delta_{j}
respectively, hence getting
Observation

6.3
.
∎

The goal of this section is threefold: in
Section

6.1.3
, we bound the number of iterations that any execution of
Algorithm

4
can go through; in
Section

6.1.4
, we bound the recursion depth of
Algorithm

3
; in
Section

6.1.5
, we show the output of calling
Algorithm

3
as
\textsc{Decompose}(V,0)
is a BLD of
G
. Interestingly,
Section

6.1.5
crucially uses the results from
Section

6.1.3
and
Section

6.1.4
about recursion depth and number of iterations. Before, we give some preliminary results: in
Section

6.1.1
we give properties of cuts of boundary-linked subgraphs; in
Section

6.1.2
, we show how relations between volume estimates (such as the comparisons performed by
Algorithm

3
in line (
10
) and by
Algorithm

4
in lines (
17
) and (
18
)) translate to relations between the actual volumes of the corresponding cuts.

6.1.1
Properties of nested cuts

When
Algorithm

3
makes a cut in the input cluster
U
, this cut is then fed as input to the recursive call in line (
10
), which will in turn try to find a cut inside it. Similarly,
Algorithm

4
makes a cut in the current cluster
A
in line (
19
), and in the next iteration it will try to find a cluster inside the remainder of
A
. The leitmotif of these algorithms is that they try to make cuts inside a cluster that is one side of a cut previously made. It is then useful for the analysis to relate volume and sparsity of nested cuts.

Lemma 6.4

(Properties of nested cut)
.

Let
b,\phi\in(0,1)
and
\delta>0
be such that
\phi\leq b
,
\delta<1/b
. Fix a cluster
U\subseteq V
, let
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
be a cut such that
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\delta\cdot\phi
, and let
T\subseteq S
. Then we have the following relations between volumes:

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)\overset{(1)}{\leq}\operatorname{vol}_{S}^{\circ}(T)\overset{(2)}{<}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)+\delta b\cdot\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)\}\overset{(3)}{\leq}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,.

If it also holds that
\emptyset\neq T\subsetneq S
has
\Phi_{S}^{\circ}(T)<\delta\cdot\phi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T\cup(U\setminus S))<\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
then

\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(T\cup(U\setminus S))<\delta\cdot\phi\,.

Proof.

For convenience let
\psi=\delta\cdot\phi
. Inequality (1) holds simply because
T\subseteq S\subseteq U
, so the number of self-loops attached to
T
cannot decrease when going from
G[U]^{\circ}
to
G[S]^{\circ}
. Inequality (2) can be obtained by rewriting the volumes in
G[S]^{\circ}
as

\displaystyle\operatorname{vol}_{S}^{\circ}(T)=\vol(T)+\frac{b-\phi}{\phi}\operatorname{bnd}_{S}(T)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)+\frac{b-\phi}{\phi}|E(T,U\setminus S)|

(6.1)

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)+\frac{b}{\phi}|E(T,U\setminus S)|\,.

(6.2)

Since
S
is
\psi
-sparse in
G[U]^{\circ}
, we can upper-bound
|E(T,U\setminus S)|
by
{\partial}_{U}S<\psi\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)\}
, thus getting the bound. For inequality (3), note
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)
because
T\subseteq S
and
\delta b\cdot\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)\}\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)
for
\delta\leq 1/b
. Since
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)+\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)=\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
, we have the bound (3).

Suppose now that
\emptyset\neq T\subsetneq S
has
\Phi_{S}^{\circ}(T)<\psi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T\cup(U\setminus S))<\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
. First observe that because
\Phi_{S}^{\circ}(T)<\psi
, the definition of
\Phi_{S}^{\circ}(T)
yields
{\partial}_{S}T<\psi\operatorname{vol}_{S}^{\circ}(T)
. By
Eq.

6.2
, we also have

{\partial}_{S}T<\psi\operatorname{vol}_{S}^{\circ}(T)\leq\psi\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)+\frac{\psi b}{\phi}\cdot|E(T,U\setminus S)|\leq\psi\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)+|E(T,U\setminus S)|\,,

(6.3)

where we used that
\psi b/\phi\leq 1
since
\psi=\delta\phi
and
\delta\leq 1/b
. Due to
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T\cup(U\setminus S))<\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
, the definition of
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(T\cup(U\setminus S))
finally yields

\displaystyle\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(T\cup(U\setminus S))

\displaystyle=\frac{{\partial}_{U}(T\cup(U\setminus S))}{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T\cup(U\setminus S))}

since
T\subseteq S
, so
T\cap(U\setminus S)=\emptyset

\displaystyle=\frac{{\partial}_{S}T+{\partial}_{U}S-|E(T,U\setminus S)|}{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)+\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)}

by (
6.3
)

\displaystyle<\frac{\psi\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)+|E(T,U\setminus S)|+{\partial}_{U}S-|E(T,U\setminus S)|}{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)+\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\psi\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)+{\partial}_{U}S}{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)+\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)}

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{\psi,\frac{{\partial}_{U}S}{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)}\right\}=\max\left\{\psi,\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)\right\}

since
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\psi

\displaystyle\leq\psi\,.

∎

Algorithm

4
makes cuts iteratively. It finds a cut in the cluster, it trims it off in line (
19
), it finds another cut in the remainder, it trims it off, and so on, provided that these cuts verify certain conditions: their individual volumes are neither too large (if one of them was very large in volume, the algorithm would return in line (
17
)) nor too small (see condition (
18
)). The following lemma proves properties of such cut sequences.
Figure

3
should help the reader to visualize the statement and the proof.

Figure 3:
Sequence of nested cuts.

Lemma 6.5

(Properties of a sequence of nested cuts)
.

Let
b,\phi\in(0,1)
be such that
\phi\leq b
, and
\delta\in(0,1]
. Fix a cluster
U\subseteq V
, let
\theta\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be such that
\theta\leq\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
, and let
R_{1},\dots,R_{t}
be a sequence of cuts
\emptyset\neq R_{i}\subsetneq U^{<i}
where
U^{<i}=U\setminus(\cup_{z=1}^{i-1}R_{z})
for all
i\in[t]
. If

1.

for all
\emptyset\neq X\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(X)<\delta\phi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(X)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus X)
one has
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(X)~<~\theta
, and

2.

for all
i\in[t]
one has
\Phi_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})<\delta\phi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})<\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
,

then we have the following.

1.

For all
i\in[t]
, one has
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i}R_{z})<\delta\phi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i}R_{z})<\theta
.

2.

For all
i\in[t]
, if there is
\theta^{\low}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})\geq\theta^{\low}
then one also has
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R_{i})\geq\theta^{\low}-b\cdot\theta
.

3.

Let
U^{<t+1}=U\setminus(\cup_{z=1}^{t}R_{z})
. If
\emptyset\neq R\subsetneq U^{<t+1}
is a cut such that
\Phi_{U^{<t+1}}^{\circ}(R)<\delta\phi
and
\theta^{\low}\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<t+1}}^{\circ}(R)\leq\frac{5}{9}\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<t+1}}^{\circ}(U^{<t+1})
for some
\theta^{\low}\leq\frac{4}{9}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
, then
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(R)<\delta\phi\cdot\frac{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)}{\theta^{\low}-b\cdot\theta}
and
\theta^{\low}-b\cdot\theta\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq\frac{5}{9}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
.

The end goal of part (
1
) is to upper bound the number of edges crossing and the remaining volume when
Algorithm

4
returns
(A,\top)
and therefore
Algorithm

3
recurses on
U\setminus A
only (see line (
8
) in
Algorithm

3
). The idea is that in this case
U\setminus A
corresponds to the union of nested cuts that are trimmed off by
Algorithm

4
.

Part (
2
) will be useful to bound the number of iterations that
Algorithm

4
can take. In every iteration a cut is trimmed off, and from condition (
18
) we know the volume of these cuts to be not too small in
U^{<i}
. From part (
2
) we then know these volumes to be not too small in
U
either, and hence the number of such cuts cannot be too large, since they are disjoint.

The end goal of part (
3
) is to upper bound the number of edges crossing and the remaining volume when
Algorithm

4
returns
(R,\bot)
and therefore
Algorithm

3
recurses on
R
and
U\setminus R
(see line (
10
) in
Algorithm

3
). The idea is that in this case, every cut trimmed off by
Algorithm

4
is sparse and far from being balanced (because of condition (
17
) in
Algorithm

4
), whereas the last one is both sparse and somewhat balanced (because of condition (
17
) in
Algorithm

4
).

In the remainder of this section we prove the three parts of this lemma.

Proof of
Lemma

6.5
, part (
1
).

Intuitively, the union of a sequence of nested sparse and small cuts should be sparse and small too. We prove this by induction on
i\in[t]
. Denote for convenience
\psi~=~\delta~\phi
.

The base case for
i=1
readily follows. Since
U^{1}=U
, we have
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(R_{1})<\psi
and also
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R_{1})<\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
from precondition (
2
). Moreover, by precondition (
1
), there is no
\psi
-sparse cut
X
with
\theta\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(X)<\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
. Hence, it must be the case that
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R_{1})<\theta
.

For the inductive step, take
i\in[t-1]
and assume that
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i}R_{z})<\psi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i}R_{z})<\theta
. We now apply Lemma
6.4
with
S=U^{<i+1}
and
T=R_{i+1}
. We can do so because
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)=\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i}R_{z})<\psi
, so the lemma gives

\displaystyle\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R_{i+1})\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<i+1}}^{\circ}(R_{i+1})\leq\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)<\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,.

Combining this bound with the inductive hypothesis that
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i}R_{z})<\theta
, one has

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i+1}R_{z})=\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i}R_{z})+\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R_{i+1})<\theta+\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R_{i+1})<\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)+\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)<\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,,

which rewrites as
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T\cup(U\setminus S))<\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
with
S=U^{<i+1}
and
T=R_{i+1}
. Also, from precondition (
2
), we have
\Phi_{U^{<i+1}}^{\circ}(R_{i+1})<\psi
, or in other words
\Phi_{S}^{\circ}(T)<\psi
with
S=U^{<i+1}
and
T=R_{i+1}
. Then, the second part of Lemma
6.4
gives
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i+1}R_{z})<\psi
, so one part of the induction is proved. For the other part, we combine
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i+1}R_{z})<\psi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i+1}R_{z})<\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
with precondition (
1
): since we assume there is no
\psi
-sparse cut in
G[U]^{\circ}
with volume sandwiched between
\theta
and
\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
, it must be the case that
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i+1}R_{z})<\theta
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.5
, part (
2
).

We prove this using part (
1
) and
Lemma

6.4
. The case of
i=1
follows from the preconditions, since
U^{1}=U
. Let then
i=2,\dots,t
, and denote for convenience
\psi~=~\delta~\phi
. Then, part (
1
) gives that
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i-1}R_{z})<\psi
. If we set
S=U^{<i}
and
T=R_{i}
we have
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)=\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i-1}R_{z})<\psi
, and can then apply
Lemma

6.4
to get the lower bound

\displaystyle\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R_{i})

\displaystyle>\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})-\delta b\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i-1}R_{z})

since
\delta\leq 1

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})-b\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i-1}R_{z})\,.

Part (
1
) also gives
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i-1}R_{z})<\theta
, and since we assume that
\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})\geq\theta^{\low}
, one concludes

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R_{i})>\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})-b\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{i-1}R_{z})\geq\theta^{\low}-b\cdot\theta\,.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.5
, part (
3
).

We begin by analysing the volume
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)
. Again, from part (
1
) we have

\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{i=1}^{t}R_{i})<\delta\phi\,,\quad\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{i=1}^{t}R_{i})<\theta\,.

Setting
\psi=\delta\phi
,
S=U^{<t+1}
and
T=R
, we have
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)=\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{t}R_{z})<\psi
, so we can apply
Lemma

6.4
to yield

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)>\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<t+1}}^{\circ}(R)-b\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{i=1}^{t}R_{i})>\theta^{\low}-b\cdot\theta\,,

(6.4)

and

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<t+1}}^{\circ}(R_{t})\leq\frac{5}{9}\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<t+1}}^{\circ}(U^{<t+1})\,.

(6.5)

We can once more apply Lemma
6.4
with
S=T=U^{<t+1}
(since again
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)=\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{t}R_{z})<\psi
), so

\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<t+1}}^{\circ}(U^{<t+1})\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,.

(6.6)

We then have from (
6.4
), (
6.5
), (
6.6
)

\theta^{\low}-b\cdot\theta\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq\frac{5}{9}\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<t+1}}^{\circ}(U^{<t+1})\leq\frac{5}{9}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,.

(6.7)

Next we analyse the sparsity of
R
by first upper-bounding the quantity
{\partial}_{U}R
:

\displaystyle{\partial}_{U}R

\displaystyle\leq{{\partial}_{U^{<t+1}}R+{\partial}_{U}(\cup_{i=1}^{t}R_{i})}

(6.8)

since
\Phi_{U^{<t+1}}^{\circ}(R)<\psi
by assumption

\displaystyle<{\psi\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<t+1}}^{\circ}(R)+{\partial}_{U}(\cup_{i=1}^{t}R_{i})}

(6.9)

since
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{i=1}^{t}R_{i})<\psi
from part (
1
)

\displaystyle<{\psi\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<t+1}}^{\circ}(R)+\psi\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{i=1}^{t}R_{i})}

(6.10)

since
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)<\frac{5}{9}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
from (
6.7
)

\displaystyle<{\psi\cdot\frac{5}{9}\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)+\psi\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{i=1}^{t}R_{i})}

(6.11)

since
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{i=1}^{t}R_{i})<\theta
from part (
1
)

\displaystyle<\psi\cdot\frac{5}{9}\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)+\psi\cdot\theta

(6.12)

by assumption on
\theta

\displaystyle<\psi\cdot{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)}\,.

(6.13)

On the other hand, the bounds in (
6.7
) yield

\displaystyle\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus R)\}

\displaystyle\geq\min\left\{\theta^{\low}-b\cdot\theta,\frac{4}{9}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right\}

(6.14)

since
\theta^{\low}\leq\frac{4}{9}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
by assumption

\displaystyle=\theta^{\low}-b\cdot\theta\,.

(6.15)

From the bound (
6.13
) and (
6.15
) we finally conclude

\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(R)=\frac{{\partial}_{U}R}{\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus R)\}}<\frac{\psi\cdot{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)}}{\theta^{\low}-b\cdot\theta}\,.

∎

6.1.2
Relations between volume estimates

In this section we show how relations between volume estimates (such as the comparisons performed by
Algorithm

3
in line (
10
) and by
Algorithm

4
in lines (
17
) and (
18
)) translate to relations between the actual volumes of the corresponding cuts.

Lemma 6.6

(Relations between volume estimates)
.

Let
b,\phi,c\in(0,1)
be such that
c\leq 1/4
is a constant and
\phi\leq b
. Fix a cluster
U\subseteq V
, and also let
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
such that
H[U]^{\circ}\approx_{c}G[U]^{\circ}
, and let
\omega
be a
(\psi,\alpha^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},c)
-BSCW of
G[U]^{\circ}
for some
\psi\in(0,\phi]
and
\alpha^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}\geq 1
. If
\omega=(R,\nu)
for some
\emptyset\neq R\subsetneq U
and
\nu\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, then for any
\rho,\tau\in[0,1]
and
X\subseteq V
we have

1.

if
\nu\geq\rho\cdot\left(\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ\,w}(X)\right)^{\tau}
, then
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\geq\rho/2\cdot\left(\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ}(X)\right)^{\tau}
.

2.

if
\nu<\rho\cdot\left(\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ\,w}(X)\right)^{\tau}
, then
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)<2\rho\cdot\left(\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ}(X)\right)^{\tau}
and for every cut
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\psi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)
one has
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)<2\lambda^{\prime}\rho\cdot\left(\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ}(X)\right)^{\tau}
.

Proof.

From property (
2d
) of the definition of BSCW (see
Definition

5.1
), we know
(1-c)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq\nu\leq(1+c)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)
, and from property (
2b
) we know that for every cut
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\psi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)
one has
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\lambda^{\prime}\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)
.

On the other hand,
(1-c)\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ}(X)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ\,w}(X)\leq(1+c)\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ}(X)
for any
X\subseteq V
. To see why, first note that
\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ\,w}(X)=\operatorname{vol}^{w}(X)+\frac{b-\phi}{\phi}\operatorname{bnd}_{X}^{w}(X)=\operatorname{vol}^{w}(X)+\frac{b-\phi}{\phi}\partial^{w}X
, and analogously
\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ}(X)=\vol(X)+\frac{b-\phi}{\phi}{\partial}X
. Then, since
H[U]^{\circ}\approx_{c}G[U]^{\circ}
, we have the approximation guarantee for global cuts (see
Definition

4.1
): applying it to all singleton cuts in
X
we can approximate
\vol(X)
, and applying it to
X
we can approximate
{\partial}X
.

From the above discussion it follows that for all
\rho,\tau\in[0,1]
and
X\subseteq V
,
\nu\geq\rho\cdot\left(\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ\,w}(X)\right)^{\tau}
implies
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\geq\rho\frac{1-c}{1+c}\cdot\left(\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ}(X)\right)^{\tau}\geq\rho/2\cdot\left(\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ}(X)\right)^{\tau}
, since
c\leq 1/4
. If instead
\nu<\rho\cdot\left(\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ\,w}(X)\right)^{\tau}
, we have
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)<\rho\frac{1+c}{1-c}\cdot\left(\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ}(X)\right)^{\tau}\leq 2\rho\cdot\left(\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ}(X)\right)^{\tau}
. Moreover, since by definition of
\omega
one has
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\lambda^{\prime}\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)
for every cut
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\psi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)
, we get
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\lambda^{\prime}\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq\lambda^{\prime}2\rho\cdot\left(\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ}(X)\right)^{\tau}
for any such
S
.
∎

6.1.3
Bounding the number of iterations

Combining the structural properties of nested cuts together with
Lemma

6.6
, we can show the correctness of the algorithms. We begin by observing that whenever
Algorithm

4
breaks the inner loop in line (
21
) we have
j\leq k
. This ensures that restraining the outer loop to indices
j=1,\dots,k+1
(see line (
7
)) does not lead to undefined behaviour.

Lemma 6.7

(
Algorithm

4
does few outer loop iterations)
.

Let
b,\phi,c\in(0,1)
such that
c\leq 1/30
is a constant,
\phi<b
and
b\leq c
. Also let
C\geq 3
be a constant, and let
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
be an
(\alpha,\lambda)
-BSCA where
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
. If
Assumption

2
holds, then if Algorithm
4
breaks in line (
21
) it is in some outer loop iteration
j
with
j\leq k
.

Proof.

We prove this by contradiction, so suppose the algorithm enters line (
21
) with
j=k+1
for some inner loop iteration
h\geq 1
. Let
A
be the value of the corresponding variable in
Algorithm

4
at the beginning of inner loop iteration
h
within outer loop iteration
k+1
. Also let
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
be the graph gotten from line (
11
) in the same iteration. From the description of the algorithm, since it entered line (
21
), the call
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[A]^{\circ},(1+{1}/{(2\log n)})\cdot\phi_{k+1})
from line (
12
) has returned a BSCW of the form
(R,\nu)
such that
\nu<\frac{1}{C\lambda}(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U))^{1-(k+1-1)/k}=\frac{1}{C\lambda}(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U))^{0}
. Moreover, since we have
c\leq 1/30
,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
, and
H[A]\approx_{\delta_{k+1}}G[A]
(from
Assumption

2
),
Lemma

5.3
ensures that
(R,\nu)
is a
(\phi_{j},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[A]^{\circ}
(see
Observation

6.3
).

We now want to use Lemma
6.6
with
\psi=\phi_{k+1}
,
\alpha^{\prime}=(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha
,
\lambda^{\prime}=(1+c)\lambda
,
\rho=\frac{1}{C\lambda}
,
\tau=0
, and
X=U
. These parameters verify the conditions for applying
Lemma

6.6
to
(R,\nu)
on
G[A]^{\circ}
:
c\leq 1/30\leq 1/4
,
\psi=\phi_{k+1}\leq\phi
(by definition of
\phi_{k+1}
in the algorithm),
\alpha^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}\geq 1
,
\rho\leq 1
,
\tau\in[0,1]
,
H[A]\approx_{c}G[A]
(since
H[A]\approx_{\delta_{k+1}}G[A]
and
\delta_{k+1}\leq c
), and hence
(R,\nu)
is a
(\phi_{j},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[A]^{\circ}
. Then
Lemma

6.6
gives

\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(R)<2\rho(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U))^{\tau}=\frac{2}{C\lambda}<1\,,

which in particular implies
R=\emptyset
. However, this contradicts the fact that
(R,\nu)
is a BSCW of
G[A]^{\circ}
(see
Definition

5.1
).
∎

Next, we show that
Algorithm

4
verifies the preconditions (
1
) and (
2
) of
Lemma

6.5
. Specifically, if
Algorithm

3
calls
Algorithm

4
, there cannot be sparse and balanced cuts in
U
, and also every cut trimmed by
Algorithm

4
is sparse and small in volume. This will later allow us to apply
Lemma

6.5
for bounding the number of inner loop iterations (and also for bounding the recursion depth, in the next section).

Lemma 6.8

(
Algorithm

4
makes a sequence of nested sparse small cuts)
.

Let
b,\phi,c\in(0,1)
such that
c\leq 1/30
is a constant,
\phi<b
and
b\leq c
. Also let
C\geq 11
be a constant, and let
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
be an
(\alpha,\lambda)
-BSCA where
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
. Consider an execution of
Algorithm

4
called from
Algorithm

3
on input cluster
U\subseteq V
. Also let
R_{1},\dots,R_{q}\subseteq U
be the sequence of
q
cuts trimmed in line (
19
) by
Algorithm

4
, and let
U^{<i}=U\setminus(\cup_{z-1}^{i-1}R_{z})
. If
Assumption

1
and
Assumption

2
hold, then

1.

for all
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\phi
,
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)
, one has
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
,

2.

for all
i\in[q]
one has
\Phi_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})<\phi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})<\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
.

Proof.

From the description of Algorithms
3
and
4
, we see that Algorithm
4
starts only if line (
6
) of
Algorithm

3
returns a BSCW
\omega=(R,\nu)
such that
\nu<\frac{1}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U)
, as per line (
12
) (otherwise,
Algorithm

3
