would not call
Algorithm

4
). Now we want to apply
Lemma

5.3
to
\omega
. Because we have
c\leq 1/30
,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
,
\delta=c^{2}b/\log n
,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
and
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
from
Assumption

1
, all the preconditions of
Lemma

5.3
are met for us to apply it to the call
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[U]^{\circ},(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi)
from line (
6
) of
Algorithm

3
: hence,
\omega
is a
(\phi,(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[U]^{\circ}
. Since we observed just above that
\omega
is of the form
(R,\nu)
and
\nu<\frac{1}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U)
, we now use
Lemma

6.6
with parameters
\psi=\phi
,
\alpha^{\prime}=(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha
,
\lambda^{\prime}=(1+c)\lambda
,
\rho=\frac{1}{C\lambda}
,
\tau=1
, and
X=U
. These parameters verify the requirement of
Lemma

6.6
since we have
c\leq 1/30\leq 1/4
,
H[U]\approx_{c}G[U]
(from
Assumption

1
, since
\delta\leq c
),
\psi\leq\phi
,
\alpha^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}\geq 1
,
\rho,\tau\leq 1
, and
(R,\nu)
is a
(\phi,(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[U]^{\circ}
. Then we get that for every cut
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\phi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)
one has

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)<2\lambda^{\prime}\rho\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)=\frac{2(1+c)\lambda}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\leq\frac{3}{C}\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,.

This gives part (
1
).

For all
i\in[q]
, denote by
j(i)\in[k+1]
the outer loop iteration of
Algorithm

4
where
R_{i}
was trimmed, and also let
U^{<i}=U\setminus(\cup_{z=1}^{i-1}R_{z})
(i.e., at the beginning of the iteration where
R_{i}
has been trimmed we had
A=U^{<i}
). Then, from the description of the algorithm we know that
R_{i}
was paired with a value
\nu_{R_{i}}
such that
(R_{i},\nu_{R_{i}})
was the result of
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[U^{<i}]^{\circ},(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi_{j(i)-1})
, where
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
verifies
H[U^{<i}]\approx_{\delta_{j(i)}}G[U^{<i}]
because of
Assumption

2
. As we have
c\leq 1/30
,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
,
Lemma

5.3
ensures that the pair
(R_{i},\nu_{R_{i}})
is a
(\phi_{j(i)},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[U^{<i}]^{\circ}
for all
i\in[q]
(see
Observation

6.3
). From the description of the algorithm we also know that
\nu_{R_{i}}
must also not satisfy condition (
17
), as otherwise
Algorithm

4
would have returned, so
\nu_{R_{i}}<\frac{1}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U)
for all
i\in[q]
. For each
i\in[q]
, we now use
Lemma

6.6
with parameters
\psi=\phi_{j(i)}
,
\alpha^{\prime}=(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha
,
\lambda^{\prime}=(1+c)\lambda
,
\rho=\frac{1}{C\lambda}
,
\tau=1
, and
X=U
. These parameters verify the requirement for applying
Lemma

6.6
to
(R_{i},\nu_{R_{i}})
in
G[U^{<i}]
:
c\leq 1/30\leq 1/4
,
H[U^{<i}]\approx_{c}G[U^{<i}]
(from
Assumption

2
and
\delta_{j(i)}\leq c
),
\psi=\phi_{j(i)}\leq\phi
,
\alpha^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}\geq 1
,
\rho,\tau\leq 1
, and
(R_{i},\nu_{R_{i}})
is a
(\phi_{j(i)},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[U^{<i}]^{\circ}
. Then, for all
i\in[q]

Lemma

6.6
gives

\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})<\frac{2}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)<\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,,

(6.16)

since
C\geq 11
.
Furthermore, because every
(R_{i},\nu_{R_{i}})
is a
(\phi_{j(i)},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[U^{<i}]^{\circ}
, we have by
Definition

5.1

\Phi_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})<\left(1+\frac{1}{\log n}\right)\alpha\phi_{j(i)}=\left(1+\frac{1}{\log n}\right)\alpha\frac{\phi}{\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{\log n}\right)\alpha\right)^{j(i)}}\leq\phi\,,

(6.17)

since
j(i)\geq 1
. The bounds in (
6.16
) and (
6.17
) give part (
2
).
∎

Finally, we argue that
Algorithm

4
goes through few inner loop iterations within each outer loop iteration. The idea for this is that we can upper bound the total volume trimmed in line (
19
), but we can also lower bound the volume of each individual cut.

Lemma 6.9

(
Algorithm

4
does few inner loop iterations)
.

Let
b,\phi,c\in(0,1)
such that
c\leq 1/30
is a constant,
\phi<b
and
b\leq c
. Also let
C\geq 31
be a constant, let
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
be an
(\alpha,\lambda)
-BSCA where
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
and
\lambda\leq\frac{c}{2b}
, and let
k
be an integer such that
k\geq\log\frac{n^{2}}{\phi}/\log\frac{c}{\lambda\cdot b}
. If
Assumption

1
and
Assumption

2
hold, then
Algorithm

4
goes through at most
1/{b}
inner loop iterations within each outer loop iteration.

Proof.

Let
U
be the input cluster of
Algorithm

4
. Fix hereafter an outer loop iteration
j=1,\dots,k+1
, and let
A
be the value of the corresponding variable of the algorithm at the beginning of the
j
-th outer loop iteration. Because line (
19
) of
Algorithm

4
removes a non-empty cut from
A
at every inner loop iteration, there are finitely many iterations. Denote then by
t
the number of inner loop iterations for outer loop iteration
j
. Then, for every inner loop iteration
h\in[t]
define
A^{<h}
to be the value of variable
A
at the beginning of inner loop iteration
h
.

Since
Assumption

2
holds, we also know that to every inner loop iteration
h\in[t]
corresponds a graph
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
from line (
11
) which satisfies
H[A^{<h}]\approx_{\delta_{j}}G[A^{<h}]
. As we have
c\leq 1/30
,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
, and
H[A^{<h}]\approx_{\delta_{j}}G[A^{<h}]
,
Lemma

5.3
ensures that calling
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[A^{<h}]^{\circ},(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi_{j})
gives a
(\phi_{j},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[A^{<h}]^{\circ}
in line (
12
) of
Algorithm

4
(see
Observation

6.3
). Moreover, from the description of the algorithm, it must be the case that the BSCW obtained is of the form
(R_{h},\nu_{h})
for all
h\in[t-1]
, where
\emptyset\neq R_{h}\subsetneq A^{<h}
and
\nu_{h}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. One can then see that for every
h\in[t]
we have
A^{<h}=A\setminus(\cup_{z=1}^{h-1}R_{z})
, and because of conditions (
17
) and (
18
) we have that for every
h\in[t-1]
the following holds:

\frac{1}{C\lambda}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U)\right)^{1-(j-1)/k}\leq\nu_{h}<\frac{1}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U)\,.

We now want to translate the above bounds to
\operatorname{vol}_{A^{<h}}^{\circ}(R_{h})
using
Lemma

6.6
. To do so, note that
H[A^{<h}]\approx_{\delta_{j}}G[A^{<h}]
in particular implies
H[A^{<h}]\approx_{c}G[A^{<h}]
(since we have defined
\delta_{j}=c^{2}b_{j}/\log n\leq c
), and as discussed before we know that

(R_{h},\nu_{h})
is a
\left(\phi_{j},\left(1+\frac{1}{\log n}\right)\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c\right)
-BSCW of
G[A^{<h}]^{\circ}

(6.18)

Let us then set
\psi=\phi_{j}
,
\alpha^{\prime}=(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha
,
\lambda^{\prime}=(1+c)\lambda
,
\rho=\frac{1}{C\lambda}
,
\tau
being either
1-(j-1)/k
(for the lower bound) or
1
(for the upper bound), and
X=U
. These parameters verify the conditions for applying
Lemma

6.6
to
(R_{h},\nu_{h})
on
G[A^{<h}]^{\circ}
:
c\leq 1/30\leq 1/4
,
\psi=\phi_{j}\leq\phi
,
\alpha^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}\geq 1
,
\rho\leq 1
, both values of
\tau
are bounded by
1
(this is because
j\leq k+1
),
H[A^{<h}]\approx_{c}G[A^{<h}]
(since from
Assumption

2
we have
H[A^{<h}]\approx_{\delta_{j}}G[A^{<h}]
), and
(R_{h},\nu_{h})
is a
(\phi_{j},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[A^{<h}]^{\circ}
(see (
6.18
)). Then
Lemma

6.6
gives

\frac{1}{2C\lambda}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1-(j-1)/k}\leq\operatorname{vol}_{A^{<h}}^{\circ}(R_{h})<\frac{2}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,,

(6.19)

where the lower bound is obtained with
\tau=1-(j-1)/k
and the upper bound with
\tau=1
. The application of
Lemma

6.6
with
\tau=1
also gives that for every cut
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq A^{<h}
with
\Phi_{A^{<h}}^{\circ}(S)<\phi_{j}
and
\operatorname{vol}_{A^{<h}}^{\circ}(S)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{A^{<h}}^{\circ}(A^{<h}\setminus S)
one has

\operatorname{vol}_{A^{<h}}^{\circ}(S)<2\lambda^{\prime}\rho\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)=\frac{2(1+c)\lambda}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\leq\frac{3}{C}\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,.

(6.20)

We consider now the case of
j=1
and
2\leq j\leq k+1
separately.

Case
j=1
.

In this case
A
is identical to the input cluster
U
. We then want to apply Lemma
6.5
with
\delta=1
and
\theta=\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
to the sequence of cuts
R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{t-1}
in the cluster
U
. Let for convenience
U^{i}=U\setminus(\cup_{z=1}^{i-1}R_{z})
for all
i\in[t]
.

First note that this value of
\theta
meets the requirement of
Lemma

6.5
that
\theta<\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
, since
C\geq 16
. Secondly, our set of assumptions matches that of
Lemma

6.8
, so we get that for all
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\phi
,
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)
, one has
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
, and for all
i\in[q]
one has
\Phi_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})<\phi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})<\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
. These facts match preconditions (
1
) and (
2
) of
Lemma

6.5
for
\delta
and
\theta
as we set them above.

Part (
1
) of
Lemma

6.5
then upper-bounds the total volume of the sequence as

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{h=1}^{t-1}R_{h})<\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,.

(6.21)

Since the lower bound of (
6.18
) with
j=1
means that
\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<h}}^{\circ}(R_{h})\geq\frac{1}{2C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
for all
h\in[t-1]
, using part (
2
) of
Lemma

6.5
with
\theta^{\low}=\frac{1}{2C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
we further get

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R_{h})\geq\frac{1}{2C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)-b\cdot\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\geq\frac{1}{3C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\quad\text{ for all $h\in[t-1]$}\,,

(6.22)

since
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{18b}
.
Therefore, taking the ratio of the cumulative upper bound in (
6.21
) and the individual lower bounds from (
6.22
), we conclude that
t-1
cannot exceed

\frac{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{h=1}^{t-1}R_{h})}{\min_{h\in[t-1]}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R_{h})}\leq\frac{\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)}{\frac{1}{3C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)}\leq 9\lambda\,.

Thus, the number of inner loop iterations for
j=1
is at most
9\lambda+1\leq\frac{1}{2b}+1\leq 1/b
.

Case
2\leq j\leq k+1
.

The approach is analogous to the first iteration, except that we use the bounds from
Lemma

6.5
on the sequence
R_{1},\dots,R_{t-1}
in the cluster
A
(for
j=1
we applied the lemma to the cluster
U
). We remark that, in this case,
A
can also be seen as the value of the corresponding variable of
Algorithm

4
at the very end of outer loop iteration
j-1
.

Consider the call
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[A]^{\circ},(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi_{j-1})
to
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
in the last inner loop iteration within outer loop iteration
j-1
, where
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
is accordingly the graph obtained from line (
11
) at the last inner loop iteration within outer loop iteration
{j-1}
. Since
Assumption

2
holds, we know that the graph
H
satisfies
H[A]\approx_{\delta_{j-1}}G[A]
. As we have
c\leq 1/30
,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
, and
H[A]\approx_{\delta_{j-1}}G[A]
,
Lemma

5.3
ensures that the call
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[A]^{\circ},(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi_{j-1})
gives a
(\phi_{j-1},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW
\omega
of
G[A]^{\circ}
(see
Observation

6.3
). On the other hand, from the description of the algorithm we know that iteration
j
starts only if this
\omega
is of the form
(R,\nu)
with
\nu<\frac{1}{C\lambda}(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U))^{1-(j-2)/k}
(due to condition (
18
)). We now use
Lemma

6.6
with parameters
\psi=\phi_{j-1}
,
\alpha^{\prime}=(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha
,
\lambda^{\prime}=(1+c)\lambda
,
\rho=\frac{1}{C\lambda}
,
\tau=1-(j-2)/k
, and
X=U
. These parameters verify the requirement of
Lemma

6.6
since we have
c\leq 1/30\leq 1/4
,
H[A]\approx_{c}G[A]
(from
Assumption

2
and
\delta_{j-1}\leq c
),
\psi=\phi_{j-1}\leq\phi
,
\alpha^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}\geq 1
,
\rho,\tau\leq 1
, and
(R,\nu)
is a
(\phi_{j-1},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c),c\lambda)
-BSCW of
G[A]^{\circ}
. Then we get that for every cut
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq A
with
\Phi_{A}^{\circ}(S)<\phi_{j-1}
and
\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(S)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(A\setminus S)
one has

\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(S)<2\lambda^{\prime}\rho\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{\tau}=\frac{2(1+c)\lambda}{C\lambda}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1-(j-2)/k}\leq\frac{3}{C}\cdot\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1-(j-2)/k}\,.

(6.23)

This is still not enough to apply
Lemma

6.5
to cluster
A
: the bound in (
6.19
) bounds the volumes of nested cuts
R_{h}
in
A^{<h}
by
\frac{2}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
, but applying
Lemma

6.5
demands an upper bound of
\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(A)
on the volumes of cuts
R_{h}
in
A^{<h}
. Hence, we first show the volume of
U
to be not much larger than that of
A
.

Claim 6.10
.

We have
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\leq 2\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(A)
.

Proof.

If
A=U
the statement is trivial. Otherwise, we know there must have been a sequence of nested cuts
S_{1},\dots,S_{r}\subseteq U
that
Algorithm

4
trimmed off from
U
in line (
19
) so that
A=U\setminus(\cup_{i\in[r]}S_{i})
. We then want to apply Lemma
6.5
to the sequence of cuts
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{r}
in the cluster
U
with
\delta=1
and
\theta=\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
. Let for convenience
U^{<i}=U\setminus(\cup_{z=1}^{i-1}S_{z})
for all
i\in[r+1]
.

First note that this value of
\theta
meets the requirement of
Lemma

6.5
that
\theta<\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
, since
C\geq 16
. Secondly, our set of assumptions matches that of
Lemma

6.8
, so we get that for all
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\phi
,
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)
, one has
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
, and for all
i\in[r]
one has
\Phi_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(S_{i})<\phi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(S_{i})<\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
(the latter follows because part (
2
) of
Lemma

6.8
bounds the sparsity and volume of every cut in the entire sequence of cuts trimmed by the algorithm, and
S_{1},\dots,S_{r}
is a prefix of the entire sequence). These facts match preconditions (
1
) and (
2
) of
Lemma

6.5
for
\delta
and
\theta
as set above. Part (
1
) of
Lemma

6.5
then upper-bounds the total volume of the sequence as

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{i=1}^{r}S_{i})<\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,.

(6.24)

To conclude it is sufficient to notice

\displaystyle\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(A)+\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus A)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(A)+\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{i=1}^{r}S_{i})

\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(A)
cannot have less self-loops than
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(A)

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(A)+\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{i=1}^{r}S_{i})

by (
6.24
)

\displaystyle<\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(A)+\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,,

and the claim follows since
C\geq 6
.
∎

By
Claim

6.10
and (
6.19
), we have that for every
h\in[t-1]

\operatorname{vol}_{A^{<h}}^{\circ}(R_{h})<\frac{2}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\leq\frac{4}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(A)<\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(A)\,,

(6.25)

since
C\geq 21
. Furthermore, because every
(R_{h},\nu_{h})
is a
(\phi_{j},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[A^{<h}]^{\circ}
, we have

\Phi_{A^{<h}}^{\circ}(R_{h})<\left(1+\frac{1}{\log n}\right)\alpha\phi_{j}=\left(1+\frac{1}{\log n}\right)\alpha\frac{\phi}{\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{\log n}\right)\alpha\right)^{j}}\leq\phi_{j-1}\,.

(6.26)

We are now in shape to apply Lemma
6.5
to the sequence of cuts
R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{t+1}
in the cluster
A
. Specifically, we do this with
\delta=\phi_{j-1}/\phi
and
\theta=\frac{3}{C}(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U))^{1-(j-2)/k}
. First observe that
\delta\leq 1
(by definition of
\phi_{j-1}
) and
\theta\leq\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\leq\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(A)
(again by
Claim

6.10
and
C\geq 31
), as demanded by
Lemma

6.5
. For these parameters, one can see that precondition (
2
) is given by (
6.25
) and (
6.26
). Precondition (
1
) is instead given by (
6.23
). Part (
1
) of
Lemma

6.5
then upper-bounds the total volume of the sequence as

\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(\cup_{h=1}^{t-1}R_{h})<\theta=\frac{3}{C}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1-(j-2)/k}\,.

(6.27)

We are left with lower bounding the volume of each individual cut. We do this with part (
2
) of
Lemma

6.5
with
\theta^{\low}=\frac{1}{2C\lambda}(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U))^{1-(j-1)/k}
, which meets the requirement that
\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(R_{h})\geq\theta^{\low}
thanks to the bound in (
6.19
). Part (
2
) of
Lemma

6.5
then gives that for all
h\in[t-1]

\displaystyle\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(R_{h})\geq\theta^{\low}-b\cdot\theta=\frac{1}{2C\lambda}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1-(j-1)/k}-b\cdot\frac{3}{C}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1-(j-2)/k}\,.

(6.28)

Therefore, taking the ratio of the upper bound on the total volume from (
6.27
) and the lower bound on the volume of each individual cut from (
6.28
), we conclude that
t-1
is at most

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(\cup_{h=1}^{t-1}R_{h})}{\min_{h\in[t-1]}\operatorname{vol}_{A}^{\circ}(R_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\frac{3}{C}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1-(j-2)/k}}{\frac{1}{2C\lambda}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1-(j-1)/k}-b\cdot\frac{3}{C}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1-(j-2)/k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{3}{C}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1/k}}{\frac{1}{2C\lambda}-b\cdot\frac{3}{C}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1/k}}\,.

Next, we observe that
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\leq\vol(V)\cdot b/\phi\leq\vol(V)/{\phi}\leq n^{2}/\phi
(since in the worst case we over-count every edge in
G
as a self-loop in
G[U]^{\circ}
and at most
b/\phi
times). Then, using that
k\geq\log(\frac{n^{2}}{\phi})/\log(\frac{c}{\lambda\cdot b})
, it holds that
\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1/k}\leq\frac{c}{\lambda\cdot b}
. Now we can upper-bound the above as

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{3}{C}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1/k}}{\frac{1}{2C\lambda}-b\cdot\frac{3}{C}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1/k}}\leq\frac{\frac{3}{C}\left(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\right)^{1/k}}{\frac{1}{2C\lambda}-b\cdot\frac{3}{C}\frac{c}{\lambda\cdot b}}\leq\frac{\frac{3}{C}\cdot\left(\frac{n^{2}}{\phi}\right)^{1/k}}{\frac{1}{5C\lambda}}\leq\frac{\frac{3}{C}\cdot\frac{c}{\lambda\cdot b}}{\frac{1}{5C\lambda}}=15\frac{c}{b}\,,

since
c\leq\frac{1}{30}
. Hence, the number of inner loop iterations for
2\leq j\leq k+1
is at most
\frac{15c}{b}+1\leq\frac{1}{b}
.
∎

6.1.4
Bounding the recursion depth

From the description of the algorithms one can see that we recurse on both sides of a cut (in line (
16
) of
Algorithm

3
) only when the conditions (
10
) in
Algorithm

3
and (
17
) in
Algorithm

4
suggest that the cut is balanced, so as to maintain the recursion depth small. When instead
Algorithm

3
recurses on one side only, in line (
14
), we can use the properties of nested cuts to upper bound the volume of the recursed side. To control the number of self-loops that we add after each recursion, we also want to ensure that we recurse on sparse cuts only.

Lemma 6.11

(
Algorithm

3
recurses on balanced sparse cuts)
.

Let
b,\phi,c\in(0,1)
such that
c\leq 1/30
is a constant,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
. Also let
C\geq 16
be a constant, and let
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
be an
(\alpha,\lambda)
-BSCA where
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
and
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{18b}
. If
Assumption

1
and
Assumption

2
hold, whenever Algorithm
3
with input cluster
U\subseteq V
makes a recursive call, the recursion is on a cluster
\emptyset\neq X\subsetneq U
such that

\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(X)<(3C\lambda+2\alpha)\cdot\phi\quad\text{ and }\quad\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(X)\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{3C\lambda}\right)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,.

Proof.

There are three lines where a recursive call can happen: (
10
), (
14
), (
16
). We analyse them separately.

Recursion in line (
10
).

Let
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
be the graph returned in line (
5
) of
Algorithm

3
. Since the algorithm recurses in line (
10
), the call
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[U]^{\circ},(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi)
of line (
6
) must have returned a BSCW of the form
(R,\nu)
such that

\nu\geq\frac{1}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U)\,.

(6.29)

As we have
c\leq 1/30
,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
, and
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
(because of
Assumption

1
), Lemma
5.3
gives that
(R,\nu)
is a
(\phi,(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[U]^{\circ}
. By
Definition

5.1
, this means

\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(R)<\left(1+\frac{1}{\log n}\right)\alpha\cdot\phi\leq 2\alpha\cdot\phi\,.

We now want to use Lemma
6.6
with
\psi=\phi
,
\alpha^{\prime}=(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha
,
\lambda^{\prime}=(1+c)\lambda
,
\rho=\frac{1}{C\lambda}
,
\tau~=~1
, and
X=U
. These parameters verify the conditions for applying
Lemma

6.6
to
(R,\nu)
on
G[U]^{\circ}
:
c\leq 1/30\leq 1/4
,
\psi\leq\phi
,
\alpha^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}\geq 1
,
\rho\leq 1
,
\tau\leq 1
,
H[U]\approx_{c}G[U]
(since
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
and
\delta\leq c
), and
(R,\nu)
is a
(\phi,(1+\frac{1}{\log n},c)\alpha,(1+c)\lambda)
-BSCW of
G[U]^{\circ}
. Then
Lemma

6.6
with (
6.29
) gives

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\geq\frac{\rho}{2}(\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U))^{\tau}=\frac{1}{2C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,,

which we can use to upper bound the volume of
U\setminus R
:

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus R)=\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)-\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{2C\lambda}\right)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U).

Finally, by property (
2c
) of BSCW applied to
R
,
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq(1+c)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus R)
. Because
c\leq 1/30\leq 1/2
and
C\geq 2
,

\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(R)\leq\frac{1+c}{2}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{2C\lambda}\right)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U).

Since in this case the algorithm recurses on
R
and
U\setminus R
, we have the claim.

Recursion in lines (
14
) and (
16
).

These cases can only happen if
Algorithm

3
resorts to
Algorithm

4
. Let then
R_{1},\dots,R_{q}\subseteq U
be the sequence of cuts trimmed off by
Algorithm

4
in line (
12
). We then want to apply Lemma
6.5
to the sequence of cuts
R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{q}
in the cluster
U
with
\delta=1
and
\theta=\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
. Let for convenience
U^{<i}=U\setminus(\cup_{z=1}^{i-1}R_{z})
for all
i\in[q+1]
.

First note that this value of
\theta
meets the requirement of
Lemma

6.5
that
\theta<\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
, since
C\geq 16
. Secondly, our set of assumptions matches that of
Lemma

6.8
, so we get that for all
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
with
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\phi
,
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)
, one has
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
, and for all
i\in[q]
one has
\Phi_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})<\phi
and
\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<i}}^{\circ}(R_{i})<\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
. These facts match preconditions (
1
) and (
2
) of
Lemma

6.5
for
\delta
and
\theta
as we set them above.

•

Line (
14
)
. Part (
1
) of
Lemma

6.5
then upper-bounds the total sparsity and volume of the sequence as

\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{q}R_{z})<\phi\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(\cup_{z=1}^{q}R_{z})<\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,.

(6.30)

For
Algorithm

3
to recurse in line (
14
), it must be the case that
Algorithm

4
returned a pair
(S,\top)
in line (
12
) (see condition (
13
)). Looking into
Algorithm

4
, one can then see that if it returns a pair of the form
(S,\top)
, it must be the case that
S
is the value of variable
A
at the end of the algorithm, i.e.
S=A=U\setminus(\cup_{z\in[q]}R_{z})
. In line (
14
), the recursive call is
\textsc{Decompose}(U\setminus S,\ell+1)
, so we recurse on
U\setminus S=\cup_{z\in[q]}R_{z}
. From (
6.30
) we then have the claim since
C\geq 6
.

•

Line (
16
)
. The difference from the previous case is that for
Algorithm

3
to recurse in line (
16
), it must be the case that
Algorithm

4
returned a pair
(S,\bot)
in line (
12
) (see condition (
13
)), and in particular this implies that
Algorithm

4
returned in line (
17
).

Let
p
be the outer loop iteration where
Algorithm

4
returns, and let
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
be the graph from line (
11
) in the last inner loop iteration of outer loop iteration
p
(i.e. the inner loop iteration where it returns). Since in such iteration we must have that variable
A
actually equals
U^{<q+1}
, let
\omega
be the result of
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[U^{q+1}]^{\circ},(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\phi_{p})
in that same iteration.
As we have
c\leq 1/30
,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
, and
H[U^{<q+1}]\approx_{\delta_{p}}G[U^{<q+1}]
(from
Assumption

2
),
Lemma

5.3
ensures that
\omega
is a
(\phi_{p},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[U^{<q+1}]^{\circ}
(see
Observation

6.3
). Moreover, since
Algorithm

4
returned in line (
17
), it must be the case that
\omega
is of the form
(S,\nu)
, where
S
is the cut returned from
Algorithm

4
to
Algorithm

3
in the pair
(S,\bot)
. From the description of the algorithm it must also be the case that

\nu\geq\frac{1}{C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ\,w}(U)\,.

(6.31)

As we have
c\leq 1/30\leq 1/4
,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
, and
H[U^{<q+1}]\approx_{c}G[U^{<q+1}]
(from
Assumption

2
and
\delta_{p}\leq c
), the fact that
(S,\nu)
is a
(\phi_{p},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[U^{<q+1}]^{\circ}
, and the bound (
6.31
),
Lemma

6.6
gives

\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<q+1}}^{\circ}(S)\geq\frac{1}{2C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,.

(6.32)

Since
(S,\nu)
is a
(\phi_{p},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[U^{<q+1}]^{\circ}
, by
Definition

5.1
we also know

\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<q+1}}^{\circ}(S)\leq(1+c)\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<q+1}}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)\,,

so

\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<q+1}}^{\circ}(S)\leq\frac{5}{9}\operatorname{vol}_{U^{<q+1}}^{\circ}(U)\,,

(6.33)

since
c\leq 1/30\leq 1/10
. Now, setting
R=S
and
\theta^{\low}=\frac{1}{2C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
, the bounds in (
6.32
) and (
6.33
), together with the fact that
\theta^{\low}\leq\frac{4}{9}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
(since
C\geq 2
), allow us to apply part (
3
) of
Lemma

6.5
. This gives

\displaystyle\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)

\displaystyle<\phi\frac{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)}{\theta^{\low}-b\cdot\theta}

(6.34)

\displaystyle=\phi\cdot\frac{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)}{\frac{1}{2C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)-b\cdot\frac{3}{C}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)}

(6.35)

since
b\leq\frac{1}{18\lambda}

\displaystyle\leq 3C\lambda\cdot\phi\,,

(6.36)

and

\frac{1}{3C\lambda}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\leq\theta^{\low}-b\cdot\theta\leq\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S)\leq\frac{5}{9}\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,.

(6.37)

We can now conclude: since the result of
Algorithm

4
is
(S,\bot)
,
Algorithm

3
recurses on both
S
and
U\setminus S
in line (
16
). Let
X\in\{S,U\setminus S\}
. Then from (
6.36
) we have that
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(X)<3C\lambda\cdot\phi
, and from (
6.37
) we have that
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(X)\leq(1-\frac{1}{3C\lambda})\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)
since
C\geq 2
.

∎

We now upper-bound the recursion depth of the algorithm, using the above lemma.

Lemma 6.12

(
Algorithm

3
has small recursion depth)
.

Let
b,\phi,c\in(0,1)
such that
c\leq 1/30
is a constant,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
. Also let
C\geq 16
be a constant, and let
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
be an
(\alpha,\lambda)
-BSCA where
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{144C\lambda b}
,
\lambda\leq\min\{\frac{1}{18b},\,\frac{1}{15C\sqrt{b}}\}
. If
Assumption

1
and
Assumption

2
hold, then the recursion depth of
\textsc{Decompose}(V,0)
(i.e.
Algorithm

3
) is at most
9C\lambda\cdot\log n
.

Proof.

The crux is that recursion only happens on one of the sides of a not too large cut. As our set of assumptions matches that of
Lemma

6.11
(note that the preconditions are the same except
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{144C\lambda b}\leq\frac{1}{2b}
and
\lambda\leq\min\{\frac{1}{18b},\,\frac{1}{15C\sqrt{b}}\}\leq\frac{1}{18b}
), we get the following: for any recursion from
U
to
X

\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(X)<(2\alpha+3C\lambda)\phi\quad\text{ and }\quad\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(X)\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{3C\lambda}\right)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)

(6.38)

To get a bound on the recursion depth, we translate these bounds to a bound on
\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ}(X)
.

We use
Lemma

6.4
with
\delta=3C\lambda+2\alpha
,
S=X
, and
T=X
. These setting verifies the requirements of
Lemma

6.4
:
\delta\leq 1/b
(since
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{4b}
, and
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{6Cb}
),
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(S)<\delta\phi
from (
6.38
), and
T\subseteq S
. Hence, inequality (2) from
Lemma

6.4
gives

\displaystyle\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ}(X)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{vol}_{S}^{\circ}(T)

inequality (2) of
Lemma

6.4

\displaystyle<\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(T)+\delta b\cdot\min\{\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(S),\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U\setminus S)\}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta b)\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(X)

from (
6.38
)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\delta b\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{3C\lambda}\right)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)

since
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{144C\lambda b}
and
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{15C\sqrt{b}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{36C\lambda}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{3C\lambda}\right)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{4C\lambda}\right)\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(U)\,.

From this we conclude that if we do a recursion on
X
at level
\ell
, then

\operatorname{vol}_{X}^{\circ}(X)\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{4C\lambda}\right)^{\ell}\operatorname{vol}_{V}^{\circ}(V)=\left(1-\frac{1}{4C\lambda}\right)^{\ell}\vol(V)<\left(1-\frac{1}{4C\lambda}\right)^{\ell}n^{2}\,.

The bound follows.
∎

6.1.5
Correctness

Finally, one can see from the algorithms that they recurse only on the sides of cuts resulting from
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
. We know such cuts to be sparse, so intuitively there should be few inter-cluster edges in the resulting partition of
V
. We also know that when the algorithm returns a cluster
\{U\}
, it is because the corresponding BSCW declares it to be an expander. One can then prove that the result of this process is a BLD.

Lemma 6.13

(
Algorithm

3
outputs a BLD)
.

Let
b,\phi,c\in(0,1)
such that
c\leq 1/30
is a constant,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
. Also let
C\geq 31
be a constant, let
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
be an
(\alpha,\lambda)
-BSCA where
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{144C\lambda b}
,
\lambda\leq\min\{\frac{1}{18b},\,\frac{1}{15C\sqrt{b}}\}
, and let
k
be an integer such that
k\leq\log n
. If
Assumption

1
and
Assumption

2
hold, then the result of
\textsc{Decompose}(V,0)
(i.e.
Algorithm

3
)
is a
(b,\epsilon,\phi,\gamma)
-BLD of
G
where

\epsilon=4(3C\lambda+2\alpha)\cdot\phi\cdot 9C\lambda\cdot\log n\cdot\mathrm{e}^{2b(3C\lambda+2\alpha)9C\lambda\cdot\log n}\quad\text{ and }\quad\gamma=6\alpha^{k+1}\,.

Proof.

First note that our assumptions subsume the preconditions of
Lemma

6.7
. Hence, if Algorithm
4
breaks in line (
21
) it is in some outer loop iteration
j
with
j\leq k
, so the behaviour of the algorithm is well defined (see the outer loop for
j=1,\dots,k+1
in line (
7
) of
Algorithm

4
).

From the description we then see that
\textsc{Decompose}(V,0)
always outputs a valid partition
\mathcal{U}
of
V
. We show that
\mathcal{U}
verifies the two properties of Definition
3.1
.

Property (
2
).

Let
X\in\mathcal{U}
, which can either be the result of the return statement in line (
8
) or in line (
14
) of Algorithm
3
.

•

Return statement in line (
8
)
. Let
U\subseteq V
be the input cluster to the instance of
Algorithm

4
that returned
X
. Also let
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
be the graph from line (
5
) and
\omega
be the result of
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[U]^{\circ},(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi)
in line (
6
) of that same instance. From the description of the algorithm, we know that the return statement in line (
8
) only occurs if
\omega=\bot
, and in particular
X
is the input cluster
U
.

As our parameter regime implies
c\leq 1/30
,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
, and also we have
H[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
from
Assumption

1
, we can use Lemma
5.3
. This ensures that
\omega
is a
(\phi,(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[U]^{\circ}
. From
Definition

5.1
,
\omega=\bot
implies that
G[U]^{\circ}
is a
\phi
-expander.

•

Return statement in line (
14
)
. In this case,
Algorithm

3
must have called
Algorithm

4
, which in turn returned a pair
(S,\top)
. Then,
Algorithm

4
must have returned in line (
14
). Denoting by
A
the value of the corresponding variable in
Algorithm

4
when it returns, we must have
A=X
. Also let
p\in[k+1]
be the outer loop iteration where
Algorithm

4
returns, let
H=(V,E^{\prime},w)
be the graph from line (
11
), and let
\omega
be the result of
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(H[A]^{\circ},(1+\frac{1}{2\log n})\cdot\phi_{p})
in line (
6
) of the inner loop iteration where the algorithm returns.

As our parameter regime implies
c\leq 1/30
,
\phi<b
,
b\leq c
,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2b}
, and also we have
H[A]\approx_{\delta_{p}}G[A]
from
Assumption

2
, we can use Lemma
5.3
. This ensures that
\omega
is a
(\phi_{p},(1+\frac{1}{\log n})\alpha,(1+c)\lambda,c)
-BSCW of
G[U]^{\circ}
(see
Observation

6.3
). From
Definition

5.1
,
\omega=\bot
implies that
G[A]^{\circ}=G[X]^{\circ}
is a
\phi_{p}
-expander. Since
p\leq k+1
,
k\leq\log n
, and
\phi_{p}
is defined as

\phi_{p}=\frac{\phi}{\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{\log n}\right)\alpha\right)^{p}}\,,

we conclude that
G[X]^{\circ}
is in fact a
\psi
-expander with

\psi=\frac{\phi}{\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{\log n}\right)\alpha\right)^{p}}\geq\frac{\phi}{\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{\log n}\right)\alpha\right)^{k+1}}\geq\frac{\phi}{6\alpha^{k+1}}\,.

From the above case analysis we conclude that for every
X\in\mathcal{U}
,
G[U]^{\circ}
is a
\phi/\gamma
-expander with
\gamma=6\alpha^{k+1}
.

Property (
1
).

We will bound the number of inter-cluster edges by counting the number of edges cut at every recursion level, and then sum over levels. For convenience, let then
D=9C\lambda\cdot\log n
. We can then employ Lemma
6.12
(since our set of assumptions matches that of the lemma), which bounds the recursion depth by
D
.

Next, for
\ell=0,1,\dots,D
, let
\mathcal{I}^{\ell}
be the set of instances of Algorithm
3
at the
\ell
-th level of the recursion triggered from
\textsc{Decompose}(V,0)
. Each instance
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{I}^{\ell}
is a pair
\mathcal{I}=(U,X)
, where
U\subseteq V
is the input cluster to the instance, and:

1.

if the instance returns
\{U\}
in line (
8
), then
X=\emptyset
;

2.

if the instance makes a recursive call, then
\emptyset\neq X\subsetneq U
is a set on which the instance invokes Algorithm
3
.

Then, since our set of assumptions matches that of
Lemma

6.11
, we get that
\Phi_{U}^{\circ}(X)<(2\alpha+3C\lambda)\phi
for every
\ell=0,\dots,D
and every
(U,X)\in\mathcal{I}^{\ell}
such that
X\neq\emptyset
. Hence,

\text{for all $\ell=0,\dots,D$, and all $(U,X)\in\mathcal{I}^{\ell}$, we have }\quad{\partial}_{U}X<(2\alpha+3C\lambda)\phi\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(X)\,.

(6.39)

With this notation, define the set of edges cut at level
\ell
to be

C_{\ell}=\bigcup_{(U,X)\in\mathcal{I}^{\ell}}E(X,U\setminus X)\,.

Now let
\mu=(3C\lambda+2\alpha)
, and for
\ell=0,\dots,D
also define

T(\ell)=\mu\phi\vol(V)+2\mu\cdot b\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell-1}T(\ell^{\prime})\,.

To bound the number of inter cluster edges, we first show that
|C_{\ell}|\leq T(\ell)
, then we resolve the recurrence
T(\ell)
, and finally we sum over
\ell=0,\dots,D
to conclude.

Claim 6.14
.

For every
\ell=0,1,\dots,D
one has
|C_{\ell}|\leq T(\ell)
.

Proof.

We prove the statement by induction. At level
0
there can be only one instance
(V,X)\in\mathcal{I}^{0}
, so the number of edges cut is
|C_{0}|={\partial}_{V}X
. By (
6.39
)

|C_{0}|<\mu\phi\operatorname{vol}_{V}^{\circ}(X)=\mu\phi\vol(X)\leq\mu\phi\vol(V)=T(0)\,,

thus proving the base case.

Next take any
1\leq\ell\leq D
, and assume that
|C_{\ell^{\prime}}|\leq T(\ell^{\prime})
for all
\ell^{\prime}=0,\dots,\ell-1
. By (
6.39
) we know
{\partial}_{U}X<\mu\phi\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(X)
for all
(U,X)\in\mathcal{I}^{\ell}
. Using the definition of
\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(X)
, we get

\displaystyle{\partial}_{U}X<\mu\phi\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{U}^{\circ}(X)

\displaystyle=\mu\phi\cdot\left(\vol(X)+\frac{b-\phi}{\phi}\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(X)\right)

(6.40)

since
\operatorname{bnd}_{U}(X)\leq{\partial}U

\displaystyle\leq\mu\phi\cdot\vol(X)+\mu b\cdot{\partial}U\,.

(6.41)

Observe that the union of all
E(U,V\setminus U)
on level
\ell
is a subset of all edges cut on previous recursion levels. This means that

\sum_{(U,X)\in\mathcal{I}^{\ell}}{\partial}U\leq 2\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell-1}|C_{\ell^{\prime}}|\,,

(6.42)

since every
e\in C_{\ell^{\prime}}
is counted at most twice by the sum over
\mathcal{I}^{\ell}
.

Using the induction hypothesis and observing that the cuts
\{X:(U,X)\in\mathcal{I}^{\ell}\}
must be disjoint, we can upper-bound
|C_{\ell}|
as

\displaystyle|C_{\ell}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{(U,X)\in\mathcal{I}^{\ell}}{\partial}_{U}X

by (
6.41
) and disjointness of the
X
’s

\displaystyle<\mu\phi\vol(V)+\mu b\sum_{(U,X)\in\mathcal{I}^{\ell}}{\partial}U

by (
6.42
)

\displaystyle\leq\mu\phi\vol(V)+2\mu b\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell-1}|C_{\ell^{\prime}}|

by the induction hypothesis

\displaystyle\leq\mu\phi\vol(V)+2\mu b\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell-1}T(\ell^{\prime})

\displaystyle=T(\ell)\,.

thus showing the inductive statement.
∎

Now we solve the recurrence.

Claim 6.15
.

T(\ell)\leq\mu\phi\vol(V)(1+2\mu b)^{\ell}
for all
\ell\geq 0
.

Proof.

Let
\ell\geq 1
and assume the statement holds true for all
0\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell-1
. Then,

\displaystyle T(\ell)

\displaystyle=\mu\phi\vol(V)+2\mu b\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell-1}T(\ell^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\mu\phi\vol(V)+2\mu b\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell-1}\mu\phi\vol(V)(1+2\mu b)^{\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\mu\phi\vol(V)\left(1+2\mu b\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell-1}(1+2\mu b)^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu\phi\vol(V)\left(1+2\mu b\frac{(1+2\mu b)^{\ell}-1}{1+2\mu b-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu\phi\vol(V)(1+2\mu b)^{\ell}\,.

∎

Now combing
Claim

6.14
and
Claim

6.15
, one has that the number of inter-cluster edges in
\mathcal{U}
is

\displaystyle\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}{\partial}U\leq 2\sum_{\ell=0}^{D}|C_{\ell}|\leq 2\sum_{\ell=0}^{D}T(\ell)\leq 2\mu\phi\vol(V)\cdot(D+1)\cdot(1+2\mu b)^{D}\leq 4\mu\cdot\phi\cdot\vol(V)\cdot D\cdot e^{2b\mu D}\,.

∎

6.2
Dynamic stream implementation

In the previous sections we analysed
Algorithm

3
and
Algorithm

4
in an ideal, offline setting with “free” access to sparsifiers. To get
Lemma

6.1
, we only need to lift this assumption, and combine lemmas from the previous section.

See
6.1

Proof.

Let
C=40,c=1/40
, and define the quantities
D=9C\lambda\cdot\log n
,
\delta=c^{2}b/\log n
, and
\delta_{j}=\delta\cdot(\alpha(1+1/\log n))^{-j}
for
j\in[k+1]
. We process the stream as follows.

1.

We maintain
D+1
independent samples from the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\delta}
using the algorithm of
Lemma

4.2
, resulting in a collection of sparsifiers
H_{\ell}
for
0\leq\ell\leq D
.

2.

For every
j\in[k+1]
, we maintain
(D+1)\cdot 1/b
samples from the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\delta_{j}}
using the algorithm of
Lemma

4.2
, resulting in a collection of sparsifiers
H_{\ell,j,h}
for
0\leq\ell\leq D
,
j~\in~[k+1]
,
h\in[1/b]
.

After having processed the stream as described above, we want to use
Algorithm

3
and
Algorithm

4
to decode these samples into a BLD. In
Algorithm

3
and
Algorithm

4
we have hardwired parameters
k,\alpha,\lambda,C,c
: the parameters
k,\alpha,\lambda
are as in the lemma statement, and
C,c
are set as defined above (i.e.
C=40,c=1/40
).
Algorithm

3
and
Algorithm

4
also use BLD parameters
b,\phi
:
b
is just the value in the lemma statement, and we set

\phi=\frac{\epsilon}{4(3C\lambda+2\alpha)\cdot D\cdot\mathrm{e}^{2b(3C\lambda+2\alpha)D}}\,.

The only thing left to specify about the algorithms is the sparsifier-access. Since we process the stream by essentially computing a number of sparsifiers, there is a natural way to do this: for
0\leq\ell\leq D
, we define the procedure
\textsc{Sparsifier}^{c}_{\ell}(U,\cdot)
from line (
5
) in
Algorithm

3
to return
H_{\ell}
for every
U\subseteq V
; similarly, for
0\leq\ell\leq D
,
j~\in~[k+1]
,
h\in[1/b]
, we define the procedure
\textsc{Sparsifier}^{c}_{\ell,j,h}(A,\cdot)
from line (
11
) in
Algorithm

4
to return
H_{\ell,j,h}
for every
A\subseteq V
.

Correctness.

Since
C,\lambda=O(1)
,
\alpha b\leq 1/\log n
,
D=O(\log n)
, one can see that
\phi=\Omega(\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha\log n})
. From this value of
\phi
we also get
\phi<b
, since
C,\lambda,\alpha\geq 1
,
\epsilon\leq b\log n
, the exponential is at least
1
, and
D>\log n
. Then, our parameter regime has all the conditions for using
Lemma

6.12
,
Lemma

6.9
,
Lemma

6.13
: we have an
(\alpha,\lambda)
-BSCA
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
,
\phi<b
,
b\leq 1/\log n\leq c\leq 1/30
,
C\geq 31
,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{b\log n}\leq\frac{1}{144C\lambda b}\leq\frac{1}{2b}
,
\lambda=O(1)
so
\lambda\leq\min\{\frac{1}{18b},\,\frac{1}{15C\sqrt{b}},\frac{c}{2b}\}
,
k\geq\frac{\log{n^{5}\alpha}}{\log{b^{-1/2}}/{\lambda}}
implies
k\geq\log\frac{n^{2}}{\phi}/\log\frac{c}{\lambda\cdot b}
(since
\phi=\Omega(\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha\log n})
,
\epsilon\geq 1/n^{2}
, and
c
is a constant), and
k\leq\log n
. We now just want to lift
Assumption

1
and
Assumption

2
, so as to apply these lemmas. From our definition of
\textsc{Sparsifier}_{\ell}(U,\cdot)
and
\textsc{Sparsifier}_{\ell,j,h}(A,\cdot)
above, we have the following claim.

Claim 6.16
.

Assumptions
1
and
2
hold with high probability as long as
\ell\leq D
and
h\leq 1/b
.

Proof.

The claim follows from the way we process the stream, described above, and from our definition of
\textsc{Sparsifier}^{c}_{\ell}(U,\cdot)
and
\textsc{Sparsifier}^{c}_{\ell,j,h}(A,\cdot)
.

•

For every
0\leq\ell\leq D
and any
U\subseteq V
,
\textsc{Sparsifier}^{c}_{\ell}(U,\cdot)
outputs the graph
H_{\ell}
, independently sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{\delta}
.
Algorithm

3
only calls
\textsc{Sparsifier}^{c}_{\ell}(U,b)
for a cluster
U
that was found using randomness from levels before
\ell
(simply by the description of the algorithm, see recursive calls in lines (
10
), (
14
), (
16
) of
Algorithm

3
). Thus, by
Lemma

4.2
we know that
H_{\ell}[U]\approx_{\delta}G[U]
with high probability. Since there can be at most
n
calls to
\textsc{Sparsifier}^{c}_{\ell}(U,b)
for any
\ell
, we can conclude that
H_{\ell}
works for each of them with high probability.

•

For every
0\leq\ell\leq D,\,j\in[k+1],\,h\in[1/b]
and any
A\subseteq V
,
\textsc{Sparsifier}^{c}_{\ell,j,h}(A,\cdot)
outputs the graph
H_{\ell,j,h}
, independently sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{\delta_{j}}
. Similarly as before, we can see from the description of
Algorithm

4
that
\textsc{Sparsifier}^{c}_{\ell,j,h}(A,b_{j})
can only be called on a cluster
A
that depends on the randomness of a previous level (in case
A
equals the input cluster
U
to
Algorithm

4
) or a previous iteration (in case
A
was updated in line (
19
)). Thus,
A
is independent from
H_{\ell,j,h}
. Thus, by
Lemma

4.2
we know that
H_{\ell,j,h}[A]\approx_{\delta_{j}}G[A]
with high probability. Also in this case there can be at most
n
calls to
\textsc{Sparsifier}^{c}_{\ell,j,h}(A,b_{j})
for any triple
\ell,j,h
, so
H_{\ell,j,h}
works for each of them with high probability.

∎

Provided
Assumption

1
and
Assumption

2
hold,
Lemma

6.12
and
Lemma

6.9
are guaranteeing that there is no point in the execution of the algorithm with recursion level
\ell
larger than
D
or with inner loop index
h
(see line (
10
)) larger than
1/b
. On the other hand,
Claim

6.16
ensures that
Assumption

1
and
Assumption

2
hold with high probability as long as
\ell\leq D
and
h\leq 1/b
. Hence, we have that the results from
Lemma

6.12
,
Lemma

6.9
,
Lemma

6.13
hold simultaneously with high probability. In particular, the output
\mathcal{U}
of
\textsc{Decompose}(V,0)

is a
(b,\epsilon,\phi,\gamma)
-BLD of
G
with high probability for
\gamma=O(\alpha^{k+1})
(note that we set
\phi
exactly by rearranging the value of
\epsilon
given by
Lemma

6.13
).

Space complexity.

The space requirement for processing the stream is dominated by the sparsifiers that we maintain, plus the space used by any call to
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
for the decoding phase. From
Lemma

4.2
we know that sampling from
\mathcal{D}_{\delta}
can be done by maintaining a linear sketch of
\widetilde{O}(n/\delta^{2})
bits, and sampling from
\mathcal{D}_{\delta_{j}}
can be done maintaining a linear sketch of
\widetilde{O}(n/\delta_{j}^{2})
bits. Since we maintain
D+1
samples from
\mathcal{D}_{\delta}
, and
(D+1)\cdot 1/b
samples from
\mathcal{D}_{\delta_{j}}
for every
j\in[k+1]
, the space complexity for processing the stream is

(D+1)\cdot\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{n}{\delta^{2}}\right)+(D+1)\cdot\frac{1}{b}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{n}{\delta_{j}^{2}}\right)\,.

Because we defined
D=9C\lambda\cdot\log n
,
\delta=c^{2}b/\log n
,
\delta_{j}=\delta\cdot(\alpha(1+1/\log n))^{-j}
, and we have
C,\lambda=O(1),\,k=O(\log n),c=\Omega(1)
, we use at most

O(\log n)\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{n}{b^{2}}\right)+O(\log n)O\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)O(k)\cdot\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{n}{b^{2}}\right)\cdot\alpha^{O(k)}=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{n}{b^{3}}\right)\cdot\alpha^{O(k)}

bits of space for the sparsifiers. Finally, note that
Algorithms

3
and
4
run
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
on sparsifiers with at most
n
vertices and at most

\max\left\{\widetilde{O}(n/\delta^{2}),\max_{j\in[k+1]}\left\{\widetilde{O}(n/\delta_{j}^{2})\right\}\right\}=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{n}{b^{2}}\right)\cdot\alpha^{O(k)}

edges, by
Lemma

4.2
. As we can reuse the space for each call, this takes an extra
S(n,\widetilde{O}(n/b^{2})\alpha^{O(k)})
bits of space, leading to the claimed space complexity for the decoding.

Time complexity.

Throughout the recursion of
\textsc{Decompose}(V,0)
,
Algorithms

3
and
4
make at most
\poly(n)
calls to
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
. As noted above, each of these calls is on a graph with at most
n
vertices and
\widetilde{O}(n/b^{2})\alpha^{O(k)}
edges.
Lemma

4.2
also guarantees that decoding the linear sketch to get a sample from
\mathcal{D}_{\delta}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\delta_{j}}
takes
\poly(n)
time. Hence the running time of the decoding is
\poly(n)\cdot T(n,\widetilde{O}(n/b^{2})\alpha^{O(k)})
.

Sparsity parameter in BSCA calls.

By the way we set
\phi
and by definition of the algorithm, we have that every call to
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(\cdot,\psi)
is made with a sparsity parameter
\psi\in(0,1)
that is
\psi\leq\phi\leq\frac{1}{10\alpha}
.

∎

7
Lower bound for two-level expander decomposition

The goal of this section is to show that repeatedly computing EDs on the inter-cluster edges necessarily incurs a dependence on the sparsity parameter. Formally, the result is the following.

See
3

By Yao’s minimax principle, it is sufficient to give a distribution
\mathcal{G}
over graphs with vertex set
V
such that any deterministic streaming algorithm that computes, with probability at least
0.9
, an
(\epsilon,\phi,\ell)
-RED of
G\sim\mathcal{G}
takes
\Omega(n/\epsilon)
bits of space. We are not going to use the fact that the stream can be dynamic to show the lower bound, so any stream that reveals the edges of
G\sim\mathcal{G}
in an arbitrary order serves our purposes.

We then proceed as follows. In
Section

7.1
we describe our hard distribution over graphs, and show a structural property of its two-level
(\epsilon,\phi)
-RED. In
Section

7.2
we introduce a communication problem,
\recover
, and show a reduction to RED. Finally, in
Section

7.3
we give a communication lower bound for
\recover
. We combine these results into proving
Theorem

3
in
Section

7.4
.

7.1
Hard distribution

The randomness of our hard distribution
\mathcal{G}
arises from sampling Erdős-Rényi graphs. We recall the definition for convenience of the reader.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
N
be an integer and let
p\in[0,1]
. The distribution of Erdős-Rényi graphs
\er(N,p)
is the distribution over
N
-vertex graphs
H=(U,F)
where each pair
e\in\binom{U}{2}
is in
F
with probability
p
independently of other pairs.

Let
d,m
be integers such that
3\leq d<m<n
. We now define our hard distribution
\mathcal{G}
over
n
-vertex graphs
G=(V,E)
, and we illustrate it in
Figure

4
.

Definition 7.2

(Distributions
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
)
.

We partition
V=[n]
arbitrarily into two sets
S
and
T
with
n/2
vertices each, and further partition
S
into
n/m
sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{n/m}
with
m/2
vertices each. The distribution
\mathcal{G}_{n,d,m}
is supported over graphs
G=(V,E)
where
E
is defined as follows.

1.

For each
i~\in~[n/m]
, the induced subgraph
G[S_{i}]
is sampled independently from the Erdős-Rényi distribution
\er({m}/{2},4d/m)
(see Definition
7.1
above). We denote by
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{n,d,m}
the distribution of the subgraph
G[S]
, i.e.
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{n,d,m}=\er({m}/{2},4d/m)^{\otimes n/m}
.

2.

The induced subgraph
G[T]
is a fixed
d
-regular
\psi
-expander, for
\psi=\Omega(1)
independent of
d,m,n
.

3.

We fix for convenience an arbitrary labelling
s_{i,1},\dots,s_{i,m/2}
of the vertices in each
S_{i},\,i\in[n/m]
, and we sample
K\sim\unif([m/2])
. Then, for every
i\in[n/m]
, we add
dm/2
edges from
s_{i,K}
to
T
so that each
t\in T
has
d
incident edges in
E(S,T)
(one way to do so is by partitioning
T
into
n/(dm)
subsets
T_{1},\dots,T_{n/(dm)}
with
dm/2
vertices each and connecting
s_{i,K}
to each vertex of
T_{\lceil i/d\rceil}
for every
i\in[n/m]
).

Since the parameters
n,d,m
are fixed hereafter, we may drop the subscript from
\mathcal{G}_{n,d,m}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{n,d,m}
and simply denote them by
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
respectively.

Figure 4:
Illustration of the graph we use for proving the lower bound. Thick bullets represent important vertices, thick lines represent important edges, dotted lines represent edges connecting the important vertices to
T
.

Roughly speaking, our hard instances should be composed of
n/m
regular expanders that are densely connected to
T
, which is also an expander, through a selection of “special” vertices. We will show that the hardness arises from recovering information about certain important vertices and edges, defined below and also illustrated in
Figure

4
.

Definition 7.3

(Important vertices and edges)
.

Let
G=(V,E)\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{G})
. Denote by
k\in[m/2]
the unique index such that
\{s_{i,k}:i\in[n/m]\}=\{s\in S:\{t\in T:\{s,t\}\in E\}\neq\emptyset\}
. Then, we define the set of important vertices
V^{*}=\{s_{i,k}:i\in[n/m]\}
to be the set of vertices of
S
that are adjacent to
T
, and define the set of important edges
E^{*}=\{\{u,v^{*}\}:u\in S\,,v^{*}\in V^{*}\}
to be the set of edges in the induced subgraph
G[S]
that are incident on
V^{*}
.

Remark 1
.

Note that any sample
G\sim\mathcal{G}
can be defined by
(G^{\prime},K)
, where
G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})
is the graph consisting of the disjoint union of the subgraphs
(G[S_{i}])_{i\in[n/m]}
, and
K\in[m/2]
is the random index determining the important vertices and edges. This is because the edges within
G[T]
are the same for all
G\sim\mathcal{G}
, and the edges
E(S,T)
are determined by
K
. By virtue of this observation we abuse notation and write
G=(G^{\prime},K)\sim\mathcal{G}
. Moreover, for any
(G^{\prime},K)\sim\mathcal{G}
one has
G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and conversely, for any
G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
K\sim\unif([m/2])
one also has
(G^{\prime},K)\sim\mathcal{G}
.

In the remainder of the section, we call a graph
(1\pm\delta)\Delta
-regular for an integer
\Delta>0
and
\delta\in[0,1]
, if each of its vertices has degree in the range
[(1-\delta)\Delta,(1+\delta)\Delta]
. With this terminology, we show that in any valid
(\epsilon,\phi)
-ED of
G
, most of the important edges are crossing edges, as depicted in
Figure

5
. This works under the assumption that each
G[S_{i}]
is a near-regular expander.

Figure 5:
Illustration of the ideal expander decomposition of the graph. Thick bullets represent important vertices, smaller bullets represent ordinary vertices, thick lines represent important edges, dotted lines represent the rest of the edges. Grey areas represent the clusters in the decomposition.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
G=(V,E)\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{G})
, and let
\epsilon,\phi\in(0,1)
such that
\epsilon\leq 10^{-5}\cdot\psi\cdot\frac{d}{m}
and
\phi\geq 11/m
. If
n\geq 100m
,
m\geq 500
, and every subgraph
(G[S_{i}])_{i\in[n/m]}
is a
(1\pm 1/10)2d
-regular
\frac{1}{3}
-expander, then any
(\epsilon,\phi)
-ED
\mathcal{U}
of
G
satisfies

\left|E^{*}\setminus\mathcal{U}\right|\geq\frac{4}{5}\cdot\left|E^{*}\right|\,.

Proof.

Let
G=(V,E)\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{G})
and let
k\in[m/2]
such that the important vertices are
s_{i,k}
for
i\in[n/m]
(note that such value of
k
can be uniquely inferred from seeing
G
since the ordering of the vertices is fixed). Note that by
Definition

7.2
together with our assumption, the graph
G
has at most
11/10\cdot dn/2+3/4\cdot dn\leq 1.3dn
edges.

Hereafter, let
\mathcal{U}
be any
(\epsilon,\phi)
-ED of
G
. We begin by arguing that there is a giant cluster in
\mathcal{U}
that covers most of
T
and most of the important vertices.

Claim 7.5
.

There is a component
U^{*}\in\mathcal{U}
such that

|T\cap U^{*}|\geq\left(1-12\frac{\epsilon}{\psi}\right)|T|\quad\text{and}\quad|V^{*}\cap U^{*}|\geq\left(1-12\frac{\epsilon}{\psi}\right)|V^{*}|\,.

Proof.

For any valid
(\epsilon,\phi)
-ED
\mathcal{U}
, we must have
|E\setminus\mathcal{U}|\leq\epsilon\cdot 1.3dn
. Since
G[T]
is a
d
-regular
\psi
-expander, there is a component
U^{*}\in\mathcal{U}
that contains at least
(1-3\epsilon/\psi)\cdot n/2
vertices from
T
, i.e.

|T\cap U^{*}|\geq\left(1-3\frac{\epsilon}{\psi}\right)\cdot\frac{n}{2}=\left(1-3\frac{\epsilon}{\psi}\right)|T|\,.

To see why, note that if this was not the case then we could construct a cut
S=\cup_{U\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}U\cap T
for some
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
such that
3\epsilon/\psi\cdot|T|<|S|\leq|T|/2
and conclude that there would be at least
\psi\vol_{G[T]}(S)>\epsilon\cdot 1.5dn
edges crossing
\mathcal{U}
.

Since each vertex in
T
has an edge to
d
important vertices (see
Definition

7.2
), there are at least
(1-6\epsilon/\psi)\cdot n/m
important vertices with at least
dm/4
neighbours in
U^{*}\cap T
, i.e.

\Big|\Big\{s\in V^{*}:\,\left|\{t\in U^{*}\cap T:\{s,t\}\in E\}\right|\geq\frac{1}{4}dm\Big\}\Big|\geq\left(1-6\frac{\epsilon}{\psi}\right)\cdot\frac{n}{m}=\left(1-6\frac{\epsilon}{\psi}\right)\cdot|V^{*}|\,.

To see why, note that if this was not the case, we would have
|\{s,t\}\in E:\,s\in V^{*}\,,t\in T\setminus U^{*}\}|
to be larger than
(6\epsilon/\psi\cdot n/m)\cdot dm/4
, which is impossible since every
t\in T
has
d
incident edges in
E(S,T)
and
|T\setminus U^{*}|\leq 3\epsilon/\psi\cdot n/2
.

Now, because most of the important vertices have many edges to
T\cap U^{*}
, most of these should also be in
U^{*}
, otherwise too many edges would cross: since
|E\setminus\mathcal{U}|\leq\epsilon\cdot 1.3dn
, there must be at most
6\epsilon/\psi\cdot n/m+\frac{\epsilon\cdot 1.3dn}{dm/4}\leq 12\epsilon/\psi\cdot n/m
important vertices that are not in
U^{*}
, i.e.

|V^{*}\cap U^{*}|\geq\left(1-12\frac{\epsilon}{\psi}\right)\cdot\frac{n}{m}=\left(1-12\frac{\epsilon}{\psi}\right)\cdot|V^{*}|\,.

∎

We want to confine our attention to important vertices whose corresponding subgraph
G[S_{i}]
locally preserves property (
1
) of
Definition

1.1
, i.e. has few inter-cluster edges as compared to its own volume. Formally, we consider the set of good vertices, defined as

V^{*}_{\text{g}}=\left\{s_{i,k}\in V^{*}:s_{i,k}\in U^{*}\,,\left|(E\setminus\mathcal{U})\cap\binom{S_{i}}{2}\right|\leq{200\epsilon}dm\right\}\,.

(7.1)

We next show that the subgraph
G[S_{i}]
corresponding to a good vertex is covered almost entirely by a single cluster, except the good vertex itself which is assigned to
U^{*}
.

Claim 7.6
.

Let
s_{i,k}\in V^{*}_{{\good}}
. Then, there is a cluster
U\in\mathcal{U}
such that

s_{i,k}\notin U\quad\text{and}\quad|U\cap S_{i}|\geq\left(1-700{\epsilon}\right)|S_{i}|\,.

Proof.

Since
G[S_{i}]
is a
(1\pm 1/10)2d
-regular
\frac{1}{3}
-expander by assumption, there is at least one component
U\in\mathcal{U}
containing at least
(1-700\epsilon)m/2
vertices in
S_{i}
, i.e.

|U\cap S_{i}|\geq\left(1-700{\epsilon}\right)\frac{m}{2}=\left(1-700{\epsilon}\right)|S_{i}|\,,

(7.2)

To see why, note that if this was not the case we could construct a cut
R=\cup_{W\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}W\cap S_{i}
for some
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
such that
10/9\cdot 700\epsilon\cdot|S_{i}|<|R|\leq 9/11\cdot|S_{i}|/2
and conclude that there would be at least
1/3\cdot\vol_{G[S_{i}]}(R)>230\epsilon dm
edges crossing
\mathcal{U}
inside
S_{i}
, contradicting the fact that
s_{i,k}\in V^{*}_{{\good}}
(see the definition in (
7.1
)).

Hereafter, let
U\in\mathcal{U}
be the cluster that satisfies (
7.2
) (which is unique, since
\epsilon\leq 10^{-5}
). We know from
Claim

7.5
that there is a cluster
U^{*}\in\mathcal{U}
that contains all good vertices (by definition of good vertex, see (
7.1
)) and that covers most of
T
. To show that
s_{i,k}\notin U
, we leverage the fact that
s_{i,k}\in U^{*}
and that
G[U^{*}]
must be a
\phi
-expander by property (
2
) of
Definition

1.1
. Suppose towards a contradiction that
U^{*}=U
, and let
Q=(U^{*}\cap S_{i})\setminus\{s_{i,k}\}
. Because
G[S_{i}]
is
(1\pm 1/10)2d
-regular, the volume of this set in
G[U^{*}]
is at least

\displaystyle\vol_{G[U^{*}]}(Q)

\displaystyle\geq-\deg_{G[S_{i}]}(s_{i,k})+\sum_{u\in U^{*}\cap S_{i}}\deg_{G[U^{*}\cap S_{i}]}(u)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{11}{10}2d+\sum_{u\in U^{*}\cap S_{i}:\,\deg_{G[U^{*}\cap S_{i}]}(u)\geq(1-600{\epsilon})\deg_{G[S_{i}]}(u)}\deg_{G[U^{*}\cap S_{i}]}(u)\,.

We next observe that the sum above has at least
|U^{*}\cap S_{i}|/2
many terms, for if this was not the case we would have at least
300\epsilon|U^{*}\cap S_{i}|\cdot 9/10\cdot 2d\geq 200\epsilon dm
edges from
U^{*}\cap S_{i}
to
S_{i}\setminus U^{*}
, which would contradict
s_{i,k}\in V^{*}_{{\good}}
(see the definition in (
7.1
)). We then continue from above and hence get

\displaystyle\vol_{G[U^{*}]}(Q)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{11}{10}2d+\frac{1}{2}|U^{*}\cap S_{i}|\cdot\left(1-600{\epsilon}\right)\deg_{G[S_{i}]}(u)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{11}{10}2d+\frac{1}{2}|U^{*}\cap S_{i}|\cdot\left(1-600{\epsilon}\right)\frac{9}{10}2d

since
|S_{i}\cap U^{*}|\geq(1-700\epsilon)|S_{i}|

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{11}{10}2d+\frac{9}{10}2d\frac{1}{2}\left(1-1400{\epsilon}\right)\frac{m}{2}\,.

Moreover, we know that
|U^{*}\cap T|\geq(1-3\epsilon/\psi)n/2\geq(1-1/8000)n/2
by our assumption on
\epsilon
, so the fact that
G[T]
is
d
-regular implies
\vol_{G[U^{*}]}(U^{*})\geq\vol_{G[U^{*}\cap T]}(U^{*}\cap T)\geq dn/10
. Also, by near-regularity of
G
and because
m\leq n/100
, we have
\vol_{G[U^{*}]}(Q)\leq 3d\cdot m/2<dn/20\leq\frac{1}{2}\vol_{G[U^{*}]}(U^{*})
. This means that
Q
is the smaller side of the cut. On the other hand, the number of edges crossing the cut
Q
in
G[U^{*}]
is too small, since vertices in
Q
can only connect to the rest of
U^{*}
through edges incident on
s_{i,k}
, i.e.

{\partial}_{U^{*}}Q=\deg_{G[S_{i}]}(s_{i,k})\leq\frac{11}{10}2d\,.

Therefore,
U_{i}=U^{*}
implies

\Phi_{G[U^{*}]}\leq\Phi_{G[U^{*}]}(Q)=\frac{{\partial}_{U^{*}}Q}{\vol_{G[U^{*}]}(Q)}\leq\frac{\frac{11}{10}2d}{\frac{9}{10}d\left(1-1400{\epsilon}\right)\frac{m}{2}-\frac{11}{10}2d}\leq\frac{10}{m}<\phi\,,

a contradiction. Here we used that
m\geq 500
,
\phi\geq 11/m
, and
\epsilon\leq 10^{-5}
.
∎

By virtue of
Claim

7.6
, we have the following setting: for every good vertex
s_{i,k}\in V^{*}_{\text{g}}
, there is one component
U
covering a
(1-700\epsilon)
fraction of
S_{i}
except
s_{i,k}
itself. Therefore, many important edges incident on each good vertex are crossing edges, i.e.

\{\{s,s^{\prime}\}\in E^{*}:\,s=s_{i,k},s^{\prime}\notin U^{*}\}\subseteq E(U^{*}\cap S_{i},S_{i}\setminus U^{*})\subseteq E\setminus\mathcal{U}\quad\text{for all $s_{i,k}\in V^{*}_{\text{g}}$,}

and

|U^{*}\cap S_{i}|\leq|U\setminus S_{i}|\leq 700{\epsilon}|S_{i}|=700{\epsilon}\frac{m}{2}\quad\text{for all $s_{i,k}\in V^{*}_{\text{g}}$.}

In particular, the above conditions implies that, among the important edges incident on each good vertex, at most
700\epsilon\cdot m/2
of them can land in the same cluster
U^{*}
as the good vertex itself, i.e.

|\{\{s,s^{\prime}\}\in E^{*}:\,s=s_{i,k},s^{\prime}\notin U^{*}\}|\geq\frac{9}{10}2d-700{\epsilon}\frac{m}{2}\quad\text{for all $s_{i,k}\in V^{*}_{\text{g}}$.}

(7.3)

If there are many good vertices, we can conclude that a lot of the important edges are crossing.

Claim 7.7
.

One has
|V^{*}_{\good}|\geq\frac{49}{50}|V^{*}|
.

Proof.

Since the number of crossing edges is
|E\setminus\mathcal{U}|\leq\epsilon\cdot 1.3dn
, one has

\left|\left\{s_{i,k}\in V^{*}:\,\left|(E\setminus\mathcal{U})\cap\binom{S_{i}}{2}\right|\leq{200\epsilon}dm\right\}\right|\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{100}\right)\frac{n}{m}=\left(1-\frac{1}{100}\right)|V^{*}|\,.

(7.4)

Combining
Claim

7.5
and the above bound, we know
V^{*}_{\good}
to be large:

|V^{*}_{\text{g}}|\geq\left(1-12\frac{\epsilon}{\psi}-\frac{1}{100}\right)\cdot|V^{*}|\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{50}\right)\cdot|V^{*}|\,,

where we use the assumption that
\epsilon\leq 10^{-5}\cdot\psi\cdot\frac{d}{m}
.
∎

By
Claim

7.7
and the bound in (
7.3
), we can conclude the proof:

\displaystyle|E^{*}\setminus\mathcal{U}|\geq\sum_{s_{i,k}\in V^{*}_{\text{g}}}|\{\{s,t\}\in E^{*}:\,s=s_{i,k}\}|\geq\left(\frac{9}{10}-700{\epsilon}\frac{m}{2}\frac{1}{2d}\right)2d\cdot|V^{*}_{\text{g}}|\,,

and by
Claim

7.7
we get

\displaystyle|E^{*}\setminus\mathcal{U}|\geq\left(\frac{9}{10}-700{\epsilon}\frac{m}{2}\frac{1}{2d}\right)2d\cdot\frac{49}{50}\cdot\frac{n}{m}\geq\frac{4}{5}\cdot|E^{*}|\,,

where we used
\epsilon\leq 10^{-5}\psi\frac{d}{m}
in the last inequality.
∎

7.2
Reduction to a communication problem

We introduce the two-party one-way communication problem
\recover
, defined as follows.

Definition 7.8

(Communication problem
\recover
)
.

Let
\xi>0
be a real parameter. In the communication problem
\recover_{\xi}
,
Alice’s input is a graph
G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})
, and Bob’s output is a set of pairs of vertices
F~\subseteq~\binom{S}{2}
such that

1.

|F|\leq 6\xi\cdot|E^{\prime}|
, and

2.

at least a
1/10
fraction of the pairs in
F
are edges of
G^{\prime}
, i.e.
|F\cap E^{\prime}|\geq 1/10\cdot|E^{\prime}|
.

We give a reduction from
\recover_{\epsilon m}
to
(\epsilon,\phi,2)
-RED, in the setting where Alice’s input
G^{\prime}
is an
n/2
-vertex graph drawn from
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. We recall that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{n,d,m}=\er({m}/{2},4d/m)^{\otimes n/m}
is the distribution over
n/2
-vertex graphs
G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})
consisting of the disjoint union of
n/m
disjoint Erdős-Rényi graphs on
m/2
vertices (see
Definition

7.2
). We then begin by verifying that, with high probability, an input
G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a collection of
n/m
nearly regular expanders.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. If
m\geq 500
and
d\geq 600\log n
then, with probability at least
1-1/n
, for every
i\in[n/m]
one has that the subgraph
G^{\prime}[S_{i}]
is a
(1\pm 1/10)2d
-regular
1/3
-expander

Proof.

The statement follows from the following fact about Erdős-Rényi graphs, whose proof is deferred to
Section

A.2
.

Fact 7.10
.

Let
N\geq 10
be an integer and
p\in[0,1]
. Then, letting
\bar{d}=p(N-1)
one has

\Pr_{H=(U,F)\sim\er(N,p)}\left[\left(\Phi_{H}<\frac{1}{3}\right)\text{ or }\left(\exists u\in U\text{ s.t. }\left|\deg_{H}(u)-\bar{d}\right|>\frac{1}{11}\bar{d}\right)\right]\leq 4N\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{pN}{600}\right)\,.

In our case, each subgraph
G^{\prime}[S_{i}]
is drawn from
\er({m}/{2},4d/m)
, and note that
m/2\geq 250\geq 10
, thus meeting the precondition of
Fact

7.10
. Hence, for any
i\in[n/m]

Fact

7.10
gives

\Pr\left[\left(\Phi_{G^{\prime}[S_{i}]}<\frac{1}{3}\right)\text{ or }\left(\exists u\in S_{i}\text{ s.t. }\left|\deg_{G^{\prime}[S_{i}]}(u)-2d\right|>\frac{1}{10}2d\right)\right]\leq 2m\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{d}{300}\right)\,,

where we used
m\geq 500
to express the degree bounds in terms of
2d
instead of
\bar{d}
.
Therefore, taking a union bound gives

\Pr\left[\forall\,i\in[n/m]\,,\left(\Phi_{G^{\prime}[S_{i}]}\geq\frac{1}{3}\text{ and }\forall u\in S_{i}\,,\left|\deg_{G^{\prime}[S_{i}]}(u)-2d\right|\leq\frac{1}{10}2d\right)\right]\geq 1-\frac{1}{n}\,,

where we used
d\geq 600\log n\geq 600\ln n
.
∎

By virtue of the above, most of Alice’s inputs can be embedded into a sample from
\mathcal{G}
where a large fraction of important edges are inter-cluster edges of any ED, as per
Lemma

7.4
. This will allow Alice and Bob to solve
\recover_{\epsilon m}
.

Reduction 7.11
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic streaming algorithm for computing a
2
-level
(\epsilon,\phi)
-RED of a graph given in a stream of edges. For a graph
G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, we reduce
\recover_{\epsilon m}
to computing a
2
-level
(\epsilon,\phi)
-RED as follows. Alice instantiates
\mathcal{A}
for the vertex set
V
, and feeds her edges
E^{\prime}
and the fixed edges of
G[T]
to
\mathcal{A}
. Then, she sends the memory state
\Pi
of
\mathcal{A}
to Bob. Then, Bob instantiates
m/2
copies of
\mathcal{A}
, call them
\mathcal{A}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{m/2}
, and sets the memory state of them to
\Pi
. Next, for each
k\in[m/2]
let
G_{k}=(V,E_{k})
be the graph defined by
(G^{\prime},k)
, and note that Bob knows the edges between
S
and
T
(see
Definition

7.2
). Then, Bob feeds the edges
E_{k}(S,T)
to
\mathcal{A}_{k}
. For each
k\in[m/2]
, let
\mathcal{U}^{k}_{1},\mathcal{U}^{k}_{2}
be the output of
\mathcal{A}_{k}
. Bob finally constructs his output set
F
as follows: for each
k\in[m/2]
, if the number of non-isolated vertices in
\mathcal{U}^{k}_{2}
is at most
3\epsilon dn
, add the pair
\{s,s_{i,k}\}
to
F
for every
i\in[n/m]
and every
s\in S_{i}
that is not an isolated vertex in
\mathcal{U}^{k}_{2}
. More formally, for a partition
\mathcal{U}
of
V
, let
V\setminus\mathcal{U}=\{u\in V:\,\{u\}\notin\mathcal{U}\}
be the set of non-isolated vertices in
\mathcal{U}
. With this notation, Bob outputs

F=\bigcup_{k\in[m/2]:|V\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}|\leq 3\epsilon dn}F_{k}\,,\quad\quad\text{with }F_{k}=\left\{\{s,s_{i,k}\}:i\in[n/m],\,s\in S_{i}\cap(V\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k})\right\}\,.

(7.5)

We remark that if Bob learns the important edges
E^{*}_{k}
for all
k\in[m/2]
, then he learns all of
E^{\prime}
.

Remark 2
.

In the setting of
Reduction

7.11
, we have
E^{\prime}=\bigcup_{k\in[m/2]}E^{*}_{k}
and
|E^{\prime}|=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in[m/2]}|E^{*}_{k}|
.

As suggested by
Remark

2
, it is enough to ensure that each instance
\mathcal{A}_{k}
recovers the important edges of
G_{k}
in order for Bob to learn the edges of
G^{\prime}
. We in particular restrict ourselves to indices
k
for which the algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{k}
, the graph
G_{k}
, and the RED
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k},\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}
are well behaved, as defined below.

Definition 7.12
.

In the setting of
Reduction

7.11
, for every
k\in[m/2]
let
\mathcal{P}_{k}
be the property that

\text{$\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k},\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}$ is an $(\epsilon,\phi,2)$-RED of $G_{k}$}\,,\quad\left|E^{*}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}^{k}_{1}\right|\geq\frac{4}{5}\cdot\left|E^{*}_{k}\right|\,,\quad|E_{k}^{*}|\geq\frac{9}{5}\frac{dn}{m}\,,\quad|E_{k}|\leq 1.3dn\,.

(7.6)

We first show a preliminary result: if there are many
k\in[m/2]
that satisfy
\mathcal{P}_{k}
, then Bob’s output set contains many edges of
G^{\prime}
.

Lemma 7.13
.

In the setting of
Reduction

7.11
, if
\epsilon\in(0,1)
satisfies
\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{30\sqrt{m}}
, then

|E^{\prime}\cap F|\geq\frac{49}{100}\frac{dn}{m}|\{k\in[m/2]:\mathcal{P}_{k}\}|\,.

Proof.

Recall from
Reduction

7.11
that
F
is made by the union of
F_{k}
over
k\in[m/2]
. We then first give a guarantee on the
F_{k}
’s individually.

Claim 7.14
.

Let
k\in[m/2]
such that
\mathcal{P}_{k}
holds. Then

1.

|V\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}|\leq 3\epsilon dn
, and

2.

|F_{k}\cap(E^{*}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}^{k}_{1})|\geq\frac{dn}{m}-\epsilon^{2}\cdot 1.3dn
.

Proof of (
1
).

Since
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is verified,
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k},\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}
is an
(\epsilon,\phi,2)
-RED of
G_{k}
(see
Definition

7.12
). By definition of RED (see
Definition

3.2
), we know
|E_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}^{k}_{1}|\leq\epsilon|E_{k}|
. Moreover, one can note that the number of non-isolated vertices in
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}
is at most twice the number of edges in
E_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k}
. This is because every non isolated vertex must have at least one edge incident on it in
E_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k}
(which is the input to the second level ED), as otherwise property (
2
) of
Definition

1.1
would be violated for any
\phi>0
. Thus,

|V\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}|\leq 2|E_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}^{k}_{1}|\leq 2\epsilon|E_{k}|\leq 3\epsilon dn\,,

where the last inequality follows because
|E_{k}|\leq 1.3dn
(see
Definition

7.12
).
∎

Proof of (
2
).

From part (
1
), together with the assumptions that
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k},\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}
is an
(\epsilon,\phi,2)
-RED of
G_{k}
and
|E_{k}|\leq 1.3dn
(see
Definition

7.12
), we have

\left|V\setminus\mathcal{U}^{k}_{2}\right|\leq 3\epsilon dn\quad\text{and}\quad\left|E_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}\right|=\left|\left(E_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k}\right)\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}\right|\leq\epsilon\left|E_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k}\right|\leq\epsilon^{2}\cdot|E_{k}|\leq\epsilon^{2}1.3dn\,.

We want to argue that many of the important edges
E^{*}_{k}
of
G_{k}
are present in the set of pairs
F_{k}
(see
Reduction

7.11
). To do this, we make the following observation for any edge
\{s,s^{\prime}\}\in E^{*}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}^{k}_{1}
: if none of
s,s^{\prime}
is an isolated vertex in
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}
, i.e.
\{s\}\notin\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}
and
\{s^{\prime}\}\notin\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}
, then
\{s,s^{\prime}\}\in F_{k}
; otherwise, if
\{s\}\in\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}
or
\{s^{\prime}\}\in\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}
, then
\{s,s^{\prime}\}\in(E_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k})\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}
(and this is because
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}
is an ED of the graph
(V,E_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k})
and
\{s,s^{\prime}\}\in E_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k}
by assumption). Therefore,

\displaystyle|F_{k}\cap(E^{*}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}^{k}_{1})|\geq|E^{*}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}^{k}_{1}|-|(E_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k})\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}|\,.

(7.7)

To conclude the claim, we use again the property
\mathcal{P}_{k}
: since
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k},\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}
is an
(\epsilon,\phi,2)
-RED of
G_{k}
, we have

\left|(E_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k})\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}\right|\leq\epsilon|E_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k}|\leq\epsilon^{2}\cdot|E_{k}|\,.

Using again
\mathcal{P}_{k}
, we know
|E^{*}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}^{k}_{1}|\geq\frac{4}{5}\cdot|E^{*}_{k}|
,
|E_{k}^{*}|\geq\frac{9}{5}{dn}/{m}
, and
|E_{k}|\leq 1.3dn
, so from (
7.7
) we conclude

|F_{k}\cap(E^{*}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}^{k}_{1})|\geq|E^{*}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}^{k}_{1}|-|(E_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k})\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}|\geq\frac{4}{5}\cdot|E^{*}_{k}|-\epsilon^{2}1.3dn\geq\frac{dn}{m}-\epsilon^{2}1.3dn\,.

(7.8)

∎

From the definition of Bob’s output
F
(see
Reduction

7.11
) and by
Remark

2
, one has

\displaystyle|E^{\prime}\cap F|=\left|\left(\bigcup_{k\in[m/2]}E_{k}^{*}\right)\cap\left(\bigcup_{k\in[m/2]:|V\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}|\leq 3\epsilon dn}F_{k}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\bigcup_{k\in[m/2]:|V\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}|\leq 3\epsilon dn}\left(E_{k}^{*}\cap F_{k}\right)\right|

by
Remark

2

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in[m/2]:|V\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}|\leq 3\epsilon dn}\left|E_{k}^{*}\cap F_{k}\right|\,,

and we finish using
Claim

7.14
:

\displaystyle|E^{\prime}\cap F|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in[m/2]:|V\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}|\leq 3\epsilon dn}\left|E_{k}^{*}\cap F_{k}\right|

by part (
1
) of
Claim

7.14

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in[m/2]:\mathcal{P}_{k}\text{ holds}}\left|E_{k}^{*}\cap F_{k}\right|\,,

and then

\displaystyle|E^{\prime}\cap F|\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in[m/2]:\mathcal{P}_{k}\text{ holds}}\left|E_{k}^{*}\cap F_{k}\right|\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in[m/2]:\mathcal{P}_{k}\text{ holds}}\left|(E^{*}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}^{k}_{1})\cap F_{k}\right|\,,

so by part (
2
) of
Claim

7.14
we have

\displaystyle|E^{\prime}\cap F|\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in[m/2]:\mathcal{P}_{k}\text{ holds}}\left(\frac{dn}{m}-\epsilon^{2}\cdot 1.3dn\right)\geq\frac{49}{100}\frac{dn}{m}|\{k\in[m/2]:\mathcal{P}_{k}\}|\,,

where we used
\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{30\sqrt{m}}
in the last inequality.
∎

Finally, we prove that an efficient RED algorithm gives an efficient
\recover
protocol, using the lemma we just showed.

Lemma 7.15
.

Let
\epsilon,\phi\in(0,1)
such that
\epsilon\leq\min\{10^{-5}\psi\frac{d}{m},\frac{1}{30\sqrt{m}}\}
and
\phi\geq 11/m
. If
n\geq 100m
,
m\geq 500
,
d\geq 600\log n
, and there is a deterministic
L
-bit space streaming algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that computes an
(\epsilon,\phi,2)
-RED with probability at least
9/10
over inputs
G\sim\mathcal{G}
, then there is a deterministic protocol that solves
\recover_{\epsilon m}
with probability at least
3/5
over inputs
G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
with communication complexity at most
L
.

Proof.

The protocol is given by
Reduction

7.11
. We observe that the communication complexity bound immediately follows, since
\mathcal{A}
has space complexity
L
by assumption, and Alice only sends to Bob the state of the algorithm.

We now focus on proving correctness of the protocol with constant probability. We do this by first proving that
F
satisfies each of the two properties of
Definition

7.8
provided that some conditions hold, and then verify that such conditions occur with constant probability.

Claim 7.16

(Property (
1
) of
Definition

7.8
)
.

If
\left|\left\{k\in[m/2]:\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right\}\right|\geq\frac{m}{3}
, then
|F|\leq 6\cdot\epsilon m|E^{\prime}|
.

Claim 7.17

(Property (
2
) of
Definition

7.8
)
.

If
\left|\left\{k\in[m/2]:\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right\}\right|\geq\frac{m}{3}
, then
|F\cap E^{\prime}|\geq 1/10\cdot|E^{\prime}|
.

Claim 7.18
.

One has
\Pr_{G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\left[\left|\left\{k\in[m/2]:\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right\}\right|\geq{m}/{3}\right]\geq{3}/{5}
.

By
Claim

7.16
and
Claim

7.17
, the protocol given by
Reduction

7.11
succeeds provided that the input
G^{\prime}
satisfies
\left|\left\{k\in[m/2]:\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right\}\right|\geq\frac{m}{3}
. By
Claim

7.18
, the latter condition is verified with probability at least
3/5
. The lemma statement is then proven, modulo showing
Claim

7.16
,
Claim

7.16
, and
Claim

7.18
, which we do next.

Proof of
Claim

7.16
.

As per
Reduction

7.11
,
F
is the union of
F_{k}=\{\{s,s_{i,k}\}:i\in[n/m],\,s\in S_{i}\cap(V\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k})\}
over
k
such that
|V\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}|\leq 3\epsilon dn
. Note that
|\{\{s,s_{i,k}\}:i\in[n/m],\,s\in S_{i}\cap(V\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k})\}|
is in fact upper-bounded by
|V\setminus\mathcal{U}_{2}^{k}|
. One then gets that
|F|\leq 3\epsilon dn\frac{m}{2}
. In order to relate this bound to
|E^{\prime}|
, we use our assumption: for at least
m/3
values of
k\in[m/2]
,
|E^{*}_{k}|\geq 9/5\cdot dn/m
. By
Remark

2
, this means
|E^{\prime}|\geq 1/2\cdot m/3\cdot(9/5\cdot dn/m)=3/10\cdot dn
, so one concludes
|F|\leq 6\epsilon|E^{\prime}|m
.
∎

Proof of
Claim

7.17
.

We can use
Lemma

7.13
, since our bound on
\epsilon
ensures the precondition of the lemma. We then have
|E^{\prime}\cap F|\geq\frac{49}{100}\frac{dn}{m}|\{k\in[m/2]:\mathcal{P}_{k}\}|
. Now, we conclude the claim by virtue of our assumption:

|E^{\prime}\cap F|\geq\frac{49}{100}\frac{dn}{m}|\{k\in[m/2]:\mathcal{P}_{k}\}|\geq\frac{m}{3}\frac{49}{100}\frac{dn}{m}\geq\frac{1}{10}|E^{\prime}|\,,

where in the last inequality we used that our assumption further implies
|E^{\prime}|\leq|E_{k}|\leq 1.3dn
for some
k\in[m/2]
.
∎

Proof of
Claim

7.18
.

We recall that for
G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
K\sim\unif([m/2])
one has
(G^{\prime},K)\sim\mathcal{G}
(see
Remark

1
). For
k\in[m/2]
, we then interchangeably write
\mathcal{A}(G)
and
\mathcal{A}(G^{\prime},k)
to refer to an execution of
\mathcal{A}
on the graph
G
associated with
(G^{\prime},k)
. Our assumption on
\mathcal{A}
rewrites as

\Pr_{G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\,K\sim\unif([m/2])}\left[\text{$\mathcal{A}(G^{\prime},K)$ fails}\right]=\Pr_{(G^{\prime},K)\sim\mathcal{G}}\left[\text{$\mathcal{A}(G^{\prime},K)$ fails}\right]\leq\frac{1}{10}\,.

Now recall
Reduction

7.11
: for each
k\in[m/2]
, the result of
\mathcal{A}_{k}
computed by Bob is exactly
\mathcal{A}(G^{\prime},k)
, where
G^{\prime}
denotes Alice’s input. Therefore, using that
G^{\prime}
and
K
are independent and that
K
is drawn uniformly, we get

\ex_{G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\left[\left|\left\{k\in[m/2]:\text{ $\mathcal{A}_{k}$ fails }\right\}\right|\right]=\ex_{G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\left[\left|\left\{k\in[m/2]:\text{ $\mathcal{A}(G^{\prime},k)$ fails }\right\}\right|\right]\leq\frac{1}{10}\cdot\frac{m}{2}\,.

Hence, if we sample
G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, only a small fraction of the values of
k
will make
(G^{\prime},k)
a failing instance. Specifically, for any
\alpha\in[1/10,1]
, Markov’s inequality gives

\Pr_{G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\left[\left|\left\{k\in[m/2]:\text{ $\mathcal{A}(G^{\prime},k)$ fails }\right\}\right|>\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{1}{10}\cdot\frac{m}{2}\right]\leq\alpha\,.

In other words, at least a
1-\alpha
fraction of the probability measure of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
yields a graph
