G^{\prime}
such that
(G^{\prime},k)
is a succeeding instance for
\mathcal{A}
with at least a
1-0.1/\alpha
fraction of the possible values for
k\in[m/2]
. By
Lemma

7.9
(which applies since we assume
m\geq 500
,
d\geq 600\log n
), we also have that a
1-1/n
fraction of the measure of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
yields a graph
G^{\prime}
where each component is a
(1\pm 1/10)2d
-regular
\frac{1}{3}
-expander. This in particular implies that at least a
1-\alpha-1/n
fraction of the probability measure of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
yields a graph
G^{\prime}
such that:

•

for every
k\in[m/2]
, the graph
G_{k}=(V,E_{k})
given by
(G^{\prime},k)
has
|E_{k}|\leq 1.3dn
, by near-regularity and
Definition

7.2
;

•

for every
k\in[m/2]
, the graph
G_{k}=(V,E_{k})
given by
(G^{\prime},k)
has
|E^{*}_{k}|\geq\frac{9}{5}\frac{dn}{m}
, by near regularity and
Definition

7.2
;

•

with at least a
1-0.1/\alpha
fraction of the possible values for
k\in[m/2]
we have the following:
\mathcal{A}_{k}
outputs an
(\epsilon,\phi,2)
-RED
\mathcal{U}^{k}_{1},\mathcal{U}^{k}_{2}
of the graph
G=(V,E_{k})
given by
(G^{\prime},k)
. Moreover, by
Lemma

7.4
(which applies, since our assumptions on
n
,
m
,
\epsilon
,
\phi
meet its preconditions) one has that
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{k}
verifies the property

\left|E^{*}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{U}^{k}_{1}\right|\geq\frac{4}{5}\cdot\left|E^{*}_{k}\right|\,.

We have then obtained that at least a
1-\alpha-1/n
fraction of the probability measure of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
yields a graph
G^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{P}_{k}
holds for at least a
1-0.1/\alpha
fraction of the possible values for
k\in[m/2]
. Setting
\alpha=3/10
gives the claim.
∎

∎

7.3
Hardness of the communication problem

We show that
\recover_{\xi}
requires linear communication in the number of edges when
G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
\xi=\epsilon m
. Intuitively, this is because Bob is only allowed to output an
\approx\epsilon m
factor more pairs than
|E^{\prime}|
, and for an appropriate parameter regime
\epsilon m|E^{\prime}|<\frac{1}{1000}\binom{m/2}{2}\frac{n}{m}
(where
\binom{m/2}{2}\frac{n}{m}
is the total number of vertex pairs in a graph consisting of
n/m
disjoint subgraphs on
m/2
vertices).

Lemma 7.19
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
such that
\epsilon\leq{10^{-33}}/{d}
. If
m\geq 500
and
d\geq 600\log n
, then any deterministic protocol that solves
\recover_{\epsilon m}
with probability at least
3/5
over inputs
G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
requires
\Omega(dn)
bits of communication.

Proof.

Let us fix hereafter
\mathcal{Q}
to be a deterministic protocol that solves
\recover_{\epsilon m}
with probability at least
3/5
over inputs
G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. We define
\mathcal{I}^{(1)}\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
to be the subset of input graphs
G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})
in the support of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{Q}
outputs correctly on input
G^{\prime}
. We also partition
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
based on the number of edges, so for every integer
t
we define
\mathcal{I}_{t}\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
to be the subset of graphs
G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
with
|E^{\prime}|=t
. We also let for convenience
\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{t}=\mathcal{I}^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{I}_{t}
be the subset of correct graphs with
t
edges. For any
G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
, we denote by
M_{G^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
the message sent by Alice on input
G^{\prime}
using the protocol
\mathcal{Q}
.

Claim 7.20
.

If
t
is an integer such that
t\in[9/10\cdot dn/2,\,11/10\cdot dn/2]
and
|\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{t}|\geq{1}/{2}\cdot|\mathcal{I}_{t}|
, then

\textup{{H}}\left(M_{G^{\prime}}\,\middle|\,G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(1)}\right)\geq\frac{dn}{3}\,.

Proof.

For any
G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{t}
, following the reception of
M_{G^{\prime}}
, Bob deterministically outputs at most
6\cdot(\epsilon m)\cdot|E^{\prime}|=6\cdot\epsilon mt
pairs of vertices
F~\subseteq~\binom{S}{2}
, such that at least
1/10\cdot|E^{\prime}|=t/10
of them are actually edges of
G^{\prime}
. Thus, for any
t
and any message
M\in\{0,1\}^{\leq nm}
(where we restrained messages to be at most
nm
-bit long without loss of generality), the number of
G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{t}
such that
M_{G^{\prime}}=M
is at most

\displaystyle\left|\left\{G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{t}:\,M_{G^{\prime}}=M\right\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0.1t}^{t}\binom{6\cdot\epsilon mt}{j}\cdot\binom{\frac{n}{m}\binom{m/2}{2}}{t-j}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0.1t}^{t}\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}\cdot 6\cdot\epsilon mt}{j}\right)^{j}\cdot\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}\cdot nm/8}{t-j}\right)^{t-j}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{t}\sum_{j=0.1t}^{t}\left(\frac{60\cdot\epsilon mt}{t}\right)^{j}\cdot\left(\frac{nm/8}{t}\right)^{t-j}\cdot\left(\frac{t}{t-j}\right)^{t-j}\,.

We employ the following fact to bound the above, whose proof is deferred to
Section

A.3
.

Fact 7.21
.

For any
0<x\leq y
,
(y/x)^{x}\leq\mathrm{e}^{y/\mathrm{e}}
.

We apply
Fact

7.21
to the factor
(t/t-j)^{(t-j)}
with
x=t-j
and
y=t
, and hence we get

\displaystyle\left|\left\{G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{t}:\,M_{G^{\prime}}=M\right\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{(1+1/\mathrm{e})t}\sum_{j=0.1t}^{t}\left(60\epsilon m\right)^{j}\cdot\left(\frac{nm/8}{t}\right)^{t-j}

(7.9)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{(1+1/\mathrm{e})t}\left(\frac{nm}{8t}\right)^{t}\sum_{j=0.1t}^{t}\left(60\cdot\frac{\epsilon 8t}{n}\right)^{j}

(7.10)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{(1+1/\mathrm{e})t}\left(\frac{nm}{8t}\right)^{t}\left({500}\cdot\frac{\epsilon t}{n}\right)^{t/10}t\,,

(7.11)

where in the last inequality we used the upper bounds on
\epsilon
and
t
. On the other hand, by virtue of our assumption that
|\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(1)}|\geq{1}/{2}\cdot|\mathcal{I}_{t}|
, one has

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{t}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\left|\left\{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime}):\,|E^{\prime}|=t\right\}\right|

(7.12)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\left|\left\{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime}):\,\forall\,i\in[n/m],\,\left|E^{\prime}\cap\binom{S_{i}}{2}\right|=\frac{t}{n/m}\right\}\right|

(7.13)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\binom{\binom{m/2}{2}}{\frac{t}{n/m}}^{n/m}\geq\frac{1}{2}\binom{\frac{m^{2}}{16}}{\frac{t}{n/m}}^{n/m}\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\frac{m^{2}}{16}}{\frac{t}{n/m}}\right)^{\frac{t}{n/m}\cdot n/m}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{nm}{16t}\right)^{t}\,.

(7.14)

Next, we rewrite

\displaystyle\textup{{H}}\left(M_{G^{\prime}}\,\middle|\,G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\textup{{I}}\left(G^{\prime};M_{G^{\prime}}\,\middle|\,G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle=\textup{{H}}\left(G^{\prime}\,\middle|\,G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(1)}\right)-\textup{{H}}\left(G^{\prime}\,\middle|\,M_{G^{\prime}},G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(1)}\right)\,.

Now, observing that all the graphs with same number of edges have the same probability of being sampled, one can employ the upper and lower bounds derived above:

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}\textup{{H}}\left(G^{\prime}\,\middle|\,G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(1)}\right)-\textup{{H}}\left(G^{\prime}\,\middle|\,M_{G^{\prime}},{G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(1)}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\log\left(\left|\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{t}\right|\right)-\log\left(\max_{M\in\{0,1\}^{\leq nm}}\left|\left\{H\in\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{t}:\,M_{H}=M_{G^{\prime}}\right\}\right|\right)

by (
7.14
) and (
7.11
)

\displaystyle\geq t\log\left(\frac{\frac{1}{2}\frac{nm}{16t}}{\mathrm{e}^{(1+1/\mathrm{e})}\cdot\frac{nm}{8t}\cdot{(t)}^{1/t}\cdot\left(500\cdot\frac{\epsilon t}{n}\right)^{1/10}}\right)\,.

Then, we finish using
9/10\cdot dn/2\leq t\leq 11/10\cdot dn/2
and
\epsilon\leq{10^{-33}}/{d}
:

\displaystyle\textup{{H}}\left(M_{G^{\prime}}\,\middle|\,G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(1)}\right)\geq t\log\left(\frac{1/120}{\left(500\cdot\frac{\epsilon 11dn}{20n}\right)^{1/10}}\right)\geq t\log 6\geq\frac{9}{10}\frac{dn}{2}\geq\frac{dn}{3}\,.

∎

Claim 7.22
.

One has

\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\left[|E^{\prime}|\in\left[\frac{9}{20}dn,\,\frac{11}{20}dn\right]\,\mathrm{and}\,\frac{\left|\mathcal{I}_{|E^{\prime}|}^{(1)}\right|}{\left|\mathcal{I}_{|E^{\prime}|}\right|}\geq\frac{1}{2}\right]\geq\frac{1}{10}\,.

Proof.

We begin by defining for convenience

x=\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\left[\frac{\left|\mathcal{I}_{|E^{\prime}|}^{(1)}\right|}{\left|\mathcal{I}_{|E^{\prime}|}\right|}<\frac{1}{2}\right]\,.

Then, by
Lemma

7.9
(which applies by our assumption on
m
and
d
), one has

\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\left[|E^{\prime}|\in\left[\frac{9}{20}dn,\,\frac{11}{20}dn\right]\,\mathrm{and}\,\frac{\left|\mathcal{I}_{|E^{\prime}|}^{(1)}\right|}{\left|\mathcal{I}_{|E^{\prime}|}\right|}\geq\frac{1}{2}\right]\geq 1-\frac{1}{n}-x\,.

(7.15)

We are left with the task of upper-bounding the probability
x
. Letting
\mathcal{T}=\{0,\dots,\binom{m/2}{2}\frac{n}{m}\}
be the set of possible numbers of edges in
G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\left[\mathcal{Q}\text{ outputs correctly on input }G^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\left[G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{(1)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}[|E^{\prime}|=t]\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}[G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{(1)}\,|\,|E^{\prime}|=t]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}[|E^{\prime}|=t]\cdot\frac{\left|\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(1)}\right|}{\left|\mathcal{I}_{t}\right|}\,,

where the last equality holds because all the graphs with
t
edges have the same probability of being sampled. Hence, splitting the sum one has

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\left[\mathcal{Q}\text{ outputs correctly on input }G^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\mathcal{T}:\\
|\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(}1)|<\frac{1}{2}|\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(}1)|\end{subarray}}\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}[|E^{\prime}|=t]\cdot\frac{1}{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\mathcal{T}:\\
|\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(}1)|\geq\frac{1}{2}|\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(}1)|\end{subarray}}\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}[|E^{\prime}|=t]\cdot 1

\displaystyle=\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\left[\frac{\left|\mathcal{I}_{|E^{\prime}|}^{(1)}\right|}{\left|\mathcal{I}_{|E^{\prime}|}\right|}<\frac{1}{2}\right]\cdot\frac{1}{2}+\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\left[\frac{\left|\mathcal{I}_{|E^{\prime}|}^{(1)}\right|}{\left|\mathcal{I}_{|E^{\prime}|}\right|}\geq\frac{1}{2}\right]\cdot 1

\displaystyle=x\cdot\frac{1}{2}+(1-x)\cdot 1=1-\frac{x}{2}\,.

If we suppose for the sake of a contradiction that
x\geq 449/500
, then

\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\left[\mathcal{Q}\text{ outputs correctly on input }G^{\prime}\right]\leq\frac{551}{1000}<\frac{3}{5}\,,

which contradicts our assumption on
\mathcal{Q}
. Therefore, we plug
x<449/500
into (
7.15
) together with the assumption
n>m\geq 500
, thus getting the claim.
∎

Let now
\mathcal{C}
be the event that
|E^{\prime}|\in[9/10\cdot dn/2,\,11/10\cdot dn/2]
and
|\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{|E^{\prime}|}|\geq{1}/{2}\cdot|\mathcal{I}_{|E^{\prime}|}|
.
The size of Alice’s message on input
G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\textup{{H}}\left(M_{G^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\textup{{H}}\left(M_{G^{\prime}}\,\middle|\,\ind_{\mathcal{C}},|E^{\prime}|\right)

\displaystyle\geq\Pr\left[\mathcal{C}\right]\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\left[\frac{9}{20}dn,\,\frac{11}{20}dn\right]\cap\mathbb{N}:\\
\left|\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{t}\right|\geq{1}/{2}\cdot\left|\mathcal{I}_{t}\right|\end{subarray}}\Pr\left[|E^{\prime}|=t\,\middle|\,\mathcal{C}\right]\cdot\textup{{H}}\left(M_{G^{\prime}}\,\middle|\,{\mathcal{C}},|E^{\prime}|=t\right)\,,

and because conditioning does not increase entropy we rewrite

\displaystyle\textup{{H}}\left(M_{G^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\Pr\left[\mathcal{C}\right]\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\frac{9}{20}dn\leq t\leq\frac{11}{20}dn:\\
|\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{t}|\geq{1}/{2}\cdot|\mathcal{I}_{t}|\end{subarray}}\Pr\left[|E^{\prime}|=t\,\middle|\,\mathcal{C}\right]\cdot\textup{{H}}\left(M_{G^{\prime}}\,\middle|\,\ind_{G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{(1)}},{\mathcal{C}},|E^{\prime}|=t\right)

\displaystyle\geq\Pr\left[\mathcal{C}\right]\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\frac{9}{20}dn\leq t\leq\frac{11}{20}dn:\\
|\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{t}|\geq{1}/{2}\cdot|\mathcal{I}_{t}|\end{subarray}}\Pr\left[|E^{\prime}|=t\,\middle|\,\mathcal{C}\right]\cdot\Pr\left[G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{(1)}\,\middle|\,|E^{\prime}|=t\right]\cdot\textup{{H}}\left(M_{G^{\prime}}\,\middle|\,{G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{t}^{(1)}}\right)\,.

Finally, we use
Claim

7.20
and
Claim

7.22
and get

\displaystyle\textup{{H}}\left(M_{G^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\Pr\left[\mathcal{C}\right]\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\frac{9}{20}dn\leq t\leq\frac{11}{20}dn:\\
|\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{t}|\geq{1}/{2}\cdot|\mathcal{I}_{t}|\end{subarray}}\Pr\left[|E^{\prime}|=t\,\middle|\,\mathcal{C}\right]\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{dn}{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{dn}{6}\Pr_{G^{\prime}=(S,E^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\left[|E^{\prime}|\in\left[\frac{9}{20}dn,\,\frac{11}{20}dn\right]\,\mathrm{and}\,\frac{\left|\mathcal{I}_{|E^{\prime}|}^{(1)}\right|}{\left|\mathcal{I}_{|E^{\prime}|}\right|}\geq\frac{1}{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{dn}{60}\,.

∎

7.4
Proving the lower bound

Finally, we set
d
and
m
depending on
\epsilon,\phi,n
so as to combine
Lemma

7.15
and
Lemma

7.19
and conclude our streaming lower bound.
See
3

Proof.

We recall that for any
\epsilon=1-\Omega(1)
, computing an
(\epsilon,\phi)
-ED with constant probability requires
\Omega(n\log n)
bits for all
\phi\in(0,\epsilon]
. To see why, consider an input graph that is a matching of size
n/10
, and notice that an ED is a
1-\epsilon
fraction the edges. Therefore, we now consider the case
\epsilon=O(1/\log n)
and prove an
\Omega(n/\epsilon)
lower bound.

Let
\epsilon,\phi\in(0,1)
be any ED parameters and define
c=\psi\cdot 10^{-40}
. Note that
c
is a constant since
\psi=\Omega(1)
is the fixed sparsity from
Definition

7.2
. In order to apply
Lemma

7.15
and
Lemma

7.19
, we use distributions
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
from
Definition

7.2
with
d
and
m
set as follows:

d=\frac{\epsilon}{2c^{3}\phi}+\frac{c}{2\epsilon}\quad\text{and}\quad m=\frac{11}{c\phi}\,.

(7.16)

We verify that these values meet the preconditions set by
Lemma

7.15
and
Lemma

7.19
, i.e. we show that
d
and
m
satisfy

\epsilon\leq\min\left\{10^{-5}\psi\frac{d}{m},\,\frac{1}{30\sqrt{m}},\,\frac{10^{-33}}{d}\right\}\,,\quad\phi\geq\frac{11}{m}\,,\quad 500\leq m\leq\frac{n}{100}\,,\quad 600\log n\leq d<m\,,

(7.17)

for all
\epsilon,\phi
such that

\epsilon\leq\frac{c^{2}}{\log n}\,,\quad\phi\geq\frac{1}{c^{2}n}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{c^{4}}\leq\phi\leq\epsilon\,.

(7.18)

Substituting
d
and
m
as defined in (
7.16
) into (
7.17
) we get

10^{-5}\psi\frac{d}{m}=10^{-5}\psi\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2c^{3}\phi}+\frac{c}{2\epsilon}\right)\cdot\frac{c\phi}{11}\geq 10^{-5}\psi\frac{\epsilon}{2c^{2}11}=10^{75}\frac{\epsilon}{2\psi 11}\geq\epsilon\,,

\frac{1}{30\sqrt{m}}=\frac{\sqrt{c}\sqrt{\phi}}{30\sqrt{11}}\geq\frac{\sqrt{c}\epsilon}{30\sqrt{11}c^{2}}\geq\epsilon\,,

\frac{10^{-33}}{d}=10^{-33}\frac{4c^{3}\epsilon\phi}{2\epsilon^{2}+2c^{4}\phi}=10^{-33}\frac{4c^{3}\epsilon}{2\epsilon^{2}/\phi+2c^{4}}\geq 10^{-33}\frac{4c^{3}\epsilon}{2c^{4}+2c^{4}}=10^{-33}\frac{\epsilon}{c}\geq\epsilon\,,

\frac{11}{m}=c{\phi}\leq\phi\,,

m=\frac{11}{c\phi}\geq\frac{11}{c\epsilon}\geq\log n\geq 500\,,

m=\frac{11}{c\phi}\leq 11cn\leq\frac{n}{100}\,,

d=\frac{\epsilon}{2c^{3}\phi}+\frac{c}{2\epsilon}\geq\frac{c}{2\epsilon}\geq\frac{\log n}{2c}\geq 600\log n\,,

d=\frac{\epsilon}{2c^{3}\phi}+\frac{c}{2\epsilon}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2c^{3}\phi}+\frac{c}{2\phi}\leq\frac{1}{2c\log n\phi}+\frac{c}{2\phi}<\frac{11}{c\phi}=m\,,

so all the conditions (
7.17
) are verified.

With any
\epsilon,\phi
as in (
7.18
) and for our choice of
d,m
as in (
7.17
),
Lemma

7.15
gives that if there is an
L
-bit space deterministic streaming algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that outputs an
(\epsilon,\phi,2)
-RED of
G\sim\mathcal{G}_{n,d,m}
with probability at least
9/10
, then there is a deterministic protocol
\mathcal{Q}
that solves
\recover_{\epsilon m}
with probability at least
3/5
over inputs
G^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
with communication complexity
L
. On the other hand, by
Lemma

7.19
we know that, in the parameter regime of (
7.17
), the complexity of
\mathcal{Q}
over
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is at least
\Omega(dn)
. Hence,

L=\Omega(dn)=\Omega\left(\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\phi}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)\cdot n\right)=\Omega\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)\,.

By Yao’s minimax principle, the theorem statement is proved with
C=c^{-4}
.

∎

Appendix A
Technical facts

A.1
Polynomial time balanced sparse cut algorithm

In this section, we prove the following fast BSCA, used for our polynomial time BLD construction.

See
6.2

Proof.

First we show how to reduce the BSCA task on graphs with self-loops to graphs without self-loops. Let
G=(V,E,w)
be a weighted undirected graph with one weighted self-loop per vertex, where
w:E\cup V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
E\subseteq\binom{V}{2}
. The idea is to construct a graph with two copies of every vertex, where the surplus vertices are solely connected to their corresponding vertex with weight equal to half the weight of the self-loop. If a vertex has no self-loops then we make only one copy for it. Formally, let
A=\{u\in V:w(u)>0\}
be the set of vertices with self-loops. Then let
B
be a copy of
A
representing the self-loops of vertices in
A
, and let
s:A\rightarrow B
be a bijection mapping
u\in A
to its corresponding self-loop vertex
s(u)\in B
. We define the self-loop free graph to be
\widehat{G}=(\widehat{V},\widehat{E},\widehat{w})
, where
\widehat{V}=V\cup s(A)
,
\widehat{E}=E\cup(\cup_{u\in A}\{\{u,s(u)\}\})
, and
\widehat{w}(e)=w(e)
for every
e\in E
and
\widehat{w}(\{u,s(u)\})=\frac{1}{2}w(u)
for all
u\in A
. This is illustrated in
Figure

6
. We highlight that
\widehat{G}
has the same number of vertices and edges as
G
, up to constants.

Figure 6:
On the left: the original graph with self-loops
G
. On the right: its self-loop free version
\widehat{G}
. The weights of the internal edges of
G
are copied verbatim (not shown in the figure), while the weights of self-loops are halved.

We run a BSCA for self-loop free graphs on
\widehat{G}
, and if it asserts
\widehat{G}
to be an expander we conclude the same for
G
, whereas if it returns a cut then we tweak it to get a solution for
G
. This is summarised in Algorithm
5
. The following claim gives the correctness of the reduction. The idea is that the subgraphs given by
\{u,s(u)\}
for
u\in A
are expanders, so there is no good reason for a sparse cut to cross them. Hence,
u
and
s(u)
should be on the same side of the sparse cuts.

Algorithm 5

SelfLoopBalSparseCut
: reduction to self-loop free BSCA

1:

\triangleright

G=(V,E,w)
is a weighted undirected graph

2:

\triangleright

\phi\in(0,1)
is the sparsity parameter

3:

procedure

SelfLoopBalSparseCut
(G,\phi)

4:

\widehat{\omega}\leftarrow\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}(\widehat{G},\phi)

\triangleright

\widehat{G}
is the self-loop free version of
G
as described above

5:

if

\widehat{\omega}=\bot

then

6:

\omega\leftarrow\bot

7:

else

8:

(R,\nu)\leftarrow\widehat{\omega}

9:

X\leftarrow R\cap V

10:

X^{*}\leftarrow\argmin\{\vol_{G}(X),\vol_{G}(V\setminus X)\}

11:

\omega\leftarrow(X^{*},\vol_{G}(X^{*}))

12:

return

\omega

Claim A.1
.

Let
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
be an
(\alpha,\lambda)
-BSCA. Then
SelfLoopBalSparseCut
is a
(2\alpha,4\lambda)
-BSCA, provided that the input sparsity parameter
\phi\in(0,1)
satisfies
\phi\leq\frac{1}{10\alpha}
.

Proof.

For any cut
\emptyset\neq X\subsetneq V
, let us define for convenience
\partial_{G}X=w(X,V\setminus X)
to be the total weight of the edges crossing the cut
X
in
G
, and for any cut
\emptyset\neq R\subsetneq\widehat{V}
define
\partial_{\widehat{G}}R=\widehat{w}(R,\widehat{V}\setminus R)
to be the total weight of edges crossing
R
in
\widehat{G}
. A preliminary observation is that for any cut
\emptyset\neq X\subsetneq V
we have that
\vol_{\widehat{G}}(X\cup s(X\cap A))=\vol_{G}(X)
,
\partial_{\widehat{G}}(X\cup s(X\cap A))=\partial_{G}X
, and consequently
\Phi_{\widehat{G}}(X\cup s(X\cap A))=\Phi_{G}(X)
. By definition of
\textsc{BalSparseCut}_{\alpha,\lambda}
, we know that
\widehat{\omega}
is a
(\phi,\alpha,\lambda,0)
-BSCW of
\widehat{G}
, i.e.

1.

if
\widehat{\omega}=\bot
, then
\widehat{G}
is a
\phi
-expander;

2.

if
\widehat{\omega}=(R,\nu)
, then:

(a)

\Phi_{\widehat{G}}(R)<\alpha\cdot\phi
,

(b)

\vol_{\widehat{G}}(R)\leq\vol_{\widehat{G}}(\widehat{V}\setminus R)
,

(c)

for every other cut
\emptyset\neq T\subsetneq\widehat{V}
with
\Phi_{\widehat{G}}(T)<\phi
and
\vol_{\widehat{G}}(T)\leq\vol_{\widehat{G}}(\widehat{V}\setminus T)
one has
\vol_{\widehat{G}}(T)\leq\lambda\vol_{\widehat{G}}(R)
:

(d)

\nu=\vol_{\widehat{G}}(R)
.

Our goal is to translate these properties to
G
by showing that
\omega
, as constructed in
SelfLoopBalSparseCut
, is a
(\phi,2\alpha,4\lambda,0)
-BSCW of
G
, i.e.

1.

if
\omega=\bot
, then
{G}
is a
\phi
-expander;

2.

if
\omega=(X^{*},\nu)
, then:

(a)

\Phi_{{G}}(X^{*})<\alpha\cdot\phi
,

(b)

\vol_{{G}}(X^{*})\leq\vol_{{G}}({V}\setminus X^{*})
,

(c)

for every other cut
\emptyset\neq T\subsetneq{V}
with
\Phi_{{G}}(T)<\phi
and
\vol_{{G}}(T)\leq\vol_{{G}}({V}\setminus T)
one has
\vol_{{G}}(T)\leq\lambda\vol_{{G}}(X^{*})
:

(d)

\nu=\vol_{{G}}(X^{*})
.

Correctness of the case
\widehat{\omega}=\bot
.

This means that for every cut
\emptyset\neq R\subsetneq\widehat{V}
one has
\Phi_{\widehat{G}}(R)\geq\phi
. This in particular holds for cuts of the form
X\cup s(X\cap A)
with
\emptyset\neq X\subsetneq V
. As we observed that
\Phi_{\widehat{G}}(X\cup s(X\cap A))=\Phi_{G}(X)
, we conclude that
G
is also a
\phi
-expander.

Correctness of the case
\widehat{\omega}=(R,\nu)
.

The idea is that adding missing self-loops and removing dangling self-loops makes
R
a solution for
G
. This is illustrated in
Figure

7
.

Figure 7:
There is a straightforward correspondence between the shaded cuts in
G
and
\widehat{G}
, since they have same volume and same number of crossing edges. The dashed cut has missing self-loops and dangling self-loops (think of it as the cut
R
). The dotted cut adds the missing self-loops (think of it as the cut
Q
). The shaded cut in
\widehat{G}
removes the dangling self-loops (think of it as the cut
P
), and corresponds to the shaded cut in
G
(think of it as the cut
X
).

We know from (
2a
) that

\frac{\partial_{\widehat{G}}R}{\vol_{\widehat{G}}(R)}<\alpha\phi\,.

Consider the cut
Q=R\cup s(R\cap A)
, i.e. a version of
R
where we have added the self-loops of all the vertices in
R
that have one. Then
Q\subsetneq\widehat{V}
, as otherwise
V\subseteq R
, which would imply that
R
contains more than half the volume of
\vol_{\widehat{G}}(\widehat{V})
, contradicting (
2b
). Next, we remark that the volume of
Q
cannot decrease compared to
R
, since we added vertices, i.e.

\vol_{\widehat{G}}(Q)=\vol_{\widehat{G}}(R)+\partial_{\widehat{G}}(s(R\cap A)\setminus(R\cap B))\geq\vol_{\widehat{G}}(R)\,.

(A.1)

At the same time, we do not increase the number of crossing edges since we move the self-loop edges inside the cut, so

\frac{\partial_{\widehat{G}}Q}{\vol_{\widehat{G}}(Q)}=\frac{\partial_{\widehat{G}}R-\partial_{\widehat{G}}(s(R\cap A)\setminus(R\cap B))}{\vol_{\widehat{G}}(R)+\partial_{\widehat{G}}(s(R\cap A)\setminus(R\cap B))}\leq\frac{\partial_{\widehat{G}}R}{\vol_{\widehat{G}}(R)}\,.

Thus, we have added self-loops to
R
without increasing its sparsity nor decreasing its volume. Now we remove the dangling self-loops from
Q
, i.e. we remove the set
D=(Q\cap B)\setminus s(Q\cap A)
, and consider the cut
P=Q\setminus D
. We remark that
P\neq\emptyset
, as otherwise
R
is solely made of vertices from
s(A)
, which contradicts
\Phi_{H}(R)<\alpha\phi\leq 1/10<1
. Since we removed an equal amount of mass from both the crossing edges and the volume, we get

\frac{\partial_{\widehat{G}}P}{\vol_{\widehat{G}}(P)}\leq\min\left\{\frac{\partial_{\widehat{G}}P}{\vol_{\widehat{G}}(P)},1\right\}\leq\frac{\partial_{\widehat{G}}P+\partial_{\widehat{G}}D}{\vol_{\widehat{G}}(P)+\partial_{\widehat{G}}D}=\frac{\partial_{\widehat{G}}Q}{\vol_{\widehat{G}}(Q)}\,.

Thus, removing the dangling self-loops does not increase sparsity. However we may have decreased the volume, which threatens the balancedness property of the cut. We then bound the decrease: because we proved before
{\partial_{\widehat{G}}Q}/{\vol_{\widehat{G}}(Q)}<\alpha\phi
, we know

\partial_{\widehat{G}}D<\frac{\alpha\phi}{1-\alpha\phi}\vol_{\widehat{G}}(P)\,,

so

\vol_{\widehat{G}}(Q)=\vol_{\widehat{G}}(P)+\partial_{\widehat{G}}D<2\vol_{\widehat{G}}(P)\,.

(A.2)

Now one can see that
P
is identical to
(R\cap V)\cup s(R\cap A)
, and
X
as defined in Algorithm
5
equals
R\cap V
. Hence
\partial_{G}X=\partial_{\widehat{G}}P
and
\vol_{G}(X)=\vol_{\widehat{G}}(P)
. Therefore,

\frac{\partial_{G}X}{\vol_{G}(X)}<\alpha\phi\,.

(A.3)

To handle the case where
X
is the larger volume side of the cut,
Algorithm

5
takes
X^{*}
to be the side of
(X,V\setminus X)
with smaller volume. We show that the volume of
V\setminus X
is within a small constant factor of the volume of
X
. To do so, recall that
\Phi_{\widehat{G}}(R)<\alpha\phi
, so

\frac{\partial_{\widehat{G}}Q+\partial_{\widehat{G}}(s(R\cap A)\setminus(R\cap B))}{\vol_{\widehat{G}}(Q)-\partial_{\widehat{G}}(s(R\cap A)\setminus(R\cap B))}<\alpha\phi\,,

which implies

\partial_{\widehat{G}}(s(R\cap A)\setminus(R\cap B))<\alpha\phi\vol_{\widehat{G}}(Q)\,.

Hence, by property (
2b
) of
R
, we have

\vol_{G}(X)\leq\vol_{\widehat{G}}(Q)<\frac{1}{1-\alpha\phi}\vol_{\widehat{G}}(R)\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{1-\alpha\phi}\vol_{\widehat{G}}(\widehat{V})=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{1-\alpha\phi}\vol_{G}(V)\,.

Therefore, we have that
X^{*}=\argmin\{\vol_{G}(X),\vol_{G}(V\setminus X)\}
satisfies

(1-2\alpha\phi)\vol_{G}(X)\leq\vol_{G}(X^{*})\leq\vol_{G}(V\setminus X^{*})\,,

(A.4)

and

\vol_{\widehat{G}}(R)<\frac{2}{1-2\alpha\phi}\vol_{G}(X^{*})\,,

(A.5)

by (
A.1
), (
A.2
). We now conclude by verifying properties (
2a
), (
2b
), (
2c
), (
2d
).

•

Property (
2a
).
From (
A.3
) and (
A.4
), we get
\Phi_{G}(X^{*})<\alpha\phi/({1-2\alpha\phi})\leq 2\alpha\phi
.

•

Property (
2b
).
This property follows by definition of
X^{*}
in
Algorithm

5
.

•

Property (
2c
).
Consider any cut
\emptyset\neq T\subsetneq V
such that
\Phi_{G}(T)<\phi
and
\vol_{G}(T)\leq\vol_{G}(V\setminus T)
. Then we know that

\Phi_{G}(T)=\Phi_{\widehat{G}}(T\cup s(T\cap A))<\phi\,,

and

\vol_{\widehat{G}}(T\cup s(T\cap A))=\vol_{G}(T)\leq\vol_{G}(V\setminus T)=\vol_{\widehat{G}}(\widehat{V}\setminus(T\cup s(T\cap A))\,,

as well as

\vol_{G}(T)=\vol_{\widehat{G}}(T\cup s(T\cap A))\leq\lambda\cdot\vol_{\widehat{G}}(R)<\frac{2\lambda}{1-2\alpha\phi}\vol_{G}(X^{*})\leq 4\lambda\vol_{G}(X^{*})\,,

by an application of (
2c
) to the cut
T\cup s(T\cap A)
and (
A.5
).

•

Property (
2d
).
Follows directly by definition of
\omega
in
Algorithm

5
.

∎
Using
Claim

A.1
, we now conclude a proof of
Theorem

6.2
. To do so, we run two different algorithms, depending on how large
p
and
q
are.

The large case.

An algorithm was recently given that, for
p
-vertex
q
-edge input graphs with edge weights bounded by
\poly(p)
produces a
(O(\phi\log^{3}p),\phi)
-ED
5
5

5

In this case, the definition of ED uses the weight of edges across cuts, and weighted volumes, including self-loops.
with probability
1-1/\poly(p)
in
(p+q)\cdot\pylog(p)
time, for any
\phi\in(0,1)

[
LNPS23
]
. One can then use this ED algorithm to efficiently compute an approximate balanced
\psi
-sparse cut for all
\psi\in(0,1)
: as it was observed in
[
SW19
]
, given access to a graph and its
(O(\psi\log^{3}p),\psi)
-ED, one can easily obtain a
(\psi,c^{\prime}\cdot\log^{3}p,C^{\prime},0)
-BSCW for some constants
c^{\prime},C^{\prime}\geq 1
. This gives a
(c^{\prime}\cdot\log^{3}p,C^{\prime})
-BSCA that runs in
(p+q)\cdot\pylog(p)
time for all input sparsity parameters
\psi\in(0,1)
.

Whenever
p\geq n^{1/c_{\text{time}}}
, for a constant
c_{\text{time}}\geq 1
to be determined later, we then apply the algorithm of
[
SW19
,
LNPS23
]
together with
Claim

A.1
setting
\alpha=c^{\prime}\log^{3}n
and
\lambda=C^{\prime}
(where
c^{\prime},C^{\prime}\geq 1
are the constants one gets from the self-loop free BSCA of
[
SW19
,
LNPS23
]
). Then, we get a
(c_{\sw}\log^{3}n,C_{\sw})
-BSCA where
c_{\sw}=2c^{\prime}
and
C_{\sw}=4C^{\prime}
, provided that
\phi\leq\frac{1}{10\alpha}
. Using
\phi=\psi
and since we impose
\psi\leq\frac{1}{10c_{\sw}\log^{3}n}
, we get
\phi\leq\frac{1}{10\alpha}
.

Using our assumption on
p,q,W
, the space and random bit complexity trivially follow from the bound on the running time, which is in turn at most
\poly(n)
. The success probability is
1-1/\poly(p)
, which gives the claimed success probability of
1-1/\poly(n)
.

The small case.

When
p<n^{1/c_{\text{time}}}
, we use an algorithm running in
T(p,q,W)
time. The algorithm consists in using an algorithm that
(c^{\prime\prime}\log p)
-approximates the sparsity of the input graph (such as the deterministic polynomial time algorithm of
[
LR99
,
LLR95
]
, which allows to have weighted self-loops by incorporating them in the demand graph) as follows (which is essentially the idea of
[
SW19
]
): maintain a cut
R
initially empty and repeatedly compute the approximate sparsest cut
S
of
G[V\setminus R]^{1}
; if
S
has sparsity at least
2\psi c^{\prime\prime}\log p
then terminate, else update
R
to be
R\cup S
; if
R
accounts for at least a
1/5
fraction of the total volume of
G
then terminate, else repeat.

First note that this algorithm terminates (e.g. when
V\setminus R
is a single vertex). Also observe that if
R=\emptyset
then we can conclude that
G
is a
2\psi
-expander. Otherwise,
R
has always sparsity at most
4\psi c^{\prime\prime}\log p
in
G
and one of
R,V\setminus R
hs volume at most half of the total. Moreover, if the smaller of
R,V\setminus R
accounts for at least a
1/5
fraction of the total volume of
G
then every
\psi
-sparse cut with volume at most half the total is within a
5/3
fraction of the volume of the smaller of
R,V\setminus R
. Finally, if
R
accounts for less than a
1/5
fraction of the total volume of
G
, then
G[V\setminus R]^{1}
is a
2\psi
-expander; consider a cut
T
with sparsity at most
\psi
and volume at most half the total; then if
T
had more than half its volume in
V\setminus R
, the weight of edges crossing the cut
T
in
G
would be more than a
\psi
fraction of the volume of
T
, a contradiction; hence,
T
is within a factor of
2
of the volume of
R
. This gives a
(c^{\prime\prime}\log n,2)
-BSCA.

We pick
c_{\text{time}}
so that
T(p,q,W)
is at most
\widetilde{O}(n/b^{2})\cdot\log^{O(\frac{\log n}{\log 1/b})}n
, thus giving the claimed bound on the space and random bit complexity. In our case,
T(p,q,W)=\poly(p,q,\log W)
(which can be achieved by solving the linear programming relaxation of
[
LR99
]
with the ellipsoid method and rounding it deterministically with the algorithm of
[
LLR95
]
) and recall that
W=\poly(n)
, so there exists a constant
c_{\text{time}}\geq 1
such that
T(p,q,W)
is at most
n/b^{2}\cdot\log^{O(\frac{\log n}{\log 1/b})}n
. The running time also follows, and the success probability is
1
.

Theorem

6.2
is concluded by combining the large and small case.

∎

A.2
Expansion and regularity of random graphs

See
7.10

Proof.

Let
H=(U,F)
be a sample from
\er(N,p)
, so that
|U|=N
. Also let
L=D-A
be the Laplacian matrix of
H
, where
D
denotes its degree diagonal matrix and
A
denotes its adjacency matrix. We use the following matrix Bernstein concentration bound on
L
to lower bound its second eigenvalue, and then lower bound the expansion of
H
using Cheeger’s inequality.

Theorem A.2

(Theorem 1.6.2 in
[
Tro15
]
)
.

Let
S_{1},\dots,S_{\kappa}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
be independent symmetric random matrices such that
\ex[S_{\iota}]=0
and
\left\|S_{\iota}\right\|\leq\lambda
for every
\iota\in[\kappa]
, and let
S=\sum_{\iota=1}^{\kappa}S_{\iota}
. Then, for all
\theta\geq 0

\Pr[\left\|S\right\|\geq\theta]\leq 2N\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{\theta^{2}/2}{\left\|\sum_{\iota=1}^{\kappa}\ex[S_{\iota}^{2}]\right\|+\lambda\theta/3}\right)\,.

We start by rewriting
L
so that we can apply the above result. For each
e=\{u,v\}\in\binom{V}{2}
let
z_{e}\sim\ber(p)
be an independent Bernoulli random variable with bias
p
, and also let
L_{e}
be the Laplacian matrix of the graph
(U,\{e\})
, i.e.
(L_{e})_{ab}=1
if
a=b=u
or
a=b=v
,
(L_{e})_{ab}=-1
if
\{a,b\}=e
, and
(L_{e})_{ab}=0
otherwise. Define
S_{e}=z_{e}L_{e}-\ex[z_{e}L_{e}]
and
S=\sum_{e\in\binom{V}{2}}S_{e}
. One can now note that

\ex[S_{e}]=0\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|S_{e}\right\|\leq\max\left\{\left\|(1-p)L_{e}\right\|,\left\|-pL_{e}\right\|\right\}\leq 2\quad\text{for all $e\in\binom{V}{2}$.}

Letting
J
be the all-ones
N\times N
matrix we also have

S=\sum_{e\in\binom{V}{2}}z_{e}L_{e}-\sum_{e\in\binom{V}{2}}\ex[z_{e}L_{e}]=L-p\cdot(N\cdot I-J)\,.

Hence,
Theorem

A.2
with
\lambda=2
gives

\Pr[\left\|L-p\cdot(N\cdot I-J)\right\|\geq\theta]\leq 2N\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{\theta^{2}/2}{\left\|\sum_{e\in\binom{V}{2}}\ex[(z_{e}L_{e}-\ex[z_{e}L_{e}])^{2}]\right\|+2\theta/3}\right)\,.

(A.6)

Now we observe that the first term in the denominator can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{e\in\binom{V}{2}}\ex\left[(z_{e}L_{e}-\ex[z_{e}L_{e}])^{2}\right]\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{e\in\binom{V}{2}}2L_{e}\ex\left[(z_{e}-z_{e}\cdot p+p^{2})\right]\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{e\in\binom{V}{2}}2pL_{e}\right\|

\displaystyle=2p\left\|N\cdot I-J\right\|

\displaystyle=2pN\,,

where the last equality holds because
K\coloneqq N\cdot I-J
is the Laplacian matrix of the complete graph on
N
vertices. If we now set
\theta=pN/10
in (
A.6
), we get

\Pr\left[\left\|L-p\cdot K\right\|\geq\frac{1}{10}pN\right]\leq 2N\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{pN}{600}\right)\,.

(A.7)

Now, in order to use a Cheeger type of inequality, it is convenient to view
H
as a regular graph, so that its normalized Laplacian matrix is simply a scalar multiple of
L
. If we denote with
\deg(u)
the degree of
u\in U
in
H
, we get by a Chernoff bound that

\Pr\left[\text{$\exists u\in U$, }\left|\deg(u)-p\cdot(N-1)\right|>\frac{1}{14}p\cdot(N-1)\right]\leq 2N\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{p\cdot(N-1)}{600}\right)\,.

(A.8)

From (
A.7
) and (
A.8
), we then have

\Pr\left[\left(\left\|L-pK\right\|\geq\frac{1}{10}pN\right)\text{ or }\left(\exists u\in U:\left|\deg_{H}(u)-\bar{d}\right|>\frac{1}{14}\bar{d}\right)\right]\leq 4N\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{pN}{600}\right)\,.

(A.9)

We conclude by showing that if the event in the above probability does not occur, then the event argument of the probability in the statement of
Fact

7.10
does not occur either. To see this, let
\lambda_{2}
be the second smallest eigenvalue of
1/\bar{d}\cdot L
, and
\mu_{2}
be the second smallest eigenvalue of
1/(N-1)\cdot K
. Hence,
\left\|L-pK\right\|<\frac{1}{10}pN
implies
\lambda_{2}\geq\mu_{2}-1/9
by Weyl’s inequality. Moreover, we know that
\mu_{2}=N/(N-1)
, so
\lambda_{2}\geq 9/10
. Finally, let
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq U
be a cut with
\vol(S)\leq\vol(U\setminus S)
such that
\Phi_{H}(S)=\Phi_{H}
. Assuming
\left|\deg_{H}(u)-\bar{d}\right|\leq\frac{1}{14}\bar{d}
for all
u\in U
, we have that
\vol(S)\in[(1-1/14)\bar{d}|S|,(1+1/14)\bar{d}|S|]
. Let
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{U}
be defined as
y_{u}=1/|S|
if
u\in S
and
y_{u}=-1/|U\setminus S|
otherwise. By Courant-Fischer we then have

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{U}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}:\\
\langle\mathbf{x},\ind\rangle=0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{1}{\bar{d}}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{x}^{\top}L\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{x}}\leq\frac{1}{\bar{d}}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{y}^{\top}L\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{y}^{\top}\mathbf{y}}=\frac{{\partial}_{U}S}{\bar{d}|S|}\cdot\frac{|U|}{|U\setminus S|}\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{14}\right)\Phi_{H}\cdot\frac{|U|}{|U\setminus S|}\,.

(A.10)

Again assuming
\left|\deg_{H}(u)-\bar{d}\right|\leq\frac{1}{14}\bar{d}
for all
u\in U
, one can check that
\vol(S)\leq\vol(U\setminus S)
implies
|U\setminus S|\geq 6/7\cdot|S|
. Thus, (
A.10
) gives

\lambda_{2}\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{14}\right)\cdot\left(1+\frac{7}{6}\right)\Phi_{H}\leq\frac{27}{10}\Phi_{H}\,,

so
\Phi_{H}\geq 1/3
.
∎

A.3
A useful inequality

See
7.21

Proof.

Write
x=y\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\rho-1}
for some
\rho\leq 1
. Then
(y/x)^{x}\leq\mathrm{e}^{y/\mathrm{e}}
if and only if
(1-\rho)e^{\rho-1}\leq 1/\mathrm{e}
. Define
f(\rho)=(1-\rho)\mathrm{e}^{\rho}
. We prove the claim by showing that
f(\rho)\leq 1
for all
\rho\in(-\infty,1]
.

We begin by noting that
f
is continuous over
(-\infty,1]
. Then, we take the derivative of
f
, and we get
f^{\prime}(\rho)=-\rho\mathrm{e}^{\rho}
. We observe that
f^{\prime}(\rho)>0
for
\rho<0
, and
f^{\prime}(\rho)>0
for
\rho>0
. We further observe that
f(0)=1
,
f(1)=0
, and
\lim_{\rho\rightarrow-\infty}f(\rho)=0
. Hence,
f(\rho)
is upper-bounded by
1
for all
\rho\in(-\infty,1]
.
∎
